# Chapter 3. Prime sums with a sign: the Landau-window criterion

> Sources as of 2026-11-21. Pinned archive sha256 `9631b4f0…9c7fc`; Mathlib at commit
> `0df444a` (tag `v4.33.1`); Masullo's draft sha256 `e039d3f6…97bf5`. See `sources/SOURCES.md`.

Chapter 2 reduced the five theorems of `src/Main.lean` to `Formalism.HC.ZeroFreeAll`: for every
primitive Dirichlet character $\psi$, the function $L(s,\psi)$ has no zeros in the open half-plane
$\Re s > 1/2$ [Lean, read]
(`src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/ExplicitFormulaErrorOfHostConditions.lean:18`).
How does the archive connect zeros of $L(s,\psi)$ to the arithmetic construction of Part II?

The bridge is a localized version of Landau's oscillation theorem (`Deduction.landau_window`,
`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/landauWindow.lean:83`,
packaged for cyclic character levels as `UpperTheory.Comparison₂.formB`,
`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/Imports.lean:8`), combined with a direct
$\varepsilon \to 0$ specialization (`Formalism.HC.level_of_tiedWin`,
`src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/TopWin.lean:68`)
[Lean, read]. Conceptually, this chapter is the hinge of Part I: it explains why **one-sided**
upper bounds on smoothed prime sums, proved only on **sparse logarithmic windows**
$[Y, Y^{1+\delta}]$ for a **positive linear combination** over the cyclic group generated by
$\psi$, already suffice to rule out every zero on $\Re s > 1/2$ — both real and complex, without
any circular appeal to zero-free regions for $\psi$ itself.

Every Lean file in the Landau-window subtree (`P5` in `notes/proof-map.md`, including
`landau_dichotomy` and `landau_nearest`, which the LUMI reader's guide marked `[unread]`
[Community; LUMI doc 03]) and in the level-gluing layer (`P4`) has been read in full for this
chapter (`R3` / `[Lean, read]`). Following our textbook structure, we begin with motivation and
concrete examples, fix the definitions, give complete human proofs of every step, and close with
remarks, Masullo's counterpart, and the Lean concordance.

---

## 3.1 Motivation

Every analytic number theorist knows the equivalence between zero-free half-planes for $L(s,\chi)$
and two-sided power-saving error terms in the prime number theorem. If we form the Riesz-smoothed
(or Cesàro-weighted) prime sum
$$S_\chi(y) = \sum_{1 \le n \le y} \Lambda(n)\,\chi(n)\Bigl(\frac{y}{n} - 1\Bigr)$$
and subtract its main term at $s = 1$ to obtain a normalized remainder $G_\chi(y)$ (defined
precisely in Section 3.3.1), then Mellin inversion — the explicit formula — expresses $G_\chi(y)$
roughly as a sum over the nontrivial zeros $\rho$ of $L(s,\chi)$:
$$G_\chi(y) = -\sum_{\rho} \frac{y^\rho}{\rho(\rho - 1)} + O(1) \qquad (y \ge 2)$$
[Expository] (compare H. Davenport, *Multiplicative Number Theory*, 3rd ed., Springer, 2000,
Chapter 17, or H. L. Montgomery and R. C. Vaughan, *Multiplicative Number Theory I: Classical
Theory*, Cambridge University Press, 2007, Chapters 12 and 15).

If one could prove a **two-sided** bound $\lvert G_\chi(y) \rvert \le C y^\theta$ for *all*
$y \ge 1$, the deduction that $L(s,\chi) \neq 0$ for $\Re s > \theta$ would take three lines: the
Mellin integral $\int_1^\infty G_\chi(y) y^{-s-1}\,dy$ would converge absolutely and define a
holomorphic function on $\Re s > \theta$, whereas by direct calculation that Mellin integral
equals $-\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi) / (s(s-1))$ up to terms holomorphic at $\Re s > 0$, forcing
$\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi)$ to have no poles on $\Re s > \theta$ [Expository].

The arithmetic engine of Part II (Chapters 5–11) falls short of that textbook ideal in **three
distinct ways**, and the architecture of Chapter 3 is designed specifically to overcome all three
at once [Expository]:

1. **Only a one-sided upper bound is available.** Chebyshev- or Stepanov-type positivity arguments
   produce inequalities with a single sign: in Chapter 5, `core_gm` bounds a prime-sum combination
   from *above*, $G_m(x) \le x^{1/2 + O(\varepsilon)}$, and says nothing directly against $G_m(x)$
   being large and negative [Lean] (`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/CoreGm.lean:12`).
   Why can a one-sided bound $G_m(y) \le C y^\theta$ rule out zeros on $\Re s > \theta$?
   - If $\rho = \beta \in (\theta, 1)$ is a **real** zero, notice the sign of its explicit-formula
     contribution: because $0 < \beta < 1$, we have $\beta(\beta - 1) < 0$, so
     $$-\frac{y^\beta}{\beta(\beta - 1)} = +\frac{y^\beta}{\beta(1 - \beta)} > 0!$$
     A real zero pushes $G_m(y)$ *upward*, straight into conflict with $G_m(y) \le C y^\theta$.
     (Had the prime sum been defined with the opposite sign, an upper bound could *never* rule out
     a Siegel zero $\beta$ near $1$!)
   - If $\rho = \beta + i\tau$ ($\tau \neq 0$, $\beta > \theta$) is a **complex** zero, then in a
     real-valued combination its conjugate $\bar\rho = \beta - i\tau$ also appears, contributing a
     real oscillatory wave $\approx 2 \Re\bigl(\frac{-y^{\beta + i\tau}}{\rho(\rho - 1)}\bigr) = A y^\beta \cos(\tau \log y + \phi_0)$.
     Because cosine changes sign every $\pi / \lvert \tau \rvert$ units of $\log y$, the wave cannot
     stay negative forever — as E. Landau ("Über einen Satz von Tschebyschef", *Mathematische
     Annalen* 61 (1905), 527–550, doi:10.1007/BF01449495) and G. Pólya ("Über das Vorzeichen des
     Restgliedes im Primzahlsatz", *Nachrichten von der Gesellschaft der Wissenschaften zu
     Göttingen, Mathematisch-Physikalische Klasse* 1930, 19–27) showed by applying Landau's
     singularity theorem for nonnegative Dirichlet integrals (Montgomery–Vaughan, Theorem 1.7 and
     §15.1).

2. **The bound holds only on finite logarithmic windows $[Y, Y^{1+\delta}]$.** Classical Landau
   theory considers the Laplace integral $\int_0^\infty (C e^{\theta u} - g(u)) e^{-su}\,du$,
   which requires $g(u) \le C e^{\theta u}$ for **all** $u \ge u_0$ so that the integrand has
   constant sign on $[u_0, \infty)$. In this proof, however, both the auxiliary prime pairs
   $(q, d)$ of Chapter 4 (`PairEx`) and the Tate–Shafarevich bound of Chapter 11 (`VWin`) hold only
   on intervals $y \in [Y, Y^\lambda]$ (with $\lambda = 3/2$, i.e. $\delta = 1/2$) for an
   unbounded sequence of scales $Y \to \infty$ [Lean] (`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/WinPairs.lean:5`).
   Outside $[Y, Y^{1+\delta}]$, the archive knows only the trivial bound $\lvert G_m(y) \rvert \le C_0 y^2$.
   How do we localize Landau's theorem to $[U, (1+\delta)U]$ (where $u = \log y$, $U = \log Y$)?
   Instead of letting $s \downarrow \beta$ where the Laplace integral diverges, we fix a **large
   real abscissa $\sigma > 2$** (chosen solely in terms of $\delta$) where the Laplace transform
   $\Phi(s) = \int_0^\infty g(u) e^{-su}\,du$ converges effortlessly, and inspect its normalized
   $k$-th derivatives:
   $$A_k(s) := \frac{(-1)^k \Phi^{(k)}(s)}{k!} = \int_0^\infty g(u)\,\frac{u^k e^{-su}}{k!}\,du.$$
   As a function of $u > 0$, the Poisson/Gamma weight $w_k(u) = \frac{u^k e^{-\sigma u}}{k!}$ is a
   bell-shaped positive kernel peaking at $u = k/\sigma$ with relative width $O(k^{-1/2})$. Taking
   $k \approx (1 + \delta/2)\sigma U$ places the peak squarely in the middle of the good window
   $[U, (1+\delta)U]$! Outside the window, exponential tilting suppresses the $e^{2u}$ tails down
   to $O(\sigma^{-k})$, while a pole $\rho$ at distance $d < \sigma - \theta$ from $\sigma + i\gamma$
   forces $\lvert A_k(\sigma + i\gamma) \rvert \asymp d^{-k} \gg (\sigma - \theta)^{-k}$.

3. **Only a positive combination over a cyclic level can be isolated.** In Chapter 5, finite
   Fourier analysis on the character group of an abelian host field $E \subset \mathbb Q(\zeta_N)$
   cannot isolate $\Re G_\psi(y)$ for a single primitive character $\psi$ with nonnegative spatial
   weights; what it *can* form with nonnegative weights is a combination
   $$G_m(y) = G_\zeta(y) + \sum_{\chi \in \Lambda \setminus \{1\}} w_\chi\,\Re G_\chi(y)$$
   over all primitive characters $\chi$ arising from the cyclic group $\langle \psi \rangle$, with
   strictly positive weights $w_\chi > 0$ [Lean, read]
   (`src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/ExplicitFormulaErrorOfHostConditions.lean:36–38,121`).
   Why don't zeros of different characters in $\Lambda$ cancel one another inside $G_m(y)$? Because
   every $w_\chi$ is strictly positive, and at any complex number $\rho$, the multiplicity
   $m_\chi(\rho) = \operatorname{ord}_{s=\rho} L(s,\chi)$ is a **nonnegative integer**! In the
   Laplace transform $\Phi(s)$ of $G_m(e^u)$, the residue at $\rho$ is
   $$-\frac{1}{\rho(\rho - 1)} \sum_{\chi \in \Lambda} \frac{w_\chi}{2}\bigl(m_\chi(\rho) + m_{\bar\chi}(\rho)\bigr),$$
   in which every term has the *exact same complex phase* $-1/(\rho(\rho-1))$ and nonnegative
   weight. Once at least one character in the family vanishes at $\rho$, the sum is strictly
   nonzero — and at a real zero $\rho = \beta \in (0,1)$, strictly positive real!

---

## 3.2 Small examples

Two elementary calculations bring the analytic mechanism of `poleData` and `landau_window` into
sharp focus before we introduce the general formalism [Expository].

### 3.2.1 Why Riesz smoothing $(y/n - 1)_+$ beats a sharp cutoff

Consider the single Riesz ramp $r_n(y) = \max(y/n - 1, 0)$ for an integer $n \ge 1$. For $\Re s > 1$,
its Mellin transform over $(1, \infty)$ vanishes on $(1, n]$ (since $y/n - 1 \le 0$ there) and on
$(n, \infty)$ is elementary:
$$\int_1^\infty \max\Bigl(\frac{y}{n} - 1, 0\Bigr) y^{-s-1}\,dy
= \int_n^\infty \Bigl(\frac{1}{n} y^{-s} - y^{-s-1}\Bigr)\,dy
= \frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{n^{1-s}}{s - 1} - \frac{n^{-s}}{s}
= \frac{n^{-s}}{s(s - 1)}.$$
Summing against $\Lambda(n)\chi(n)$ on $\Re s > 1$ (justified by Tonelli's theorem since
$\sum_{n \ge 1} \Lambda(n) n^{-\Re s} < \infty$) immediately gives
$$\int_1^\infty \Bigl(\sum_{1 \le n \le y} \Lambda(n)\chi(n)\Bigl(\frac{y}{n} - 1\Bigr)\Bigr) y^{-s-1}\,dy
= \frac{1}{s(s - 1)} \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{\Lambda(n)\chi(n)}{n^s}
= -\frac{L'(s,\chi)/L(s,\chi)}{s(s - 1)}.$$

Compare this with Chebyshev's unsmoothed sum $\psi(y,\chi) = \sum_{n \le y} \Lambda(n)\chi(n)$,
whose Mellin transform is $-\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi) / s$, or with the standard integrated sum
$\psi_1(y,\chi) = \int_1^y \psi(t,\chi)\,dt = \sum_{n \le y} \Lambda(n)\chi(n)(y - n)$, which
differs from $y\,S_\chi(y)$ only by a factor of $n$:
$$S_\chi(y) = \sum_{n \le y} \Lambda(n)\chi(n)\Bigl(\frac{y}{n} - 1\Bigr)
= \int_1^y \Bigl(\sum_{n \le t} \frac{\Lambda(n)\chi(n)}{n}\Bigr)\,dt.$$
Using $(y/n - 1)_+$ instead of $\mathbf{1}_{n \le y}$ buys two decisive advantages at zero cost:

1. **A removable singularity at $s = 1$ after linear subtraction.** Notice the factor $s - 1$ in
   the denominator $s(s - 1)$. If we left $-\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi)/(s(s-1))$ untouched for
   $\chi \neq 1$, it would have a spurious simple pole at $s = 1$ with residue
   $-\frac{L'}{L}(1,\chi)$ even though $L(1,\chi) \neq 0$! Adding the linear counterterm
   $+y\,\frac{L'}{L}(1,\chi)$ to $S_\chi(y)$ adds $\int_1^\infty y \frac{L'}{L}(1,\chi) y^{-s-1}\,dy = \frac{1}{s-1}\frac{L'}{L}(1,\chi) = \frac{s}{s(s-1)}\frac{L'}{L}(1,\chi)$
   to the Mellin transform, turning it into
   $$-\frac{\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi) - s\,\frac{L'}{L}(1,\chi)}{s(s - 1)}.$$
   Now the numerator vanishes at $s = 1$, cancelling the factor $s - 1$ in the denominator! For
   $\zeta(s)$ ($N = 1, \chi = 1$), $-\frac{\zeta'}{\zeta}(s) = \frac{1}{s-1} - \gamma + O(s-1)$ near
   $s = 1$, so $-\frac{\zeta'}{\zeta}(s)/(s(s-1)) = \frac{1}{(s-1)^2} - \frac{1+\gamma}{s-1} + O(1)$;
   subtracting $y \log y - (1 + \gamma)y$ (whose Mellin transform on $(1,\infty)$ is
   $\frac{1}{(s-1)^2} - \frac{1+\gamma}{s-1}$) cancels *both* singular terms at $s = 1$! For
   instance, at $y = 4$,
   $$S_\zeta(4) = \Lambda(2)\Bigl(\frac{4}{2}-1\Bigr) + \Lambda(3)\Bigl(\frac{4}{3}-1\Bigr) + \Lambda(4)\Bigl(\frac{4}{4}-1\Bigr)
   = \log 2 + \frac{\log 3}{3} \approx 1.0594,$$
   and $G_\zeta(4) = S_\zeta(4) - 4\log 4 + 4(1 + \gamma) \approx 1.0594 - 5.5452 + 6.3089 \approx 1.8230$
   (unrounded: $1.823036\dots$).
2. **Positive real residues at real zeros.** At any zero $\rho \in (0, 1)$ of $L(s,\chi)$ of order
   $m \ge 1$, we have $\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi) = \frac{m}{s - \rho} + O(1)$, so the Mellin transform
   has principal part $\frac{-m / (\rho(\rho - 1))}{s - \rho}$. Because $\rho(\rho - 1) < 0$ on
   $(0, 1)$, the residue $\frac{m}{\rho(1 - \rho)}$ is **strictly positive**!

### 3.2.2 Two complex poles and why vertical perturbation avoids Turán's lemma

Suppose a toy Laplace transform has no real poles, and two simple poles in $\{\Re s > \theta\}$ at
$\rho_\pm = \beta \pm i\tau$ ($\theta < \beta < 1$, $\tau > 0$) with conjugate residues $c, \bar c \neq 0$:
$$\Phi_{\mathrm{toy}}(s) = \frac{c}{s - (\beta + i\tau)} + \frac{\bar c}{s - (\beta - i\tau)},
\qquad g_{\mathrm{toy}}(u) = 2\Re\bigl(c\,e^{(\beta + i\tau)u}\bigr).$$
Fix a large real $\sigma > 2$ and examine the normalized Taylor coefficients
$$A_k(z) = \frac{(-1)^k \Phi_{\mathrm{toy}}^{(k)}(z)}{k!}
= \frac{c}{(z - \beta - i\tau)^{k+1}} + \frac{\bar c}{(z - \beta + i\tau)^{k+1}}.$$

- **If we foolishly stay on the real axis ($z = \sigma$):** both poles lie at the *same* distance
  $d_0 = \sqrt{(\sigma - \beta)^2 + \tau^2}$ from $\sigma$. First, $d_0$ can easily exceed
  $\sigma - \theta$ when $\tau$ is large (indeed $d_0 \le \sigma - \theta$ fails whenever
  $\tau^2 > (\sigma - \theta)^2 - (\sigma - \beta)^2$), so derivatives at $z = \sigma$ cannot see
  high-lying zeros at all! Second, even if $\tau$ is small so $d_0 < \sigma - \theta$, writing
  $\sigma - \beta - i\tau = d_0 e^{-i\alpha}$ gives $A_k(\sigma) = 2 d_0^{-k-1}\Re(c e^{i(k+1)\alpha})$,
  which **can vanish or become tiny** at the particular integer $k = \lceil(1 + \delta/2)\sigma U\rceil$
  forced upon us by the window $[U, (1+\delta)U]$!
- **If we shift vertically to $z = \sigma + i\tau$:** now $\rho_+ = \beta + i\tau$ is at distance
  $d = \lvert (\sigma + i\tau) - (\beta + i\tau) \rvert = \sigma - \beta < \sigma - \theta$, while
  $\rho_- = \beta - i\tau$ is strictly farther away, at distance $\sqrt{(\sigma - \beta)^2 + 4\tau^2} > d$:
  $$\lvert A_k(\sigma + i\tau) \rvert
  \ge \frac{\lvert c \rvert}{(\sigma - \beta)^{k+1}}
  - \frac{\lvert c \rvert}{\bigl((\sigma - \beta)^2 + 4\tau^2\bigr)^{(k+1)/2}}
  \sim \frac{\lvert c \rvert / (\sigma - \beta)}{(\sigma - \beta)^k} \gg \frac{1}{(\sigma - \theta)^k}.$$
- **What if the actual zero set $Z$ happens to contain *another* zero $\rho'$ at the exact same
  distance from $\sigma + i\tau$ as $\rho_+$?** For example, suppose $Z$ contained both
  $\beta + i(\tau + \delta_0)$ and $\beta + i(\tau - \delta_0)$, symmetric across height $\tau$.
  At $\gamma = \tau$, the two poles are equidistant from $\sigma + i\gamma$ and could cancel at a
  prescribed $k$! Classically one invokes P. Turán's power-sum method, varying $k$ across a block
  of consecutive integers to break the cancellation. The archive uses a much simpler geometric
  observation (`Deduction.landau_nearest`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/LandauNearest.lean:36`) [Lean, read]:
  any two distinct points $a, b \in \mathbb C$ in the half-plane $\Re s < \sigma$ have a unique
  perpendicular bisector, which can intersect the vertical line $\Re s = \sigma$ in **at most one
  point** $\sigma + i\gamma_{a,b}$ (and in zero points if $\Im a = \Im b$, where the bisector is
  vertical at $\frac{1}{2}(\Re a + \Re b) < \sigma$). Because $Z$ has only finitely many points in
  any compact disk inside $\{\Re s > 0\}$, only **finitely many** heights $\gamma$ near $\tau$ have
  tied nearest poles! Shifting $\gamma$ by an arbitrarily small $\varepsilon$ off that finite set
  guarantees a **strictly unique** nearest pole to $\sigma + i\gamma$, making Cauchy's elementary
  inequality do all the work of Turán's theorem at a *single* integer $k$!

One final detail in this toy model explains why `landau_window_ineq` bounds the *sum*
$\lvert A_k(\sigma + i\gamma) \rvert + \Re A_k(\sigma)$ rather than $\lvert A_k(\sigma + i\gamma) \rvert$
alone [Expository]. For $w_k(u) = \frac{u^k e^{-\sigma u}}{k!} \ge 0$, triangle inequality gives
$\lvert A_k(\sigma + i\gamma) \rvert \le \int_0^\infty \lvert g(u) \rvert w_k(u)\,du$, which involves
$\lvert g(u) \rvert$, whereas we only have an *upper* bound $g(u) \le R(u)$ ($R \ge 0$), not a
bound on $\lvert g(u) \rvert$! Observe, however, that for real $g(u)$,
$$\lvert g(u) \rvert + g(u) = \begin{cases} 2g(u) \le 2R(u) & \text{if } g(u) \ge 0,\\ 0 \le 2R(u) & \text{if } g(u) < 0. \end{cases}$$
Integrating against $w_k(u) \ge 0$ gives
$$\lvert A_k(\sigma + i\gamma) \rvert + \Re A_k(\sigma)
\le \int_0^\infty \bigl(\lvert g(u) \rvert + g(u)\bigr) w_k(u)\,du
\le 2\int_0^\infty R(u)\,w_k(u)\,du!$$
Could a large *negative* value of $\Re A_k(\sigma)$ hide a large value of $\lvert A_k(\sigma + i\gamma) \rvert$?
Only if $\Phi(s)$ had a singularity closer to $\sigma$ than $d = \lvert \sigma + i\gamma - \rho \rvert$!
If no pole of $\Phi$ lies in the open disk $B(\sigma, D)$ around $\sigma$ (with $d < D$), Cauchy's
estimate at $\sigma$ forces $\lvert \Re A_k(\sigma) \rvert \ll r'^{-k}$ ($d < r' < D$) to be
exponentially negligible compared to $d^{-k}$; and if a pole *does* hit the boundary of $B(\sigma, D)$
at minimal real part, elementary Euclidean geometry forces that pole to be **real** ($\beta = \sigma - D$),
where its positive real residue makes $\Re A_k(\sigma)$ huge and **positive**, contradicting the
inequality at $\gamma = 0$ directly!

---

## 3.3 Definitions

The archive develops the Landau-window criterion in two cleanly separated namespaces:
`Deduction` (`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/`), which proves the
analytic theorem for any finite admissible family of Dirichlet characters, and `Formalism.HC`
(`src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/`), which defines the
cyclic character levels consumed by the rest of the proof. The bridge file
`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/LevelFamily.lean` connects the two [Lean, read].

### 3.3.1 Riesz-weighted prime sums and normalized remainders

Let $\Lambda(n)$ denote the von Mangoldt function (`ArithmeticFunction.vonMangoldt`: $\Lambda(p^k) = \log p$
for prime powers $p^k \ge 2$, and $0$ otherwise), and let $\gamma$ denote the Euler–Mascheroni
constant (`Real.eulerMascheroniConstant`).

**Definition 3.1 (Riesz prime sum and normalized character remainder).** For any arithmetic
function $f : \mathbb N \to \mathbb C$ and real $y \in \mathbb R$, define the Riesz ramp sum
[Lean, read] (`Deduction.ramp`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily.lean:10`)
$$\operatorname{ramp}(f, y) := \sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor y \rfloor_{\mathbb N}} \Lambda(n)\,f(n)\,\max\Bigl(\frac{y}{n} - 1, 0\Bigr)$$
(where $\lfloor y \rfloor_{\mathbb N} = 0$ if $y < 0$, and the $n = 0$ term vanishes since $\Lambda(0) = 0$).
For $N \ge 1$ and $\chi : \mathrm{DirichletCharacter}\ \mathbb C\ N$, define the **normalized
Riesz remainder** $G_\chi(y)$ (`Deduction.Gchi`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily.lean:15`) by
$$G_\chi(y) := \begin{cases}
\operatorname{ramp}(\chi, y) - y\log y + (1 + \gamma)\,y & \text{if }\chi = 1,\\[4pt]
\operatorname{ramp}(\chi, y) + y\,\dfrac{L'(1,\chi)}{L(1,\chi)} & \text{if }\chi \neq 1,
\end{cases}$$
and for the Riemann zeta function alone define the real-valued function (`Deduction.G`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily.lean:21`)
$$G(y) := \Re\,\operatorname{ramp}(1, y) - y\log y + (1 + \gamma)\,y.$$

*(Compatibility across namespaces.)* Two other modules define the same mathematical objects with
slightly different summation domains (`Finset.Icc 1 ⌊y⌋₊` instead of `Finset.range (⌊y⌋₊ + 1)`,
and with or without `max · 0`, which is redundant when $1 \le n \le \lfloor y \rfloor_{\mathbb N} \le y$):
`Formalism.Sz` and `Formalism.Gz` (`src/Formalism/StatementOC.lean:11,13`), and `Formalism.HC.An.G`,
`An.sumLamChi`, `An.logDerivativeAtOne`, and `An.gChi`
(`src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/Analytic.lean:11,14,18,23`)
[Lean, read]. The archive proves their exact pointwise equality:
- `UpperTheory.Comparison₂.Gz_eq` (`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/GzEq.lean:6`)
  proves $\mathrm{Formalism.Gz}(y) = \mathrm{An.G}(y) = \mathrm{Deduction.G}(y)$ for all $y \in \mathbb R$ [Lean, read];
- `UpperTheory.Comparison₂.gChi_eq` (`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/GChiEq.lean:16`) proves
  $\mathrm{An.gChi}(\psi.\chi, y) = \mathrm{Deduction.Gchi}(\psi.\chi, y)$ for every primitive
  character $\psi : \mathrm{PChar}$ and every $y \in \mathbb R$ [Lean, read].
We can therefore write $G_\zeta(y)$ for $G(y)$ and $G_\chi(y)$ unambiguously throughout.

### 3.3.2 Admissible character families and window hypotheses

**Definition 3.2 (Admissible character family, `CharFamily`).** An **admissible character family**
$F$ (`Deduction.CharFamily`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily.lean:24`)
consists of [Lean, read]:
- a finite index type $\iota$;
- for each $i \in \iota$, a modulus $N_i \ge 1$, a Dirichlet character $\chi_i \bmod N_i$, and a
  real weight $a_i > 0$ (`a_pos`);
- the **admissibility condition** (`adm`): for every $i \in \iota$, either $N_i = 1$ or $\chi_i \neq 1$.

*(Why admissibility is required.)* If $N_i > 1$ and $\chi_i = 1 \bmod N_i$ were allowed, then
$L(s,\chi_i) = \zeta(s)\prod_{p \mid N_i}(1 - p^{-s})$ would have an asymptotic expansion near
$s = 1$ containing $\sum_{p \mid N_i} \frac{\log p}{p - 1}$ in addition to $\gamma$, so the
zeta counterterm $-y\log y + (1 + \gamma)y$ in Definition 3.1 would *not* cancel the simple pole
at $s = 1$! Requiring $N_i = 1 \lor \chi_i \neq 1$ ensures that every trivial character in $F$ is
literally $1 \bmod 1$ (giving $\zeta$), while every other character in $F$ is nontrivial (so
$L(s,\chi_i)$ is entire with $L(1,\chi_i) \neq 0$) [Expository]. Notice that every primitive
character $\psi : \mathrm{PChar}$ satisfies admissibility automatically: if $\psi.\chi = 1$, then
$N = \operatorname{cond}(\psi.\chi) = \operatorname{cond}(1) = 1$ (`level_family_adm`,
`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/LevelFamily.lean:19`) [Lean, read].

Associated to $F$ are the following functions and predicates [Lean, read] (`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily.lean:36,38,41`;
`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauZeroFreeCriterion.lean:9,11,25`):

1. **Weighted prime-sum remainder (`Deduction.Gm` and `Deduction.gm`):**
   $$G_m(F, y) := \sum_{i \in \iota} a_i\,\Re G_{\chi_i}(y), \qquad g_F(u) := G_m(F, e^u) \quad (y, u \in \mathbb R).$$
2. **Zero-freeness and symmetrized zero set (`ZeroFree` and `zeroSet`):** for $\theta \in \mathbb R$,
   $$\mathrm{ZeroFree}(F, \theta) \;:\Longleftrightarrow\; \forall i \in \iota,\ \forall s \in \mathbb C,\ \Re s > \theta \implies L(s,\chi_i) \neq 0,$$
   and
   $$Z(F) := \bigl\{\rho \in \mathbb C : \Re\rho > 0 \ \text{and}\ \exists i \in \iota,\ L(\rho,\chi_i) = 0 \ \text{or}\ L(\bar\rho,\chi_i) = 0\bigr\}.$$
3. **Integral window hypotheses (`WindowHyp` and `WindowHypU`):** for $\theta, \delta \in \mathbb R$,
   `WindowHyp F θ δ` holds if for every $\eta > 0$ and every $Y_0 \in \mathbb R$, there exist
   $Y \ge Y_0$ and a function $R : \mathbb R \to \mathbb R$ such that $y \mapsto R(y) y^{-\theta - 1}$
   is integrable on $[Y, Y^{1+\delta}]$,
   $$0 \le R(y) \quad\text{and}\quad G_m(F, y) \le R(y) \qquad \forall y \in [Y, Y^{1+\delta}],$$
   and
   $$\int_Y^{Y^{1+\delta}} R(y)\,y^{-\theta - 1}\,dy \le Y^\eta.$$
   In logarithmic coordinates $u = \log y$ ($U = \log Y$), `WindowHypU g θ δ` states identically
   that for every $\eta > 0$ and $U_0 \in \mathbb R$, there exist $U \ge U_0$ and $R : \mathbb R \to \mathbb R$
   such that $u \mapsto R(u) e^{-\theta u}$ is integrable on $[U, (1+\delta)U]$, $0 \le R(u)$ and
   $g(u) \le R(u)$ on $[U, (1+\delta)U]$, and $\int_U^{(1+\delta)U} R(u) e^{-\theta u}\,du \le e^{\eta U}$.

### 3.3.3 Abstract Laplace-pole packages (`PoleData`)

Rather than tangling complex-analytic manipulations with Dirichlet series, the archive isolates the
analytic properties of $(g_F, \Phi, Z(F))$ into a clean seven-clause structure [Lean, read]
(`Deduction.PoleData`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauZeroFreeCriterion.lean:15`):

**Definition 3.3 (`PoleData g Φ Z`).** Let $g : \mathbb R \to \mathbb R$, $\Phi : \mathbb C \to \mathbb C$,
and $Z \subseteq \mathbb C$. We say `PoleData g Φ Z` holds if:

1. (`meas`) $g$ is Borel measurable;
2. (`growth`) there exists $C_0 \in \mathbb R$ such that $\lvert g(u) \rvert \le C_0 e^{2u}$ for all $u \ge 0$;
3. (`laplace`) for every $s \in \mathbb C$ with $\Re s > 2$, $\Phi(s) = \int_0^\infty g(u) e^{-su}\,du$;
4. (`pos`) every $\rho \in Z$ satisfies $\Re\rho > 0$;
5. (`diff`) $\Phi$ is complex-differentiable (hence holomorphic) on $\{s \in \mathbb C : \Re s > 0\} \setminus Z$;
6. (`iso`) $Z$ is discrete in $\{\Re s > 0\}$: for every $z$ with $\Re z > 0$, there is a punctured
   neighborhood of $z$ disjoint from $Z$;
7. (`pole`) at every $\rho \in Z$, $\Phi$ has a **simple pole** with nonzero residue $c \in \mathbb C \setminus \{0\}$
   — that is, $\Phi(s) = \frac{c}{s - \rho} + h(s)$ on a punctured neighborhood of $\rho$ for some
   function $h$ analytic at $\rho$ — and **if $\Im\rho = 0$, then $c$ is real and strictly positive**
   ($\Re c > 0$ and $\Im c = 0$).

### 3.3.4 Cyclic character levels (`Level` and `Level.cyc`)

In `Formalism.HC`, character families carry the extra group-theoretic structure needed by the
host-field Fourier analysis of Chapter 5 [Lean, read]
(`src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/PrimePairSizeCondition.lean:39`;
`src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/ExplicitFormulaErrorOfHostConditions.lean:8,24–38,105,121`; `src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/Window.lean:7,30,33`):

**Definition 3.4 (`KSet`, `Level`, `Level.Gm`, and `Level.cyc`).**

1. A **`KSet`** consists of a finite set $\mathrm{KEPT}$ of bundled primitive characters
   (`Finset PChar`) containing $\mathrm{PChar.one}$ (`one_mem`) and closed under inversion
   $\psi \mapsto \psi.\mathrm{inv}$ (`inv_mem`), together with an optional distinguished character
   $\mathrm{psK} : \mathrm{Option}\ \mathrm{PChar}$ which, when present ($\mathrm{psK} = \mathrm{some}\ \psi_0$),
   belongs to $\mathrm{KEPT}$ and is odd and real ($\psi_0.\chi(-1) = -1$ and $\Im \psi_0.\chi(a) = 0$
   for all $a$). (In Chapters 4–5, $\mathrm{psK}$ tracks a possible quadratic character coming
   from the host frame; for the cyclic levels used in the endgame below, $\mathrm{psK} = \mathrm{none}$.)
2. A **`Level`** $\Lambda$ extends `KSet` by a natural-number parameter $o = \Lambda.o \in \mathbb N$
   (recording the subgroup order). Writing $\Lambda.\mathrm{Nt} := \Lambda.\mathrm{KEPT} \setminus \{\mathrm{PChar.one}\}$,
   each nontrivial character $\psi \in \Lambda.\mathrm{Nt}$ receives the positive weight
   $$\Lambda.\mathrm{wt}(\psi) := \begin{cases}
   1 & \text{if } \Lambda.\mathrm{psK} = \mathrm{some}\ \psi,\\
   (3o + 1)^{-1} & \text{otherwise},
   \end{cases}$$
   and the level's prime-sum function is
   $$\Lambda.\mathrm{Gm}(y) := G_\zeta(y) + \sum_{\psi \in \Lambda.\mathrm{Nt}} \Lambda.\mathrm{wt}(\psi)\,\Re G_{\psi.\chi}(y).$$
3. Given any subgroup $\Xi \le \mathrm{DirichletCharacter}\ \mathbb C\ M$ ($M \ge 1$), `Level.ofSub Ξ`
   sets $\mathrm{KEPT}$ to be the image under $\mathrm{PChar.of}$ of $\Xi$ (the primitive
   characters underlying all characters in $\Xi$), sets $\mathrm{psK} = \mathrm{none}$, and sets
   $o = \lvert \Xi \rvert$. For a primitive character $\psi : \mathrm{PChar}$, the **cyclic level**
   `Level.cyc ψ` is `Level.ofSub (Subgroup.zpowers ψ.χ)`, generated by the powers $(\psi.\chi)^m$
   ($m \in \mathbb Z$) modulo $\psi.N$. Since $\psi$ is primitive, $\mathrm{PChar.of}(\psi.\chi) = \psi$
   (`PChar.of_self`, `:59`), so $\psi \in (\mathrm{Level.cyc}\ \psi).\mathrm{KEPT}$ (`Level.mem_cyc`, `:123`).
4. For a real scale exponent $\lambda > 1$ (written `la` in the code) and a set $S \subseteq \mathbb R$,
   `HasWindows S la` means: for every $y_0 \in \mathbb R$, there exists $Y \ge y_0$ such that
   $[Y, Y^\lambda] \subseteq S$. The level's localized Landau property `Λ.FormBAt la` asserts that
   for every $\theta \in (0, 1)$,
   $$\mathrm{HasWindows}\bigl(\{x \in \mathbb R : \Lambda.\mathrm{Gm}(x) \le x^\theta\},\,\lambda\bigr)
   \implies \forall \psi \in \Lambda.\mathrm{KEPT},\ \forall s \in \mathbb C,\ \Re s > \theta \implies L(s, \psi.\chi) \neq 0,$$
   and `Λ.FormB` asserts `∀ la > 1, Λ.FormBAt la`.

---

## 3.4 Lemmas and proofs

We now prove the entire chain from the Mellin transform of $G_\chi$ down to `zeroFreeAll_of_topG`.
Every proof below is a faithful human exposition of the formal proof we read in the archive, with
no steps skipped.

### 3.4.1 Mellin transforms and realization of `PoleData`

**Proposition 3.1 (Quadratic growth and Mellin/Laplace identities for $G_\chi$).** *Let $N \ge 1$
and let $\chi \bmod N$ satisfy the admissibility condition $N = 1 \lor \chi \neq 1$.*

1. *(Crude quadratic bound.) There exists a constant $C_\chi > 0$ such that
   $\lvert G_\chi(y) \rvert \le C_\chi y^2$ for all $y \ge 1$.*
2. *(Mellin transform on $\Re s > 1$.) If $\chi \neq 1$, then for every $s \in \mathbb C$ with
   $\Re s > 1$,*
   $$\int_1^\infty G_\chi(y)\,y^{-s-1}\,dy
   = -\frac{\dfrac{L'(s,\chi)}{L(s,\chi)} - s\,\dfrac{L'(1,\chi)}{L(1,\chi)}}{s(s - 1)}.$$
   *If $N = 1$ (so $\chi = 1 \bmod 1$ and $L(s,\chi) = \zeta(s)$), then for every $s \in \mathbb C$
   with $\Re s > 1$,*
   $$\int_1^\infty G_\chi(y)\,y^{-s-1}\,dy
   = -\frac{\dfrac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)} + \dfrac{1}{s - 1} + 1 - (1 + \gamma)s}{s(s - 1)}.$$
3. *(Laplace transform on $\Re s > 2$.) Substituting $y = e^u$ ($u > 0$, $dy = e^u\,du$) identifies
   the absolutely convergent Laplace integral $\int_0^\infty G_\chi(e^u) e^{-su}\,du$ on $\Re s > 2$
   with the Mellin integral $\int_1^\infty G_\chi(y) y^{-s-1}\,dy$.*

*(Lean: `Gchi_growth`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/GchiGrowth.lean:37`;
`mellin_ramp`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/MellinRamp.lean:10`; `mellin_Gchi_of_ne_one`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/mellinGchiOfNeOne.lean:116`;
`mellin_Gchi_modOne`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/mellinGchiModOne.lean:115`; `poleData_integrable` and `poleData_change`,
`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/poleData.lean:91,114`.)* [Lean, read]

*Proof.*
(1) For $y \ge 1$ and $1 \le n \le \lfloor y \rfloor$, we have $0 \le \Lambda(n) \le \log n \le n$,
$\lvert \chi(n) \rvert \le 1$, and $0 \le \max(y/n - 1, 0) \le y/n$. Thus each summand of
$\operatorname{ramp}(\chi, y)$ has modulus at most $n \cdot 1 \cdot (y/n) = y$ (and the $n = 0$
term is $0$). Since there are $\lfloor y \rfloor + 1 \le 2y$ terms, $\lvert \operatorname{ramp}(\chi, y) \rvert \le 2y^2$.
For $y \ge 1$, both $y\log y \le y^2$ and $y \le y^2$, so adding the normalization terms gives
$\lvert G_\chi(y) \rvert \le C_\chi y^2$ with $C_\chi = 3 + \lvert 1 + \gamma \rvert$ when $\chi = 1$
and $C_\chi = 2 + \lvert L'(1,\chi)/L(1,\chi) \rvert$ when $\chi \neq 1$.

(2) For each integer $n \ge 1$ and $\Re s > 1$, we already computed in Section 3.2.1 that
$$\int_1^\infty \max\Bigl(\frac{y}{n} - 1, 0\Bigr) y^{-s-1}\,dy
= \int_n^\infty \Bigl(\frac{1}{n} y^{-s} - y^{-s-1}\Bigr)\,dy = \frac{n^{-s}}{s(s - 1)},$$
and taking $\sigma_0 = \Re s > 1$ shows that the $L^1$-norm of the $n$-th term $\Lambda(n)\chi(n)\max(y/n - 1, 0)y^{-s-1}$
over $(1, \infty)$ is bounded by $\Lambda(n) n^{-\sigma_0} / (\sigma_0(\sigma_0 - 1))$, which is
summable over $n \ge 1$ because $-\frac{\zeta'}{\zeta}(\sigma_0) = \sum_{n \ge 1} \Lambda(n) n^{-\sigma_0} < \infty$.
By Fubini–Tonelli (`hasSum_integral_of_summable_integral_norm`), summation over $n$ commutes with
integration over $(1, \infty)$, yielding
$$\int_1^\infty \operatorname{ramp}(\chi, y)\,y^{-s-1}\,dy
= \frac{1}{s(s - 1)} \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{\Lambda(n)\chi(n)}{n^s}
= -\frac{L'(s,\chi)/L(s,\chi)}{s(s - 1)}$$
(with $\zeta$ in place of $L(\cdot, \chi)$ when $N = 1$). Now add the Mellin transforms of the
counterterms on $(1, \infty)$ for $\Re s > 1$:
$$\int_1^\infty y \cdot y^{-s-1}\,dy = \int_1^\infty y^{-s}\,dy = \frac{1}{s - 1} = \frac{s}{s(s - 1)},$$
and, integrating $\frac{d}{dy}\bigl(y^{1-s}\log y\bigr) = (1 - s)y^{-s}\log y + y^{-s}$ over
$[1, \infty)$ (where $y^{1-s}\log y \to 0$ as $y \to \infty$ since $\Re s > 1$, and $\log 1 = 0$),
$$\int_1^\infty (y\log y)\,y^{-s-1}\,dy = \int_1^\infty y^{-s}\log y\,dy = \frac{1}{(s - 1)^2} = \frac{s/(s - 1)}{s(s - 1)}.$$
When $\chi \neq 1$, adding $\frac{L'}{L}(1,\chi)\cdot \frac{s}{s(s-1)}$ gives the first formula.
When $N = 1$, subtracting $\frac{1}{(s-1)^2}$ and adding $(1 + \gamma)\frac{s}{s(s-1)}$ produces
$$-\frac{\zeta'(s)/\zeta(s)}{s(s - 1)} - \frac{1}{(s - 1)^2} + \frac{(1 + \gamma)s}{s(s - 1)}
= -\frac{\dfrac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)} + \dfrac{s}{s - 1} - (1 + \gamma)s}{s(s - 1)},$$
and writing $\frac{s}{s - 1} = \frac{1}{s - 1} + 1$ gives the second formula.

(3) By (1), for $u > 0$ we have $\lvert G_\chi(e^u) e^{-su} \rvert \le C_\chi e^{2u} e^{-(\Re s)u} = C_\chi e^{-(\Re s - 2)u}$,
which is integrable on $(0, \infty)$ whenever $\Re s > 2$. Since $u \mapsto e^u$ is a smooth
strictly increasing bijection $(0, \infty) \xrightarrow{\sim} (1, \infty)$ with derivative $e^u$,
the change-of-variables theorem gives
$$\int_0^\infty G_\chi(e^u)\,e^{-su}\,du
= \int_0^\infty G_\chi(e^u)\,(e^u)^{-s-1}\,e^u\,du
= \int_1^\infty G_\chi(y)\,y^{-s-1}\,dy. \qquad \square$$

Now we extend the right-hand side meromorphically to $\Re s > 0$ and verify all seven clauses of
`PoleData`.

**Theorem 3.2 (Realization of `PoleData` for any admissible character family).** *For every
admissible character family $F : \mathrm{CharFamily}$, there exists a function
$\Phi : \mathbb C \to \mathbb C$ satisfying `PoleData (gm F) Φ (zeroSet F)`.*
*(Lean: `poleData_abstract`, `poleData_char`, and `poleData`,
`src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/poleData.lean:22,137,248`;
with `logDeriv_local`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/LogDerivLocal.lean:9`; `LFunction_conj`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/LFunctionConj.lean:88`;
`Gchi_conj`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/GchiConj.lean:38`; `measurable_Gchi`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/measurableGchi.lean:24`; and
`charFamily_LFunction_ne_zero_of_one_le_re`, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/CharFamilyLFunctionNeZeroOfOneLeRe.lean:9`.)* [Lean, read]

*Proof.* We proceed in three steps, matching `poleData_abstract` $\to$ `poleData_char` $\to$ `poleData`.

**Step 1: One entire function (`poleData_abstract`).** Let $g : \mathbb C \to \mathbb C$ be any
entire function with $g(1) \neq 0$, and let $k \in \mathbb C$ be a constant. Set $c_1 = g'(1)/g(1)$
and consider
$$N(s) := \frac{g'(s)}{g(s)} - s\,c_1 + k(1 - s)$$
on the open set $U = \{s \in \mathbb C : g(s) \neq 0\}$. Since $g$ is entire and $1 \in U$, $N$ is
holomorphic on $U$ and satisfies $N(1) = c_1 - c_1 + 0 = 0$. Therefore the difference quotient
$$Q(s) := \begin{cases}
\dfrac{N(s) - N(1)}{s - 1} = \dfrac{N(s)}{s - 1} & (s \neq 1),\\[6pt]
N'(1) & (s = 1)
\end{cases}$$
(`dslope N 1` in Mathlib) is holomorphic on the *entire* open set $U$ — including at $s = 1$! Define
$\Phi_g(s) := -Q(s)/s$. Then:
- For every $s \neq 1$, $\Phi_g(s) = -\dfrac{g'(s)/g(s) - s(g'(1)/g(1)) + k(1 - s)}{s(s - 1)}$.
- At every $\rho \in \mathbb C \setminus \{0\}$ with $g(\rho) \neq 0$, $\Phi_g$ is analytic at $\rho$.
- Because $g$ is entire and not identically zero ($g(1) \neq 0$), its zeros are isolated; at any
  $\rho \neq 0$, write $g(s) = (s - \rho)^m g_0(s)$ near $\rho$ with $m = \operatorname{ord}_{s=\rho} g \ge 0$
  ($m = 0 \iff g(\rho) \neq 0$) and $g_0(\rho) \neq 0$ (`logDeriv_local`). If $m \ge 1$, then
  $\rho \neq 1$ (since $g(1) \neq 0$) and $\frac{g'}{g}(s) = \frac{m}{s - \rho} + h_0(s)$ with
  $h_0 = g_0'/g_0$ analytic at $\rho$. Writing $\frac{1}{s(s-1)} = \frac{1}{\rho(\rho-1)} + (s - \rho)q(s)$
  with $q$ analytic at $\rho$ (since $\rho \neq 0, 1$), we obtain on a punctured neighborhood of $\rho$:
  $$\Phi_g(s) = -\Bigl(\frac{m}{s - \rho} + h_0(s) - s c_1 + k(1 - s)\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{1}{\rho(\rho - 1)} + (s - \rho)q(s)\Bigr)
  = \frac{m \cdot \bigl(-1 / (\rho(\rho - 1))\bigr)}{s - \rho} + h(s),$$
  where $h(s) = -m\,q(s) - (h_0(s) - s c_1 + k(1 - s))/(s(s - 1))$ is analytic at $\rho$. (If $m = 0$,
  the same formula holds trivially with $h = \Phi_g$.)

**Step 2: Single admissible character (`poleData_char`).** Let $\chi \bmod N$ satisfy $N = 1 \lor \chi \neq 1$.
- If $\chi \neq 1$, then $g(s) = L(s,\chi)$ is entire (`differentiable_LFunction`) and satisfies
  $L(1,\chi) \neq 0$ (`LFunction_apply_one_ne_zero`, `Mathlib/NumberTheory/LSeries/Nonvanishing.lean:406`).
  Applying Step 1 with $k = 0$ produces $\Phi_\chi := \Phi_g$, which for $\Re s > 2$ agrees with
  $\int_0^\infty G_\chi(e^u) e^{-su}\,du$ by Proposition 3.1(2)–(3).
- If $\chi = 1$, admissibility forces $N = 1$, so $L(s,\chi) = \zeta(s)$. Define
  $$g(s) := \begin{cases} (s - 1)\zeta(s) & (s \neq 1),\\ 1 & (s = 1). \end{cases}$$
  Since $\lim_{s \to 1} (s - 1)\zeta(s) = 1$ (`riemannZeta_residue_one`), Riemann's removable
  singularity theorem shows that $g$ is entire with $g(1) = 1 \neq 0$. Moreover, the classical
  Laurent expansion $\zeta(s) = \frac{1}{s - 1} + \gamma + o(1)$ as $s \to 1$
  (`tendsto_riemannZeta_sub_one_div`) gives
  $$g'(1) = \lim_{s \to 1} \frac{(s - 1)\zeta(s) - 1}{s - 1}
  = \lim_{s \to 1} \Bigl(\zeta(s) - \frac{1}{s - 1}\Bigr) = \gamma,$$
  while for $s \neq 1$ the product rule gives $\frac{g'(s)}{g(s)} = \frac{1}{s - 1} + \frac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)}$
  whenever $\zeta(s) \neq 0$ (and $g(w) = 0 \iff \zeta(w) = 0$ for all $w \in \mathbb C$, since
  $g(1) = 1 \neq 0$ and $\zeta(1) \neq 0$ in Mathlib). Applying Step 1 to $g$ with $k = 1$ yields
  for $s \neq 1$:
  $$\Phi_g(s) = -\frac{\frac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)} + \frac{1}{s - 1} - \gamma s + (1 - s)}{s(s - 1)}
  = -\frac{\frac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)} + \frac{1}{s - 1} + 1 - (1 + \gamma)s}{s(s - 1)},$$
  which matches Proposition 3.1(2)–(3) identically!

**Step 3: Symmetrization and positive sum (`poleData`).** Given an admissible family
$F = (\iota, N_i, \chi_i, a_i)$, each inverse character $\chi_i^{-1}$ is also admissible
($\chi_i \neq 1 \implies \chi_i^{-1} \neq 1$). Furthermore, complex conjugation satisfies
$\overline{L(s, \chi)} = L(\bar s, \chi^{-1})$ for all $s \in \mathbb C$ (`LFunction_conj`: proved
on $\Re s > 1$ from the Hurwitz/Dirichlet series and extended to $\mathbb C$ by analytic
continuation of the regularized Hurwitz zeta function!), whence $\overline{L'(1,\chi)/L(1,\chi)} = L'(1,\chi^{-1})/L(1,\chi^{-1})$
and therefore $\overline{G_\chi(y)} = G_{\chi^{-1}}(y)$ for all $y \in \mathbb R$ (`Gchi_conj`).
Consequently,
$$g_F(u) = \sum_{i \in \iota} a_i\,\Re G_{\chi_i}(e^u)
= \sum_{i \in \iota} \frac{a_i}{2}\bigl(G_{\chi_i}(e^u) + G_{\chi_i^{-1}}(e^u)\bigr).$$
Let $\Phi_{1,i}$ and $\Phi_{2,i}$ be the meromorphic functions produced by Step 2 for $\chi_i$ and
$\chi_i^{-1}$, and define
$$\Phi(s) := \sum_{i \in \iota} \frac{a_i}{2}\bigl(\Phi_{1,i}(s) + \Phi_{2,i}(s)\bigr).$$
Notice that $L(\bar w, \chi_i) = 0 \iff \overline{L(\bar w, \chi_i)} = 0 \iff L(w, \chi_i^{-1}) = 0$,
so a point $w$ with $\Re w > 0$ belongs to $Z(F)$ if and only if $L(w, \chi_i) = 0$ or
$L(w, \chi_i^{-1}) = 0$ for some $i \in \iota$. We verify the seven clauses of `PoleData`:
1. `meas`: Each $y \mapsto G_{\chi_i}(y)$ is measurable (`measurable_Gchi`, since $y \mapsto \lfloor y \rfloor_{\mathbb N}$
   is measurable), so $g_F$ is measurable.
2. `growth`: By Proposition 3.1(1), for $u \ge 0$ ($e^u \ge 1$), $\lvert g_F(u) \rvert \le \bigl(\sum_i a_i C_{\chi_i}\bigr) e^{2u}$.
3. `laplace`: Linearity of the integral on $\Re s > 2$ gives $\int_0^\infty g_F(u) e^{-su}\,du = \Phi(s)$.
4. `pos`: By definition of $Z(F)$, $\Re\rho > 0$ for all $\rho \in Z(F)$.
5. `diff`: On $\{\Re s > 0\} \setminus Z(F)$, we have $s \neq 0$ and $L(s,\chi_i) \neq 0$,
   $L(s,\chi_i^{-1}) \neq 0$ for all $i$, so every $\Phi_{1,i}, \Phi_{2,i}$ is analytic at $s$.
6. `iso`: Finite unions of locally finite zero sets remain locally finite.
7. `pole`: Fix $\rho \in Z(F)$, so $\Re\rho > 0$ (hence $\rho \neq 0$). By classical non-vanishing
   on $\Re s \ge 1$ (`charFamily_LFunction_ne_zero_of_one_le_re`, which invokes Mathlib's
   `riemannZeta_ne_zero_of_one_le_re` when $N_i = 1$ and `LFunction_ne_zero_of_one_le_re` when
   $\chi_i \neq 1$), no character in $F$ or its inverse can vanish on $\Re s \ge 1$; thus
   $\Re\rho < 1$, and in particular $\rho \neq 1$. Let $m_{1,i} = \operatorname{ord}_{s=\rho} L(s,\chi_i)$
   and $m_{2,i} = \operatorname{ord}_{s=\rho} L(s,\chi_i^{-1})$ (using $\zeta$ when $N_i = 1$). By Step 2,
   near $\rho$ we have $\Phi(s) = \frac{c_\rho}{s - \rho} + h(s)$ with $h$ analytic at $\rho$ and
   $$c_\rho = S_\rho \cdot \Bigl(-\frac{1}{\rho(\rho - 1)}\Bigr), \qquad
   S_\rho := \sum_{i \in \iota} \frac{a_i}{2}\bigl(m_{1,i} + m_{2,i}\bigr).$$
   Since $\rho \in Z(F)$, at least one $i_0 \in \iota$ has $m_{1,i_0} \ge 1$ or $m_{2,i_0} \ge 1$.
   Because $a_i > 0$ and $m_{1,i}, m_{2,i} \ge 0$ for **every** $i \in \iota$, we have
   $$S_\rho \ge \frac{a_{i_0}}{2} \cdot 1 > 0!$$
   Since $\rho \notin \{0, 1\}$, we have $-1 / (\rho(\rho - 1)) \neq 0$, so $c_\rho \neq 0$. Finally,
   suppose $\Im\rho = 0$, so $\rho = \beta \in \mathbb R$. Because $0 < \Re\rho < 1$, we have
   $0 < \beta < 1$, whence $\beta(\beta - 1) < 0$ and $-1 / (\beta(\beta - 1)) > 0$. Multiplying by
   $S_\rho > 0$ shows that $c_\beta$ is real (`c.im = 0`) and strictly positive (`0 < c.re`). $\square$

#### Why a zero contributes its order to the residue

**Motivation.** The sign test in `PoleData` starts with an integer supplied by local analysis. We need its exact value and sign even when several functions vanish at the same point. The calculation begins with the first nonzero coefficient of an analytic germ and ends with the coefficient of $(s-\rho)^{-1}$.

**Example.** Write $t=z-\rho$ and take $f(z)=t^m(1+at)$, with $a\in\mathbb C$ and $m\ge 0$ an integer. On a small disk $1+at$ is nonzero, since its value at the centre is $1$. For $m\ge1$ and $t\ne0$, the product rule gives $f'(z)=mt^{m-1}(1+at)+at^m$. Dividing by $f$ gives $f'/f=m/t+a/(1+at)$. The second term extends across $t=0$ with value $a$. For $m=0$, the quotient is just $a/(1+at)$ throughout the disk. A double zero thus gives a simple pole with residue $2$.

**Definitions.** A germ at $\rho$ records a function up to agreement on some open disk about $\rho$. The zero germ vanishes on a whole disk. A punctured disk deletes its centre. For analytic $f$, Mathlib's `analyticOrderAt f ρ` takes values in $\mathbb N\cup\{\infty\}$, with $\infty$ for the zero germ. For a finite order, `analyticOrderNatAt` gives the same integer in $\mathbb N$. The natural-valued definition uses `toNat`, which sends $\infty$ to $0$. The full definition also uses $0$ for a function failing the analyticity hypothesis. Both conventions make the hypotheses below essential. A residue here is the coefficient $c$ in a punctured-disk expansion $c/(z-\rho)+h(z)$ with $h$ analytic at $\rho$. It is unique, since multiplying by $z-\rho$ and taking the limit gives $c$. [Mathlib, `Order.lean:47–77`][ch3-order-def]

**Local order and residue lemma [Lean, read].** Suppose $f$ is analytic at $\rho$ and its germ there is nonzero. There is a unique $m\in\mathbb N$ such that on a disk
$f(z)=(z-\rho)^m g(z)$ for some $g$ analytic at $\rho$ with $g(\rho)\ne0$. This $m$ is the finite analytic order, and $m=0$ exactly when $f(\rho)\ne0$. After shrinking the disk, $g$ is nowhere zero, and on the punctured disk $f'/f=m/(z-\rho)+h(z)$, with $h=g'/g$ analytic on the disk. In particular $m>0$ gives a simple pole with positive integer residue.

**Proof.**

1. Choose a convergent local series $p$ for $f$. Over $\mathbb C$, a multilinear coefficient is determined by its evaluation on all ones, giving the usual scalar coefficient $b_j$. The zero series would make $f$ zero throughout its convergence disk, so $p\ne0$. Let $m$ be the least index with $b_m\ne0$. This is `p.order` in the proof of existence. [Mathlib, `IsolatedZeros.lean:115`][ch3-isolated-zero] [Mathlib, `IsolatedZeros.lean:185–201`][ch3-isolated-exists]
2. For a function $F$, use the difference quotient $D_\rho F(z)=(F(z)-F(\rho))/(z-\rho)$ away from $\rho$, and set $D_\rho F(\rho)=F'(\rho)$. The source calls this `dslope`. It shows that $D_\rho F$ has coefficients shifted one place. Off the centre it subtracts the constant term of the convergent sum, reindexes the tail and multiplies by $(z-\rho)^{-1}$. At the centre the new constant coefficient is the old linear coefficient, which equals the derivative. Induction gives $D_\rho^k f$ the shifted series with coefficients $b_{k+j}$. In particular $D_\rho^k f(\rho)=0$ for $k<m$, and $D_\rho^m f(\rho)=b_m\ne0$. [Mathlib, `IsolatedZeros.lean:78–106`][ch3-isolated-shift]
3. For each $k<m$, the value zero at $\rho$ gives $D_\rho^k f(z)=(z-\rho)D_\rho^{k+1} f(z)$, including at the centre. Iterate to get the factorization with $g=D_\rho^m f$. Its shifted series proves analyticity and its constant coefficient proves nonvanishing at the centre. Continuity gives nonvanishing on a smaller disk. The same cancellation rules out such a factorization for a zero germ, since it would force $g=0$ on a punctured disk and hence $g(\rho)=0$. [Mathlib, `DSlope.lean:145–156`][ch3-dslope] [Mathlib, `IsolatedZeros.lean:185–201`][ch3-isolated-exists]
4. For uniqueness, if $m>n$ are exponents with factors $g$ and $j$ respectively, cancel $(z-\rho)^n$ on a punctured disk. Then $j(z)=(z-\rho)^{m-n}g(z)$ there and its limit gives $j(\rho)=0$, a contradiction. Mathlib proves the integer-exponent version by cancellation followed by the local identity principle for the two analytic germs, then restricts to natural exponents. The source obtains this local identity principle by applying the isolated-zero dichotomy to the difference of the two analytic germs. The order definition chooses the exponent from Step 3 in the nonzero-germ branch, and the characterization for a given finite exponent uses this uniqueness. The infinite case is precisely local vanishing, including the reverse direction because local equality to zero implies analyticity. The bridge `analyticOrderAt_ne_top` converts the finite value to the natural order and supplies the corresponding factor. Evaluation at $\rho$ shows $m=0$ if and only if $f(\rho)\ne0$. [Mathlib, `IsolatedZeros.lean:125–180`][ch3-isolated-unique] [Mathlib, `Order.lean:71–134`][ch3-order-bridge]
5. In `logDeriv_local`, use the infinite-order equivalence to exclude $\infty$, then take $g$ from the finite-order bridge. Analyticity gives $g'$ analytic and hence $h=g'/g$ analytic near $\rho$. At a point $z$ of a small punctured disk, the factorization holds on a neighborhood of $z$, so the derivatives there agree. For $m>0$ the product rule gives the numerator $m(z-\rho)^{m-1}g(z)+(z-\rho)^m g'(z)$. Both $z-\rho$ and $g(z)$ are nonzero there. Division gives the stated principal part and $h$. For $m=0$, $f=g$ locally and the quotient is $h$. This is the zero/successor split in the archive proof. [Archive A2, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/LogDerivLocal.lean:9–39`][ch3-archive]

**Notes on the centre and on signs.** The archive uses `deriv f s / f s` directly. Mathlib's total function `logDeriv f` is this quotient. Division by zero assigns $0$ at a zero of $f$, and the total derivative assigns $0$ at a point where differentiability fails. Laurent expansions describe the punctured germ, so the assigned centre value has no effect on the residue. When $m>0$, subtracting $m/(z-\rho)$ on the punctured disk gives an analytic extension with centre value $g'(\rho)/g(\rho)$. When $m=0$, the quotient itself is analytic at the centre. No branch of a logarithm enters the proof. [Mathlib, `LogDeriv.lean:34–41`][ch3-logderiv]

For a finite list of functions satisfying the lemma at the same $\rho$, use the intersection of the finitely many disks. The sum of the logarithmic derivatives with real weights $w_i$ has residue $\sum_i w_i m_i$. With nonnegative weights the coefficient is nonnegative, and it is positive exactly when some $w_i>0$ multiplies an $m_i>0$. With strictly positive weights any zero in the list suffices. A zero with weight zero can disappear from the pole set. For example orders $2,1,0$ with weights $1/2,3,1$ give residue $4$.

In `poleData_abstract`, the entire input is nonzero at $1$, so the identity principle excludes the zero germ at every point. At a zero $\rho\ne0,1$, the prefactor $B(s)=-1/(s(s-1))$ multiplies the residue by $B(\rho)$. Indeed $(B(s)-B(\rho))/(s-\rho)$ extends analytically, so the variation of $B$ contributes only to the analytic term. The normalization at $s=1$ uses a separate removable extension, as in Theorem 3.2. [Archive A2, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/poleData.lean:22–36,50–85`][ch3-archive]

The family uses the two functions for $\chi_i$ and $\chi_i^{-1}$ with weight $a_i/2$ each. The conjugation identity (`LFunction_conj`) pairs a zero at $\rho$ for the inverse character with a zero at $\bar\rho$ for the original, with the same order. Indeed conjugating both the variable and the coefficients in the local factorization gives the new analytic factor, whose centre value remains nonzero. For zeta the regularizing factor $s-1$ is nonzero at every zero under discussion and leaves its order unchanged. Nonreal conjugate points give conjugate residues at their respective points. At a real point, the two members contribute at that same point, with both multiplicities included even if the characters coincide. Thus $S_\rho=\sum_i (a_i/2)(m_i(\rho)+m_i^\vee(\rho))>0$ at each zero in the symmetrized set by strict positivity of the weights. The residue of the family transform is $B(\rho)S_\rho$, which is nonzero. At a real zero $\beta$, the half-plane restriction and nonvanishing on $\Re s\ge1$ give $0<\beta<1$, hence $B(\beta)S_\beta=S_\beta/(\beta(1-\beta))>0$. This is the real-pole sign required by `PoleData`. [Archive A2, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/poleData.lean:137–149,191–232,241–261,336–394`][ch3-archive] [Archive A2, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/LFunctionConj.lean:88`][ch3-archive]

The new proof reading here covers the finite-order characterizations and the existence and uniqueness of the local power factor, including the iterated difference-quotient construction. The [next subsection](#why-a-nonzero-analytic-germ-has-a-first-nonzero-coefficient) proves coefficient uniqueness and the scalar change of centre used by the identity principle. The archive citations above use A2, sha256 `9631b4f020199e580ba86178cb56c6e9b09f690c2369a80dc4334aa93f49c7fc`. The Mathlib links use the manifest commit `0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474`.

#### Why a nonzero analytic germ has a first nonzero coefficient

**Motivation.** In the local order lemma, the first nonzero coefficient tells us which power to extract. Two facts make this usable in §3.4.1. The coefficient sequence belongs to the germ, even if we choose a smaller disk to represent it. And for the entire function in `poleData_abstract`, the single value $g(1)\ne0$ excludes a zero germ at every centre. The first fact uses a tail estimate. The second uses the same uniqueness after a change of centre.

**Examples.** Put $t=z-\rho$. For $f(\rho+t)=t^3/(1-t)$ on $|t|<1$, the geometric series gives $b_0=b_1=b_2=0$ and $b_{3+j}=1$ for every $j\ge0$. The factor left after the cube is removed has value $1$ at the centre. For a zero germ, take a function on the union of the disjoint unit disks about $0$ and $3$, equal to $0$ on the first disk and $1$ on the second. Its expansion at $0$ has every coefficient zero, so there is no first index. The value at $3$ illustrates why using a nonzero value to exclude a zero germ requires connectedness.

**Definitions.** A series represents $f$ on $D(x,R)=\{z:|z-x|<R\}$ if it converges to $f(x+t)$ for every $|t|<R$. The radius $R>0$ of this witnessing disk may be smaller than the convergence radius. In Mathlib a one-variable formal multilinear series has maps $p_n$ as coefficients. Multilinearity gives $p_n(v_1,\ldots,v_n)=b_n\prod_{j=1}^n v_j$ with $b_n=p_n(1,\ldots,1)$, with the empty product interpreted as $1$. The ordinary neighbourhood quantifier `∀ᶠ z in 𝓝 x` means that a property holds throughout some disk about $x$, including $x$. In `𝓝[≠] x` the disk is punctured. On a set $U$, `AnalyticOnNhd` gives a full ambient disk at every $x\in U$. Connectedness enters through `IsPreconnected U`, the condition ruling out a separation into two nonempty relatively open pieces. [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/Basic.lean:69–105`][ch3-series-basic-def] [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/Uniqueness.lean:30–39,159–169`][ch3-series-def-unique]

**Lemma [Lean, read].** A convergent expansion at a centre is unique as an expansion of the germ there. Its coefficients all vanish exactly when the germ is zero. Otherwise its first nonzero index $m$ is finite and the series factors as $f(x+t)=t^m H(t)$ with $H$ analytic near $0$ and $H(0)=b_m\ne0$. If $f$ is analytic on a neighbourhood of a connected set $U$ and has a zero germ at any point of $U$, then it vanishes on $U$.

**Proof.**

1. **Recover each coefficient by a tail estimate.** Fix $0<r<R$. Since $\sum_{n\ge0}b_nr^n$ converges, its terms are bounded, say $|b_n|r^n\le M$ for all $n$. For $|t|\le r/2$ and any $N\ge0$, absolute summation yields
   $$\left|f(x+t)-\sum_{n<N} b_nt^n\right|
   \le M\sum_{n\ge N}(|t|/r)^n
   \le 2M r^{-N}|t|^N.$$
   Here evaluating the geometric bound at $t=0$ uses $t^0=1$. If $f$ vanishes on a disk about $x$, use strong induction on $k$. Once $b_n=0$ for $n<k$, the estimate with $N=k+1$ gives $|b_k||t|^k\le 2M r^{-k-1}|t|^{k+1}$ for all sufficiently small $t$. Divide for $t\ne0$ and let $t\to0$ to obtain $b_k=0$, including at $k=0$. Conversely, all coefficients zero gives zero throughout the witnessing disk. For two expansions of the same germ, restrict to a common disk of equality, subtract the series and apply this argument. This is the scalar proof of Mathlib's induction on the truncation and its cancellation of the degree-$k$ contribution by scaling. [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/Basic.lean:683–712,753–773`][ch3-series-tail] [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/ConvergenceRadius.lean:162–194,241–258`][ch3-series-radius] [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/Uniqueness.lean:46–125`][ch3-series-unique]

2. **Extract the first power.** If the germ is nonzero, its expansion cannot have every coefficient zero. The nonempty set $\{n\in\mathbb N:b_n\ne0\}$ has a least element $m$. For $0<r<R$, $\sum_{n\ge0}|b_n|r^n$ converges by the geometric estimate from a larger radius $r'<R$. Consequently
   $$\sum_{j\ge0}|b_{m+j}|r^j=r^{-m}\sum_{n\ge m}|b_n|r^n<\infty.$$
   Thus $H(t)=\sum_{j\ge0}b_{m+j}t^j$ represents an analytic germ at $0$ and has the stated centre value. Reindexing the sum gives $f(x+t)=t^mH(t)$ throughout the witnessing disk, including $t=0$. Since $H$ is continuous and $H(0)\ne0$, it stays nonzero on some smaller disk, so $f$ has no zero there away from $x$. In the pinned proof, the implication from a nonzero germ to a nonzero series is the contrapositive of the zero-series direction of `locally_zero_iff`. Its other direction uses the uniqueness just proved. The source builds the shifted series by iterating `dslope`, as in the local order lemma. [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/IsolatedZeros.lean:78–116,125–130,185–201`][ch3-series-isolated] [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Calculus/FormalMultilinearSeries.lean:276–309`][ch3-series-first]

3. **Change the centre inside the disk.** For $y=x+a$ with $|a|<R$, set $c_k=\sum_{\ell\ge0}\binom{k+\ell}{k} b_{k+\ell}a^\ell$. For any $0<v<R-|a|$ the absolute sum satisfies
   $$\sum_{n\ge0}\sum_{k=0}^n |b_n|\binom nk |a|^{n-k}v^k
       =\sum_{n\ge0}|b_n|(|a|+v)^n<\infty.$$
   The equality is the finite binomial theorem in each row. For a fixed $k$, the corresponding subseries is absolutely summable after division by $v^k>0$, so each $c_k$ exists. The same estimate bounds $\sum_k |c_k|v^k$. Given $|w|<R-|a|$, choose $v$ with $|w|<v<R-|a|$. Absolute convergence now permits grouping the expansion of $(a+w)^n$ by the power of $w$ instead of by $n$, giving $f(y+w)=\sum_{k\ge0}c_kw^k$. In the multilinear proof, each binomial entry corresponds to the choice of slots filled by $a$, with the other slots filled by $w$. Regrouping those slot choices has the same absolute majorant. [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/ChangeOrigin.lean:64–150,164–226,258–294,312–341`][ch3-series-recentre]

4. **Propagate local vanishing.** Let $V$ be the ambient set of all points where $f$ has a zero germ. This set is open because any point inside a zero disk has a smaller zero disk. To prove relative closedness on $U$, take $x\in U\cap\overline V$ and a positive finite witnessing radius $R$ at $x$. Choose $y\in V$ with $|x-y|<R/2$. Step 3 gives an expansion at $y$ on $D(y,R/2)$, which contains $x$. The germ at $y$ is zero, so Step 1 makes every translated coefficient zero. This gives a whole zero disk around $x$ and therefore $x\in V$. Thus $V\cap U$ is open and closed in $U$. Since it contains the initial point, connectedness gives $U\subseteq V$, hence $f=0$ on $U$. The proof body in Mathlib uses this propagation in a complete target. Its general statement first embeds the target into its completion by an injective continuous linear map, propagates there and then uses injectivity. Here the target $\mathbb C$ is already complete. [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/Uniqueness.lean:159–217`][ch3-series-propagate] $\square$

**Notes.** The global argument starts from a whole zero neighbourhood. The punctured-neighbourhood version starts from zeros arbitrarily close to the centre, meaning every punctured disk contains a zero. Step 2 rules out that condition for a nonzero germ, and Step 4 then propagates the resulting local vanishing. The archive calls the whole-neighbourhood version directly with $U=\mathbb C$. In `poleData_abstract`, if a germ at any $\rho$ were zero, the conclusion at $1$ would contradict $g(1)\ne0$. This supplies the nonzero-germ hypothesis used by `logDeriv_local`. The shifted factor $z\mapsto H(z-\rho)$ supplies the witness for the finite-order characterization, so $m$ is exactly `analyticOrderNatAt f ρ` in the local order and residue lemma above. [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/IsolatedZeros.lean:214–217`][ch3-series-frequent] [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/IsolatedZeros.lean:125–139`][ch3-series-isolated] [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Analytic/Order.lean:71–77,86–118`][ch3-series-order] [Archive A2, `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/poleData.lean:22–36,62–65` and `src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/LogDerivLocal.lean:9–17`][ch3-series-archive]

The classical reference gives the Taylor expansion in a disk, the first-coefficient definition of multiplicity and uniqueness of analytic continuation from agreement near an interior limit point. The reading here covers the named Mathlib proof bodies for coefficient uniqueness, the change of centre and propagation, in their application to one complex variable. The next subsection proves the differentiable-to-analytic bridge for the complex scalar application, starting from annular invariance. The archive is A2, SHA-256 `9631b4f020199e580ba86178cb56c6e9b09f690c2369a80dc4334aa93f49c7fc`, with Mathlib fixed at `0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474`. [NIST DLMF, §1.10(i)–(ii)][ch3-series-dlmf]

#### From complex differentiability to a power series

The local factorization used above starts with an analytic germ. The input $g$ to `poleData_abstract` is given as complex differentiable everywhere. A circle around a point supplies the missing data. Its boundary values determine all the coefficients at once, and the gap between the contour and the points of evaluation controls the error after truncation.

**A first example.** At the origin, $1/(2-z)$ suggests the coefficients $2^{-n-1}$ by the finite geometric identity. A contour proof of the general result must explain how to recover those numbers directly from the boundary values and how to control the approximation on a whole disk.

**Disks, orientation and coefficients.** Let $R>0$, let $D(a,R)=\{z:|z-a|<R\}$, and write $C_R$ for its boundary with the positive (counterclockwise) orientation. Here $|\cdot|$ is the complex modulus. For a continuous integrand $H$ on $C_R$, use

$$
\oint_{C_R} H(\zeta)\,d\zeta
=\int_0^{2\pi} H(a+Re^{it})\,iRe^{it}\,dt,
\qquad
\left|\oint_{C_R} H(\zeta)\,d\zeta\right|\le 2\pi R\max_{C_R}|H|.
$$

The bound follows from the triangle inequality for the integral and the constant speed $R$. For a continuous boundary function $f$, define its Cauchy coefficients by

$$
a_n=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C_R}\frac{f(\zeta)}{(\zeta-a)^{n+1}}\,d\zeta,\qquad n\ge 0.
$$

**Lemma (expansion and uniform error).** Suppose $f$ is continuous on $\overline{D(a,R)}$ and complex differentiable at every point of $D(a,R)$. In particular, these hypotheses hold if $f$ is complex differentiable on an open neighborhood of the closed disk. Put $M=\max_{C_R}|f|$. Then $|a_n|\le M/R^n$ and $f(a+w)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_nw^n$ for $|w|<R$. For $0\le r<R$, $q=r/R$, and any integer $N\ge 0$,

$$
\sup_{|w|\le r}\left|f(a+w)-\sum_{n=0}^N a_nw^n\right|
\le M\frac{q^{N+1}}{1-q}.
$$

**Proof.** First work with the normalized Cauchy integral $K(a+w)=(2\pi i)^{-1}\oint_{C_R}f(\zeta)/(\zeta-a-w)\,d\zeta$, using only continuity on the circle. On $C_R$ put $u=w/(\zeta-a)$. For $|w|\le r$, we have $|u|\le q<1$ and

$$
\frac{1}{\zeta-a-w}
=\frac{1}{\zeta-a}\sum_{n=0}^{N}u^n
+\frac{u^{N+1}}{(\zeta-a)(1-u)}.
$$

The last term has modulus at most $q^{N+1}/[R(1-q)]$, since $|1-u|\ge 1-q$. Every finite summand and the full kernel are continuous on the contour. After multiplication by $f$ and integration, the length bound cancels $R^{-1}$ and the normalization cancels $2\pi$. This gives the claimed error for $K$, uniformly for $|w|\le r$. Finite linearity identifies the integrated polynomial with $\sum_{n=0}^N a_nw^n$. The integrated error tends uniformly to zero, which justifies integrating the infinite series term by term. The coefficient bound follows from the same length estimate applied to the defining integral. In particular, $\sum_n |a_n|r^n$ is bounded by $M/(1-q)$. Each interior point lies in such a smaller disk, so the series represents $K$ throughout $D(a,R)$.

It remains to identify $K(b)$ with $f(b)$ for $b\in D(a,R)$. The input is [annular invariance of the weighted circle integral](#weighted-circle-integrals-and-the-rectangle-theorem) [Lean, read]. If $0<t<R$ and $G$ is continuous on $t\le|\zeta-a|\le R$ and complex differentiable in the interior except on a countable set, the integrals of $G(\zeta)/(\zeta-a)$ on the two positively oriented circles agree. We use the following consequences of this input. [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean:315–320`][ch3-cauchy-annulus]

If $G$ is continuous off the center on the closed disk, complex differentiable off a countable set in the punctured open disk, and tends to $L$ at $a$, use annular invariance to shrink the radius to $t$. Parametrization gives $\oint_{|\zeta-a|=t}\,d\zeta/(\zeta-a)=2\pi i$. The difference between the original weighted integral and $2\pi iL$ has modulus at most $2\pi \max_{|\zeta-a|=t}|G(\zeta)-L|$, which tends to zero. Taking $G(\zeta)=(\zeta-a)H(\zeta)$ yields the disk Cauchy–Goursat conclusion $\oint_{C_R}H\,d\zeta=0$ for $H$ continuous on the closed disk and complex differentiable off a countable set in its interior. [Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean:378–453`][ch3-cauchy-center]

Now let $H(\zeta)=(f(\zeta)-f(b))/(\zeta-b)$ for $\zeta\ne b$ and $H(b)=f'(b)$. The derivative gives continuity at $b$, and the quotient rule gives complex differentiability away from $b$ in the open disk. The disk conclusion therefore applies with the exceptional set $\{b\}$. To integrate the remaining constant numerator, use the already established kernel expansion with boundary function $1$. Direct parametrization gives

$$
\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C_R}(\zeta-a)^{-n-1}\,d\zeta
=\frac{1}{2\pi R^n}\int_0^{2\pi}e^{-int}\,dt.
$$

This is $1$ for $n=0$ and $0$ otherwise. Thus $(2\pi i)^{-1}\oint_{C_R}d\zeta/(\zeta-b)=1$. Splitting $f(\zeta)/(\zeta-b)$ into $H(\zeta)+f(b)/(\zeta-b)$ gives $K(b)=f(b)$, the Cauchy value formula, and completes the proof. [Mathlib, §Cauchy integral formula, lines 467–526][ch3-cauchy-value] [Mathlib, kernel integral, lines 729–746][ch3-cauchy-kernel-value]

**Coefficients and errors by hand.** Take $a=0$, $R=1$ for both functions below. On the unit circle the coefficient formula is the Fourier average $(2\pi)^{-1}\int_0^{2\pi} f(e^{it})e^{-int}\,dt$. For $f(z)=1/(2-z)$, the finite geometric identity applied to $e^{it}/2$ gives a series on this circle with tail bounded by $2^{-N-1}$ after degree $N$. Hence the Fourier integral kills all modes except the $n$th, giving $a_n=2^{-n-1}$. Here $M=1$ since $|2-\zeta|\ge 1$ with equality at $\zeta=1$. The lemma bounds the Taylor error on $|z|\le 1/2$ by $2^{-N}$. The finite identity gives the exact remainder $(z/2)^{N+1}/(2-z)$, with the sharper bound $(2/3)4^{-N-1}$ there.

For $F(z)=z^3/(2-z)$, the same boundary expansion gains a factor $e^{3it}$. The Fourier average gives $a_0=a_1=a_2=0$ and $a_n=2^{2-n}$ for $n\ge3$. Thus the zero is triple, with leading term $z^3/2$. Again $M=1$. For $N\ge3$ the error on $|z|\le 1/2$ is bounded by $2^{-N}$ by the lemma, and the exact tail $z^3(z/2)^{N-2}/(2-z)$ gives the sharper bound $4^{-(N-2)}/12$. In particular, the error after $z^3/2$ is at most $1/48$ there.

**Note on the formal interface.** On an open differentiability domain one chooses a positive-radius closed disk contained in the domain. In pinned Mathlib the Cauchy coefficient is encoded as the multilinear map $p_n(v_1,\ldots,v_n)=a_n v_1\cdots v_n$. The radius bound, positivity of the radius and the sum on the open disk form `HasFPowerSeriesOnBall`. Mathlib uses dominated convergence for the kernel series. In the circle parameter, the derivative of the path cancels the inverse radius in the kernel and the majorant for term $n$ is $|f(a+Re^{it})|(|w|/R)^n$. Its sum is integrable by circle integrability and $|w|<R$. The general value-formula proof also uses continuity of $K$ from that independent kernel expansion to pass across the countable exceptional set. The ball witness gives `AnalyticAt` at its center. This supplies both the entire germs at line 30 and the germ of the difference quotient on the open nonvanishing set at line 60 of A2's `poleData_abstract`. The entire germs feed the existing zero-germ and local-factor arguments. The cited expansion and bridge bodies are [Lean, read]. The following subsection proves the annulus and rectangle steps. The proof of the planar divergence theorem follows the annular argument below. #### Weighted circle integrals and the rectangle theorem

The preceding value formula uses the freedom to move a positively oriented circle inward while keeping the kernel pole at its center. The region swept out by this motion is an annulus. Its two boundary circles carry opposite boundary orientations even though the two circle integrals in the invariance statement both run counterclockwise. A logarithmic rectangle computes their difference by a single application of Cauchy–Goursat.

**Examples.** Write $C(c,t)$ for the counterclockwise circle $z=c+te^{i\theta}$, with $0\le\theta\le 2\pi$ and $t>0$. Then $dz/(z-c)=i\,d\theta$. For a constant $G(z)=A$, the weighted integral is $2\pi i A$. For $G(z)=(z-c)^m$ with integer $m\ge1$, the same integral is
$i t^m\int_0^{2\pi}e^{im\theta}\,d\theta=0$, since $e^{2\pi i m}=1$. Thus a centered simple kernel detects the constant term in these examples.

A pole away from the center gives a second test. Let $c=0$ and put the pole at $1$. On $C(0,1/2)$ the kernel $1/(z-1)$ expands as $-\sum_{n\ge0}z^n$. Multiplication by $dz=iz\,d\theta$ leaves only positive Fourier frequencies, whose integrals vanish. On $C(0,2)$ the kernel expands as $\sum_{n\ge0}z^{-n-1}$, and multiplication by $dz$ gives $i\sum_{n\ge0}z^{-n}\,d\theta$. The constant term contributes $2\pi i$ and all others integrate to zero. Both geometric expansions converge uniformly on their respective circles, with term bounds proportional to $(1/2)^n$. If the inner radius is instead $3/2$, the outer expansion applies on both circles and both integrals equal $2\pi i$. The hole then contains the pole, and the positively oriented boundary integral of the annulus, outer minus inner, is zero.

**Rectangles and their boundaries.** For $Q=[a,b]\times[u,v]$ in the coordinate $z=x+iy$, with $a<b$ and $u<v$, the positive traversal follows the bottom, right, top and left edges in that order. Its integral is
$$
\int_{\partial Q}F(z)\,dz=
\int_a^b [F(x+iu)-F(x+iv)]\,dx+
i\int_u^v [F(b+iy)-F(a+iy)]\,dy.
$$
Each edge integral exists for $F$ continuous on $Q$.

**Lemma (rectangle Cauchy–Goursat).** [Lean, read] Let $F$ be continuous on $Q$ and complex differentiable at each point of its interior outside a countable set $S$. Then $\int_{\partial Q}F(z)\,dz=0$. No derivative is required on the edges, and the two opposing edge terms cancel if a side length is zero. [Pinned Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean:266–276`][ch3-annulus-rect]

**Proof.** Use the planar divergence theorem with countable exceptions [Lean, read], proved below using the Bochner-to-box comparison. In the form used here, two complex-valued components $P,H$ are continuous on the closed rectangle and real Fréchet differentiable in its interior outside a countable set. Given real-linear derivative fields $D_P,D_H$ agreeing with those derivatives there and with integrable sum $D_P(1,0)+D_H(0,1)$ on the rectangle, its area integral is the flux
$$
\int_u^v [P(b,y)-P(a,y)]\,dy+
\int_a^b [H(x,v)-H(x,u)]\,dx.
$$
The complex-valued statement can also be read componentwise over the reals. Take $P(x,y)=iF(x+iy)$ and $H(x,y)=-F(x+iy)$. This flux is exactly the displayed boundary integral. Under the coordinate map $(x,y)\mapsto x+iy$, the divergence becomes $iD_F(1)-D_F(i)$ for the real derivative field of $F$. The pinned rectangle proof uses the equivalent convention $P=-iF$, $H=F$ with reversed vertical integration limits.

At every interior point outside $S$, complex differentiability gives the real derivative $h\mapsto F'(z)h$. Use this field there and the zero map on the exceptional set and boundary. Every map in the field is complex linear, so $D_F(i)=iD_F(1)$ everywhere. The divergence is the identically zero function, hence integrable. The argument requires integrability of this combination of derivatives. It makes no continuity or separate integrability assumption on the partial derivatives. The flux is therefore zero. $\square$ [Pinned Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean:186–222`][ch3-annulus-green] [Planar divergence theorem, `Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/DivergenceTheorem.lean:503–514`][ch3-annulus-div]

**Proposition (weighted annular invariance).** [Lean, read] Let $0<r\le R$ and $A=\{z:r\le |z-c|\le R\}$. Suppose $G$ is continuous on $A$ and complex differentiable on $r<|z-c|<R$ outside a countable set $S$. Then
$$
\oint_{C(c,R)}\frac{G(z)}{z-c}\,dz=
\oint_{C(c,r)}\frac{G(z)}{z-c}\,dz.
$$
For $r<R$, this says that the integral over the oriented boundary of $A$ vanishes. In that boundary the inner circle is traversed clockwise, so that the region lies to the left on both components. [Pinned Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean:315–345`][ch3-annulus-weight]

**Proof.** Equality is immediate for $r=R$. Otherwise set $a=\log r$, $b=\log R$ and let $\phi(w)=c+e^w$ on $[a,b]\times[0,2\pi]$. Since $|\phi(x+iy)-c|=e^x$, the rectangle maps into $A$ and its interior maps into the open annulus. The composition $F=G\circ\phi$ is continuous on the rectangle and complex differentiable inside off $\phi^{-1}(S)$. That preimage is countable since a nonempty fiber of $\phi$ is a translate of $2\pi i\mathbb Z$.

On either vertical edge the pullback of $G(z)\,dz/(z-c)$ is $G(\phi(w))\,dw$, since $\phi'(w)=\phi(w)-c$. The horizontal edge terms cancel by $F(x+2\pi i)=F(x)$. On the right edge $w=b+iy$ traverses upward, giving the positive outer circle. On the left edge the downward traversal gives the negative inner circle. Thus the boundary integral of $F(w)\,dw$ is the difference of the two weighted integrals. Rectangle Cauchy–Goursat makes that difference zero. $\square$

**Corollary (ordinary integrals and avoidance of poles).** [Lean, read] If $H$ has the above annular regularity, take $G(z)=(z-c)H(z)$ to see that its ordinary circle integrals agree. In particular, if $f$ has that regularity, the corollary applies to $H(z)=f(z)/(z-p)$ whenever $p$ is outside the closed annulus, so $|p-c|<r$ or $|p-c|>R$. In either case the reciprocal is continuous and complex differentiable throughout a neighborhood of the annulus. The same conclusion holds if the quotient admits a continuous filling on the closed annulus at $p$, since one can add $p$ to the countable exceptional set. A genuine pole in the interior violates the continuity hypothesis even when it belongs to the countable exceptional set. A pole on a boundary circle can make the contour integral itself undefined. In the example with $p=1$, crossing the pole changes the integral by $2\pi i$ because the outer expansion acquires a $1/z$ term. More generally, for isolated poles and the usual residue theorem hypotheses, the difference of the two positive integrals is $2\pi i$ times the sum of residues between the circles. [Pinned Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean:351–363`][ch3-annulus-unweight] [NIST DLMF, §1.10(iv), (1.10.8)](https://dlmf.nist.gov/1.10#E8)

**Corollary (shrinking to the center).** [Lean, read] Let $R>0$. Suppose $G$ is continuous on $0<|z-c|\le R$, complex differentiable on $0<|z-c|<R$ off a countable set, and tends to $L$ as $z\to c$ with $z\ne c$. Then
$$
\oint_{C(c,R)}\frac{G(z)}{z-c}\,dz=2\pi i L.
$$
Indeed the integral equals its value on every positive radius $t\le R$. On that circle the difference from $2\pi iL$ is $i\int_0^{2\pi}[G(c+te^{i\theta})-L]\,d\theta$, with modulus bounded by $2\pi\max_{|z-c|=t}|G(z)-L|$. The punctured limit makes this bound tend to zero. The pole of $1/(z-c)$ stays inside every hole and no contour ever passes through it. For an ordinary integrand $H$ continuous on the closed disk and complex differentiable inside off a countable set, $G(z)=(z-c)H(z)$ has limit zero. This recovers the disk vanishing used in the preceding subsection. [Pinned Mathlib, `Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean:378–453`][ch3-annulus-shrink]

**Connection to the preceding value formula.** In the formal proof the last disk vanishing is invoked at line 479, on the filled difference quotient. This is the single point at which the chain above supplies the preceding subsection's identification $K(b)=f(b)$. It supplies the vanishing of the difference term, and the simple-kernel normalization from the preceding subsection gives the value. The normalized theorem uses that auxiliary proof at line 511. The independent kernel series and the subsequent passage to analytic germs in P5 belong to the preceding subsection.
