#### Rectangular divergence with countable exceptions

The preceding rectangle argument uses an integrable **sum** of two directional derivatives. In the complex-differentiable application that sum vanishes by linearity, even when there is no estimate on either derivative by itself. The classical proof for continuously differentiable fields uses the one-dimensional fundamental theorem of calculus on parallel slices and then Fubini. At the regularity needed here, a slice derivative may lack a separate Lebesgue integrability bound. We will apply one-dimensional calculus to the local affine approximations instead, add the face differences over a partition, and use the Bochner integral only for the sum.

**Two examples.** On $[-1,1]^2$, let $U(x,y)=\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$ and $V=0$. The field is continuous and its sole interior failure of differentiability is at the origin: the restriction to the $x$-axis is $|x|$. Off the origin the divergence is $x/\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$, of absolute value at most one. Assign it zero at the origin. Its area integral is zero by oddness in $x$. The right and left traces are both $\sqrt{1+y^2}$, so the flux is zero as well.

On $[-1,1]\times[0,1]$, set $U=1$ on the face $x=1$ and $U=0$ elsewhere, with $V=0$. The field is smooth in the open rectangle, its divergence representative is identically zero, and all face integrals exist. Yet the right trace contributes $\int_0^1 1\,dy=1$, and the other three contributions are zero. Continuity on the closed rectangle links the boundary traces to the interior. The area-null property of the boundary alone cannot supply that link.

**Definition.** Let $X$ be a real Banach space. For an ordered rectangle $Q=[a,b]\times[c,d]$ and continuous fields $U,V:Q\to X$, denote the outward face difference by
$$
\Phi(Q)=\int_c^d[U(b,y)-U(a,y)]\,dy+
         \int_a^b[V(x,d)-V(x,c)]\,dx .
$$
We use this same expression if an interval collapses. Write $e_1=(1,0)$ and $e_2=(0,1)$. Given assignments of bounded real-linear maps $A(p),B(p):\mathbb{R}^2\to X$, put $D(p)=A(p)e_1+B(p)e_2$. At points of differentiability the maps will be the respective Fréchet derivatives. Exceptional points and boundary points may carry arbitrary assignments, subject to integrability of $D$. Countable sets are area-null, and each edge is area-null by product measure because one coordinate is fixed. Thus modifications at those points leave the area integral unchanged.

**Integration input.** We use the Bochner-to-box integral comparison in its tagged-sum form. For a Bochner-integrable function $D$ on a positive-area rectangle $K$ and any $\eta>0$, there is a positive gauge $r(p)$ on the closed rectangle such that every sufficiently fine tagged rectangular partition has
$$
\left\|\sum_J |J|D(p_J)-\int_K D\right\|<\eta .
$$
Here the tags $p_J$ lie in the closures of their cells, and fineness means the closed cell lies in the ball of radius $r(p_J)$ about its tag, in the maximum norm. It suffices to use partitions with bounded aspect ratio. Mathlib supplies the comparison at its unrestricted McShane parameter, hence also for these partitions, and takes the cells half-open for integration. The closed and half-open choices differ by an area-null set. We also use Bochner Fubini and dominated convergence. [The next subsection](#fubini-and-the-box-comparison-on-a-rectangle) proves the comparison and Fubini from the pinned proof bodies and separates their hypotheses. [Bochner-to-box comparison](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Integrability.lean#L195-L301) · [Set-integral Fubini](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/Prod.lean#L507-L512)

**Lemma (local face estimates).** Let $K$ have positive side lengths, let $J\subseteq K$ be a positive-area closed subrectangle, and let $p\in J$. If the errors in the two affine approximations of $U,V$ at $p$, with linear terms $A(p),B(p)$, are each bounded on $J$ by $\lambda\|q-p\|_\infty$, then
$$
\|\Phi(J)-|J|D(p)\|\le 4\lambda C |J|,
$$
where $C$ bounds the ratio of the longer to the shorter side of $J$. If $U,V$ are merely continuous at $p$ relative to $K$, then for every $\tau>0$ the same difference has norm less than $\tau$ whenever $J$ is sufficiently close to $p$, uniformly over such cells, with no aspect-ratio requirement.

**Proof.** Write $w,h$ for the width and height of $J$. For the affine approximation to $U$, the one-dimensional fundamental theorem along each horizontal segment gives the increment $w A(p)e_1$. Integrating in the transverse variable multiplies it by $h$. The affine approximation to $V$ gives $hB(p)e_2$ on each vertical segment, and the transverse integration multiplies it by $w$. The contribution from the two $U$-remainders is bounded by $2h\lambda\max(w,h)$, and that from the $V$-remainders by $2w\lambda\max(w,h)$. The claimed bound follows since each of $w,h$ is at least $\max(w,h)/C$.

For the continuity estimate, take $J$ within radius $r$ of $p$, and let $\omega(r)$ bound the oscillation from $p$ of both functions there. Continuity gives $\omega(r)\to0$. Constant field values cancel in $\Phi(J)$, leaving a norm at most $2(h+w)\omega(r)$. Also $\bigl\||J|D(p)\bigr\|\le (2r)^2\|D(p)\|$. Since $w,h\le 2r$, both bounds tend to zero. $\square$

**Proposition (rectangular divergence).** Let $Q=[a,b]\times[c,d]$ with $a\le b$ and $c\le d$. Let $U,V$ be defined on the plane and continuous on $Q$. Suppose that away from a countable set $S$, at every point of the open rectangle they have real Fréchet derivatives $A,B$. If $D$ is Bochner integrable on $Q$, then
$$
\int_Q D=\Phi(Q).
$$
For arbitrary endpoint order, use the rectangle with sorted endpoints for the hypotheses. Use oriented interval integrals on both pairs of faces in the displayed definition and replace the area integral by $\int_a^b\!\left(\int_c^d D(x,y)\,dy\right)\,dx$. The identity then holds with these conventions.

**Proof.** First take a positive-area closed rectangle $K$ strictly inside $Q$. Thus at all points of $K\setminus S$, including the faces of $K$, the affine remainder estimates are available. A grid partition satisfies $\sum_J\Phi(J)=\Phi(K)$: each internal face cancels with the adjacent face, and the outer segments add up by interval additivity.

Fix $\eta>0$ and choose $C$ to be the aspect ratio of $K$. At an ordinary tag choose a radius using Fréchet differentiability with $\lambda=\eta/(4C|K|)$. The local face lemma bounds the error by $\eta |J|/|K|$ for each fine cell of aspect ratio at most $C$. Enumerate $S\cap K$ without repetitions as $s_j$, starting at $j=1$ and stopping if the set is finite. At $s_j$, choose a radius from the continuity estimate with $\tau=\eta 2^{-j}/4$. In a partition with disjoint interiors, at most four cells can have the same point in their closures. Each such cell occupies an open coordinate quadrant locally at the point, and two cells occupying the same quadrant would have overlapping interiors. Thus the total exceptional error is at most $\eta$. This summable budget uses countability and continuity at every allowed exception.

Take the smaller of these radii and the radius from the Bochner comparison at tolerance $\eta$. A finite partition for the resulting gauge can be found by bisecting both coordinates of any cell that admits no tag in its closure with the required radius. The resulting subrectangles all have aspect ratio $C$. An infinite subdivision tree would have an infinite nested branch by finite branching. The branch shrinks to a point $p$, which would be an admissible tag for all sufficiently small cells in that branch. Hence subdivision stops. The resulting partition satisfies the face cancellation by repeated application of additivity at each split. Summing the ordinary and exceptional bounds and using the comparison gives $\|\Phi(K)-\int_K D\|\le 3\eta$. Let $\eta$ decrease to zero.

Now exhaust the open rectangle by nested positive-area closed rectangles $K_n$ with all four endpoints tending to the endpoints of $Q$. The indicators of $K_n$ converge to one almost everywhere on $Q$ by the boundary-null observation, and $\|D\|$ dominates the restricted integrands, so $\int_{K_n}D\to\int_Q D$. On each moving face, uniform continuity on $Q$ implies uniform convergence to the corresponding boundary trace on the common transverse interval. The error is bounded by the transverse length of $Q$ times the uniform difference, plus the length of the omitted end strips times the supremum norm of the trace. Both tend to zero. Consequently $\Phi(K_n)\to\Phi(Q)$, with no derivative required on $\partial Q$.

If $a=b$, the area is zero, the $U$-traces coincide, and the integrals of the $V$-traces have interval length zero. If $c=d$, the $V$-traces coincide and the $U$-integrals have length zero. This includes a rectangle collapsed in both directions. For ordered endpoints, product Fubini applied to $D$ gives the stated iterated integral, using closed intervals in place of half-open intervals by null endpoint sets. Reversing either endpoint pair multiplies both sides of the oriented formula by $-1$: one pair of traces switches places and the other is integrated in reverse. This proves the assertion for every endpoint order. $\square$

**A check on separate integrability.** Let $H(t)=t^2\sin(1/t^2)$ for $t\ne 0$ and $H(0)=0$. On $[-1,1]^2$ take $U(x,y)=H(x+y)$ and $V(x,y)=-H(x+y)$. Both fields are everywhere differentiable, including along $x+y=0$ since $H'(0)=0$. The divergence vanishes pointwise. For $t>0$,
$$
H'(t)=2t\sin(1/t^2)-2t^{-1}\cos(1/t^2).
$$
The second term has infinite absolute integral near zero: substituting $s=1/t^2$ reduces it to $\int_{1}^{\infty} |\cos s|\,ds/s$, whose contributions on fixed-width intervals around $2\pi n$ dominate a harmonic series. Each horizontal slice with $|y|<1/2$ meets this singularity, so Tonelli gives $\int_Q|\partial_x U|=\infty$, and likewise for $|\partial_y V|$. The face differences cancel by exchanging the coordinates on the square. The proposition applies through $D=0$.

**Connection with the formal proof.** [Lean, read] The Bochner proof applies the GP box integral theorem on each smaller closed box. Its coordinate lemma estimates a face difference against the linear approximation times the volume, with a distortion bound at ordinary tags and a continuity bound at exceptional tags. The GP theorem adds the coordinate results before comparing with the Bochner integral of the sum. The proof then exhausts the open box, handles collapsed sides, specializes from indexed coordinates to pairs, and applies Fubini to the sum. The proof above gives the planar estimates directly and uses the named comparison for the sum. The underlying gauge convergence machinery and Bochner construction are inputs. [GP estimates and coordinate sum](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/DivergenceTheorem.lean#L65-L279) · [Bochner proof and planar specialization](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/DivergenceTheorem.lean#L108-L536)

[Lean, read] In the actual Cauchy wrapper, write $e(x,y)=x+iy$ and $F=f\circ e$. With $L(q)$ the given real-linear derivative assignment for $f$, the derivative assignment for $F$ is $M(p)=L(e(p))\circ e$. The two fields passed to the proposition are $U=-iF$ and $V=F$, with assignments $-iM$ and $M$. Since $e(e_1)=1$ and $e(e_2)=i$, their divergence is $-\bigl(iL(e(p))(1)-L(e(p))(i)\bigr)$. The actual horizontal endpoints are $\operatorname{Re}z,\operatorname{Re}w$, and the vertical endpoints are $\operatorname{Im}w,\operatorname{Im}z$. The vertical reversal restores the sign of the wrapper's area integrand. Pullback along $e$ preserves volume, so integrability of the given combination transfers with the minus sign. The preimage of the exceptional set is countable by injectivity, and continuity transfers on the closed rectangle. For the complex-differentiable specialization the assignments come from complex-linear maps by restriction of scalars, so $L(q)(i)=iL(q)(1)$. This supplies the zero-divergence case used in the preceding annular argument. [Cauchy wrapper and specialization](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean#L186-L276)

#### Fubini and the box comparison on a rectangle

**Motivation.** The rectangular divergence proof above treats one function, the divergence $D$, in three ways. The gauge argument on an inner rectangle $K$ controls tagged sums $\sum_J|J|D(p_J)$. The proposition is stated with the Bochner integral $\int_K D$. The Cauchy wrapper consumes an iterated integral $\int_a^b\bigl(\int_c^d D(x,y)\,dy\bigr)dx$. Two identifications were imported there as inputs: tagged sums against the Bochner integral, and the area integral against the iterated one. This subsection proves both from the pinned proof bodies and records which hypothesis pays for which step. Continuity at the countably many exceptional points pays only for the gauge estimate. Absolute integrability of $D$ pays for both identifications. A change of $D$ on a null set costs nothing in either identification, for two different reasons. The arbitrary derivative assignments at exceptional and boundary points enter as such changes.

**Two examples.** On $Q=[0,1]^2$ let $U(x,y)=2\sqrt x$ and $V=0$. Both are continuous on $Q$ and differentiable in the open square, and the divergence is $D(x,y)=x^{-1/2}$, with the value $0$ assigned on the edge $x=0$. Then $D$ is unbounded and $\int_Q D=2$, which is the flux $\int_0^1[U(1,y)-U(0,y)]\,dy$. No fixed mesh controls its tagged sums: a cell $[0,h]^2$ tagged at $(t,0)$ contributes $h^2t^{-1/2}$, which is unbounded as $t\to0^+$. The admissible radius must shrink near the edge. Hand computation 2 below follows the comparison proof through this example.

Next let $D_1$ be the indicator of $Q\cap\mathbb Q^2$, and let $D_2(1/2,y)=1/y$ for $0<y\le1$, with $D_2=0$ elsewhere. Both vanish off null sets, so both are Bochner integrable on $Q$ with integral $0$. Every vertical slice of $D_1$ vanishes off a countable set, so every inner integral is $0$. The slice of $D_2$ at $x=1/2$ is not integrable on $[0,1]$. Its inner integral therefore fails to exist on one vertical line, and the iterated integral is still $0$. Fixed-mesh tagged sums of $D_1$ equal $1$ for rational tags and $0$ for irrational tags, so $D_1$ has no Riemann integral. A gauge removes this ambiguity. Enumerate $Q\cap\mathbb Q^2=\{s_1,s_2,\dots\}$ and give $s_j$ the radius $r_j=2^{-1-j/2}\sqrt\varepsilon$ in the maximum norm, with any radius at the other points. Fine cells tagged at $s_j$ have disjoint interiors and lie in a square of area $(2r_j)^2=\varepsilon2^{-j}$. Every fine tagged sum of $D_1$ therefore lies in $[0,\varepsilon]$. The unbounded values of $D_2$ need the grouping of Lemma 3(b) below.

**Definitions.** Write $\lambda$ for Lebesgue measure on the Borel sets of $\mathbb R$ and $\lambda^2=\lambda\otimes\lambda$. In the pinned library the volume of $\mathbb R\times\mathbb R$ is this product by definition, and on a rectangle the restriction of the product is the product of the restrictions. For $s\subseteq\mathbb R^2$ and $x\in\mathbb R$ the slice is $s_x=\{y:(x,y)\in s\}$, and $F(x,\cdot)$ denotes the slice of a function. Let $X$ be a real Banach space. A function $F$ is Bochner integrable on a set if it agrees almost everywhere there with a strongly measurable function and $\int\|F\|<\infty$. Strongly measurable means a pointwise limit of finitely valued measurable functions. The integral depends only on the almost-everywhere class. A simple function is a finitely valued measurable function, equivalently a finite sum $\sum_i1_{s_i}c_i$ with disjoint measurable $s_i$. When a slice is not integrable we say that its integral does not exist. The formal library assigns the value $0$ instead. All statements below concern almost every slice, so the two conventions give the same results.

A tagged partition $\pi$ of a closed rectangle $K$ is a finite family of closed rectangles $J$ with disjoint interiors and union $K$, each with a tag $p_J\in K$. A tagged prepartition is such a family whose union need not be $K$. A gauge is a function $r:K\to(0,\infty)$, and $\pi$ is $r$-fine if each $J$ lies in the closed ball of radius $r(p_J)$ about $p_J$ in the maximum norm. Put $S(F,\pi)=\sum_J|J|F(p_J)$. The function $F$ has McShane integral $y$ on $K$ if for every $\varepsilon>0$ some gauge $r$ gives $\|S(F,\pi)-y\|\le\varepsilon$ for every $r$-fine tagged partition $\pi$. The GP sums of the preceding subsection also require $p_J\in J$, and they let the gauge depend on a bound for the aspect ratios of the cells. They test fewer partitions, so a McShane integral is also a GP integral with the same value. Constant gauges with tags in their cells give the Riemann integral.

**Named inputs.** The following remain [Lean] at the pinned commit. They belong to general integration theory and are not specific to this proof.

- (N1) The Bochner integral: linearity, $\|\int F\|\le\int\|F\|$, continuity in the $L^1$ norm, invariance under null modifications, the integrals of indicators, and the passage from the lower integral of an almost everywhere finite nonnegative function to the Bochner integral of its real values.
- (N2) Tonelli's theorem for nonnegative measurable functions on a product, and measurability of parametric integrals.
- (N3) Product measurable structure and the product measure: slices of measurable sets are measurable, the $\pi$–$\lambda$ theorem for the generating rectangles, the integration of measures defining the product, uniqueness of a $\sigma$-finite measure from its values on rectangles, and invariance of the product under the coordinate swap.
- (N4) Simple functions: decomposition into indicators with disjoint supports, density in $L^1$, and the approximating sequence with the three properties in step 2 of the proof of part 1(a).
- (N5) Inner closed and outer open approximation of sets for locally finite measures on $\mathbb R^2$.
- (N6) Foundations of the box integral: the integration parameters and their filters, linearity and uniqueness of limits, and the Henstock–Sacks inequality. The latter says the following. Let $r$ be a gauge such that the sums of $f$ over all $r$-fine tagged partitions of $K$ lie within $\delta$ of its integral. Then every $r$-fine tagged prepartition $\pi'$ satisfies $\|\sum_{J\in\pi'}(|J|f(p_J)-\int_Jf)\|\le\delta$, where $\int_Jf$ is the box integral over the cell. The [next subsection](#the-henstocksacks-inequality-tagged-sums-over-part-of-a-rectangle) proves the Henstock–Sacks inequality from the pinned body and uses the [epsilon–gauge equivalence](#from-the-box-filter-to-epsilon-and-gauges), [Cousin's lemma](#cousins-lemma-fine-tagged-partitions-of-a-box), [exact half-open complements](#exact-complements-by-splitting-half-open-boxes) and the [subbox Cauchy argument](#subbox-filters-and-the-cauchy-net-of-tagged-sums) in this section. [Parameters](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Filter.lean#L33-L87) · [Henstock–Sacks](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L578-L594)

**Theorem (integration supply on a rectangle).** [Lean, read], conditional on (N1)–(N6). Let $Q=[a,b]\times[c,d]$ with $a<b$ and $c<d$, and let $D:\mathbb R^2\to X$.

1. *Absolute integrability pays for both identifications.* Suppose that $D$ is Bochner integrable on $Q$.
   - (a) $D$ has McShane integral, hence also GP integral, equal to $\int_QD$ on $Q$.
   - (b) For almost every $x\in[a,b]$ the slice $D(x,\cdot)$ is integrable on $[c,d]$. The almost everywhere defined function $x\mapsto\int_c^dD(x,y)\,dy$ is integrable on $[a,b]$, and
     $$\int_QD=\int_a^b\Bigl(\int_c^dD(x,y)\,dy\Bigr)dx .$$
     The same holds with the order of the variables exchanged.
2. *Null modifications are absorbed.* If $D'=D$ almost everywhere on $Q$, then $D'$ satisfies 1(a) and 1(b) with the same value. In 1(a) the reason is that a function vanishing almost everywhere has McShane integral $0$. In 1(b) the reason is that for almost every $x$ the slices $D(x,\cdot)$ and $D'(x,\cdot)$ agree almost everywhere. A null set of slices of $D'$ may fail to be integrable.
3. *Countable exceptions are not used.* A countable set is null. The derivative assignments on the exceptional set and on $\partial Q$ therefore enter 1 and 2 only through the almost-everywhere class of $D$. The face-difference estimates of the preceding subsection pay for those assignments by continuity of $U,V$ and a summable budget, without any integrability of $D$.
4. *Absolute integrability cannot be dropped from 1(b).* Hand computation 3 gives a function on $[0,1]^2$ whose two iterated integrals are integrals of integrable functions of one variable and differ.

| Hypothesis | Gauge limit of face differences | Comparison, part 1(a) | Interchange, part 1(b) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Countable exceptional set $S$ | Paid by continuity at $S$ and a summable budget | Not used, since $S$ is null | Not used, since $S$ is null |
| Null modification of $D$ | Allowed only on $S$, where the budget absorbs arbitrary values | Null functions have McShane integral $0$ (Lemma 3(b)) | Slices agree almost everywhere for almost every $x$ (Lemma 1(b)) |
| $\int_Q\lVert D\rVert<\infty$ | Not used (Remark 1) | $L^1$ approximation in the third estimate | Finite slice measures, Tonelli bounds and the $L^1$ closure step |

**Lemma 1 (slices of product sets).** [Lean, read] Let $\mu,\nu$ be measures on $\mathbb R$, with $\nu$ a countable sum of finite measures, as is $\lambda$ restricted to an interval.

- (a) For every measurable $s\subseteq\mathbb R^2$ the function $x\mapsto\nu(s_x)$ is measurable, and $(\mu\otimes\nu)(s)=\int\nu(s_x)\,d\mu(x)$. For measurable $A,B\subseteq\mathbb R$, $(\mu\otimes\nu)(A\times B)=\mu(A)\nu(B)$.
- (b) If $(\mu\otimes\nu)(N)=0$, then $\nu(N_x)=0$ for $\mu$-almost every $x$. Hence a property that holds $\mu\otimes\nu$-almost everywhere holds, for $\mu$-almost every $x$, at $\nu$-almost every point of the slice.

*Proof.* (a) First let $\nu$ be finite, and let $\mathcal D$ be the class of measurable $s$ for which $x\mapsto\nu(s_x)$ is measurable. For a rectangle $A\times B$ the slice is $B$ when $x\in A$ and empty otherwise, so $\nu(s_x)=\nu(B)1_A(x)$. The class contains $\varnothing$. It is closed under complements because $\nu((s^c)_x)=\nu(\mathbb R)-\nu(s_x)$ with $\nu(\mathbb R)<\infty$. It is closed under countable disjoint unions because slices of disjoint sets are disjoint. Rectangles form a $\pi$-system generating the product $\sigma$-algebra, so $\mathcal D$ contains every measurable set by the $\pi$–$\lambda$ theorem. A countable sum of finite measures gives a countable sum of measurable functions. The product measure is defined by integrating the slice measures against $\mu$, and evaluating the definition on $s$ gives the formula for $(\mu\otimes\nu)(s)$. For $s=A\times B$ the integrand is $\nu(B)1_A$. (b) Choose a measurable $t\supseteq N$ with $(\mu\otimes\nu)(t)=0$. By (a) the integral of $x\mapsto\nu(t_x)$ vanishes, so $\nu(t_x)=0$ for almost every $x$, and $N_x\subseteq t_x$. For the second statement take $N$ to be the set where the property fails. $\square$ [Slice measurability](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Measure/Prod.lean#L71-L101) · [Definition and values](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Measure/Prod.lean#L169-L247) · [Null slices](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Measure/Prod.lean#L373-L435)

**Lemma 2 (slices of integrable functions).** [Lean, read] Let $\mu,\nu$ be countable sums of finite measures on $\mathbb R$, and let $F,G$ be Bochner integrable with respect to $\mu\otimes\nu$.

- (a) For $\mu$-almost every $x$ the slice $F(x,\cdot)$ is $\nu$-integrable. The functions $x\mapsto\int\|F(x,y)\|\,d\nu(y)$ and $x\mapsto\int F(x,y)\,d\nu(y)$ are $\mu$-integrable.
- (b) The iterated integral is additive, and
$$\Bigl\|\int\Bigl(\int F(x,y)\,d\nu\Bigr)d\mu-\int\Bigl(\int G(x,y)\,d\nu\Bigr)d\mu\Bigr\|\le\int\|F-G\|\,d(\mu\otimes\nu).$$

*Proof.* (a) Suppose first that $F$ is strongly measurable. Tonelli's theorem for $\|F\|$ gives $\int\|F\|\,d(\mu\otimes\nu)=\int\bigl(\int\|F(x,y)\|\,d\nu\bigr)d\mu$ in $[0,\infty]$. The left side is finite. So the inner integral is finite for almost every $x$, and for those $x$ the strongly measurable slice is integrable. The function $x\mapsto\int\|F(x,y)\|\,d\nu$ is therefore integrable. In general choose a strongly measurable $G_0$ equal to $F$ almost everywhere. By Lemma 1(b), for almost every $x$ the slices of $F$ and $G_0$ agree almost everywhere, so these conclusions transfer to $F$. The function $x\mapsto\int F(x,y)\,d\nu$ agrees almost everywhere with $x\mapsto\int G_0(x,y)\,d\nu$, which is measurable as a parametric integral. Its norm is at most $\int\|F(x,y)\|\,d\nu$, so it is integrable. (b) For almost every $x$ both slices are integrable. There the inner integral of $F+G$ is the sum of the inner integrals, and by (a) the outer integral is additive. Likewise, for almost every $x$, $\int F(x,\cdot)-\int G(x,\cdot)=\int(F-G)(x,\cdot)$, whose norm is at most $\int\|F(x,y)-G(x,y)\|\,d\nu$. Integrate in $x$ and apply Tonelli's theorem to $\|F-G\|$. $\square$ [Integrability on a product](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/Prod.lean#L126-L256) · [Sum rules and continuity](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/Prod.lean#L335-L437)

**Lemma 3 (gauges for indicators and for null functions).** [Lean, read] Let $K$ be a closed rectangle of positive area and let $c\in X$.

- (a) For measurable $s$, the function $1_sc$ has McShane integral $|s\cap K|\,c$ on $K$.
- (b) If $F=0$ almost everywhere on $K$, then $F$ has McShane integral $0$ on $K$. Consequently, if $F$ has McShane integral $y$ on $K$ and $G=F$ almost everywhere on $K$, then $G$ has McShane integral $y$.

*Proof.* (a) Fix $\varepsilon>0$. Choose a closed $C\subseteq s\cap K$ and an open $W\supseteq s\cap K$ with $|(s\cap K)\setminus C|<\varepsilon$ and $|W\setminus(s\cap K)|<\varepsilon$. For $p\in s\cap K$ choose $r(p)$ so that the closed ball about $p$ lies in $W$. For $p\in K\setminus s$ choose $r(p)$ so that this ball misses $C$, which is possible since $C$ is closed. Let $\pi$ be $r$-fine, and let $t$ be the union of the cells with tags in $s$. These cells lie in $W\cap K$. A cell with tag outside $s$ misses $C$, and the cells cover $K$, so $C\subseteq t$. Hence $|t|-|s\cap K|<\varepsilon$ and $|s\cap K|-|t|<\varepsilon$. Since $S(1_sc,\pi)=|t|\,c$, the tagged sum is within $\varepsilon\|c\|$ of $|s\cap K|\,c$.

(b) Let $N(p)=\lceil\|F(p)\|\rceil$, a nonnegative integer. For $n\ge1$ the set $\{N=n\}\cap K$ is null. Choose $\delta_n>0$ with $\sum_n\delta_n<\varepsilon$, and an open $W_n\supseteq\{N=n\}$ with $|W_n\cap K|<\delta_n/n$. When $N(p)\ge1$, choose $r(p)$ so that the closed ball about $p$ lies in $W_{N(p)}$. In an $r$-fine partition, the cells whose tags satisfy $N=n$ have disjoint interiors and lie in $W_n\cap K$. Their tags carry values of norm at most $n$, and tags with $N=0$ carry the value $0$. Hence
$$\|S(F,\pi)\|\le\sum_{n\ge1}n\sum_{N(p_J)=n}|J|\le\sum_{n\ge1}n\,|W_n\cap K|<\sum_n\delta_n<\varepsilon .$$
For the consequence apply this to $G-F$ and add. $\square$ [Indicators](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Integrability.lean#L37-L101) · [Null functions](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Integrability.lean#L103-L163)

For $D_2$ in the second example, the level sets $\{N=n\}$ are the segments $\{1/2\}\times[1/n,1/(n-1))$ inside $(0,1]$, with $1/0=\infty$. Vertical strips of width $\delta_n/(2n)$ about $x=1/2$ serve as $W_n$.

*Proof of part 1(b) and of the interchange in part 2.* The restriction identity gives $\lambda^2|_Q=(\lambda|_{[a,b]})\otimes(\lambda|_{[c,d]})$. It therefore suffices to prove $\int F\,d(\mu\otimes\nu)=\int\bigl(\int F(x,y)\,d\nu\bigr)d\mu$ for $\mu,\nu$ as in Lemma 2 and $F$ integrable for $\mu\otimes\nu$. Call this identity $P(F)$. The integrability assertions are Lemma 2(a).

1. *Indicators.* Let $F=1_sc$ with $s$ measurable and $(\mu\otimes\nu)(s)<\infty$. The left side is $(\mu\otimes\nu)(s)\,c$. Each slice is $1_{s_x}c$, with inner integral $\nu(s_x)c$. By Lemma 1(a), $\nu(s_x)<\infty$ for almost every $x$, and the outer integral is $\bigl(\int\nu(s_x)\,d\mu\bigr)c=(\mu\otimes\nu)(s)\,c$. For a rectangle $A\times B$ both sides are $\mu(A)\nu(B)\,c$, width times height.
2. *Disjoint sums.* If $P(F)$ and $P(G)$, then $P(F+G)$ by linearity of the integral and Lemma 2(b). Every integrable simple function is a finite sum of indicators of disjoint sets of finite measure, so $P$ holds for integrable simple functions.
3. *Closure.* Let $F_n\to F$ in $L^1$ with $P(F_n)$ for every $n$. By $\|\int H\|\le\int\|H\|$ and Lemma 2(b),
$$\Bigl\|\int F-\int\Bigl(\int F\,d\nu\Bigr)d\mu\Bigr\|\le\Bigl\|\int(F-F_n)\Bigr\|+\Bigl\|\int\Bigl(\int F_n\,d\nu\Bigr)d\mu-\int\Bigl(\int F\,d\nu\Bigr)d\mu\Bigr\|\le2\int\|F-F_n\|\to0 .$$
4. *Null modifications.* If $P(F)$ and $F'=F$ almost everywhere, then $\int F'=\int F$. By Lemma 1(b) the inner integrals agree for almost every $x$, so $P(F')$. This is also the interchange half of part 2.

Steps 1 and 2 give $P$ for integrable simple functions, and step 4 transfers it to the representatives of their $L^1$ classes. These classes are dense in $L^1$, so step 3 gives $P$ for the representative of every $L^1$ class, and step 4 passes to every integrable function. The exchanged order follows by composing with the coordinate swap, which preserves the product measure. $\square$ [Set-integral Fubini](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/Prod.lean#L507-L512) · [Fubini](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/Prod.lean#L439-L469) · [Restriction](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Measure/Prod.lean#L773-L781) · [Induction principle](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/SimpleFuncDenseLp.lean#L805-L932)

*Proof of part 1(a) and of the comparison in part 2.* The proof passes from simple functions to integrable ones by an explicit approximation.

1. *Representative.* Choose a strongly measurable $g$ equal to $D$ almost everywhere on $Q$. Then $\int_Qg=\int_QD$. By Lemma 3(b) it suffices to show that $g$ has McShane integral $\int_Qg$. This argument also gives the comparison half of part 2.
2. *Simple functions and their approximation.* By Lemma 3(a) and linearity, a simple function $f=\sum_i1_{s_i}c_i$ has McShane integral $\sum_i|s_i\cap K|\,c_i=\int_Kf$ on every closed subrectangle $K$ of $Q$. Choose simple functions $f_n$ with three properties. (i) $f_n(p)\to g(p)$ for every $p$. (ii) $\|f_n(p)-g(p)\|$ is nonincreasing in $n$ for every $p$. (iii) $\int_Q\|f_n-g\|\to0$. The pinned proof takes $f_n(p)$ to be the point nearest to $g(p)$ among $0$ and the first $n$ terms of a fixed dense sequence in the range of $g$ together with $0$. Adding candidates cannot increase the distance, which gives (ii).
3. *Choices.* Fix $\varepsilon>0$. Choose $N_0$ with $\int_Q\|f_{N_0}-g\|\le\varepsilon$. For each $p\in Q$ use (i) to choose an index $n(p)\ge N_0$ with $\|f_{n(p)}(p)-g(p)\|\le\varepsilon$. Choose $\delta_n>0$ with $\sum_n\delta_n<\varepsilon$, and for each $n$ a gauge $r_n$ for $f_n$ at tolerance $\delta_n$. The box integral of $f_n$ over each cell is its Bochner integral by step 2. So by the Henstock–Sacks inequality every $r_n$-fine tagged prepartition $\pi'$ of $Q$ satisfies $\|\sum_{J\in\pi'}(|J|f_n(p_J)-\int_Jf_n)\|\le\delta_n$. Put $r(p)=r_{n(p)}(p)$.
4. *Three estimates.* Let $\pi$ be an $r$-fine tagged partition of $Q$. Since $\int_Qg=\sum_J\int_Jg$,
$$\Bigl\|S(g,\pi)-\int_Qg\Bigr\|\le\sum_J|J|\,\bigl\|g(p_J)-f_{n(p_J)}(p_J)\bigr\|+\Bigl\|\sum_J\Bigl(|J|f_{n(p_J)}(p_J)-\int_Jf_{n(p_J)}\Bigr)\Bigr\|+\sum_J\int_J\bigl\|f_{n(p_J)}-g\bigr\| .$$
The first term is at most $|Q|\varepsilon$. For the second, group the cells by the value of $n(p_J)$. The cells with $n(p_J)=n$ form an $r_n$-fine tagged prepartition, so their contribution has norm at most $\delta_n$. The second term is therefore less than $\varepsilon$. In the third, (ii) and $n(p_J)\ge N_0$ give $\|f_{n(p_J)}-g\|\le\|f_{N_0}-g\|$ pointwise. The third term is therefore at most $\int_Q\|f_{N_0}-g\|\le\varepsilon$. Altogether the error is at most $(|Q|+2)\varepsilon$. $\square$ [Simple functions](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Integrability.lean#L169-L189) · [Approximating sequence](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Function/SimpleFuncDense.lean#L55-L125) · [Approximation step](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Integrability.lean#L195-L301)

The index $n(p)$ is chosen point by point, while the third estimate is an integral over $Q$. Property (ii) connects the two. It lets the fixed index $N_0$ dominate every pointwise choice under the integral.

**Use in the formal proof.** [Lean, read] The Bochner proof of the divergence theorem applies part 1(a) at the GP parameter on each inner box, to the divergence and to the continuous face traces, which are integrable on the compact faces. Uniqueness of GP limits then equates the Bochner integral of the divergence with the sum of the face integrals. Part 1(b) is used once, at line 531 of the planar theorem. Before that line both endpoint pairs are sorted, and the interval integrals over $(a,b]$ are replaced by integrals over $[a,b]$, which differ by null sets. [Inner-box comparison](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/DivergenceTheorem.lean#L104-L136) · [Planar theorem](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Integral/DivergenceTheorem.lean#L494-L536)

**The actual call with $(-iF,F)$.** [Lean, read] The preceding subsection identified what the Cauchy wrapper passes to the planar theorem: the fields $-iF$ and $F$ with their assignments, the divergence $D(p)=-\bigl(iL(e(p))(1)-L(e(p))(i)\bigr)$, and the horizontal endpoints $\Re z,\Re w$ with the vertical endpoints $\Im w,\Im z$. Here we follow that call to part 1(b). In the usual orientation $\Re z<\Re w$ and $\Im z<\Im w$, the first sorting step does nothing. The vertical pair is reversed, so the second sorting step applies the ordered case to $(\Im z,\Im w)$ and negates both sides. In the ordered case, part 1(b) converts the area integral of $D$ over $[\Re z,\Re w]\times[\Im z,\Im w]$ into the iterated integral. Its integrability hypothesis is that of the wrapper, transported along the measure-preserving map $e$ and negated. In the Cauchy–Goursat specialization used by the [weighted annular argument](#weighted-circle-integrals-and-the-rectangle-theorem), the assignment at each point is the complex derivative restricted to real scalars, or the zero map where none exists. Hence $L(q)(i)=iL(q)(1)$, so $D\equiv0$ and both identifications are trivial there. They carry weight for integrands that are only real differentiable, as in the following check. [Wrapper](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean#L180-L222) · [Cauchy–Goursat](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/Complex/CauchyIntegral.lean#L262-L276)

**Hand computation 1 (the actual call).** Take $f(q)=\lvert q\rvert^2$, $z=0$ and $w=1+i$. For $q=x+iy$ and $h=h_1+ih_2$, the real derivative is $L(q)h=2xh_1+2yh_2$. So $L(q)(1)=2x$ and $L(q)(i)=2y$, and the wrapper integrand $iL(q)(1)-L(q)(i)$ is $2ix-2y$. Its iterated integral over the unit square is $i-1$. The boundary side of the wrapper is
$$\int_0^1x^2\,dx-\int_0^1(x^2+1)\,dx+i\int_0^1(1+y^2)\,dy-i\int_0^1y^2\,dy=\tfrac13-\tfrac43+\tfrac{4i}3-\tfrac i3=i-1 .$$
The planar theorem receives $D=2y-2ix$ with vertical limits from $1$ to $0$, and
$$\int_0^1\Bigl(\int_1^0(2y-2ix)\,dy\Bigr)dx=-\int_0^1(1-2ix)\,dx=i-1 .$$
After sorting, part 1(b) is applied to $D$ on $[0,1]^2$, where the area integral is $1-i$. The reversal restores the sign. The value agrees with $\oint f\,dq=2i\iint\partial f/\partial\bar q\,dA$, since $\partial\lvert q\rvert^2/\partial\bar q=q$ and $2i\iint(x+iy)\,dA=i-1$.

**Hand computation 2 (the approximation step for $x^{-1/2}$).** Let $g(x,y)=x^{-1/2}$ on $Q=[0,1]^2$ for $x>0$ and $g(0,y)=0$, as in the first example. For $n\ge0$ put
$$f_n(x,y)=2^{-n}\bigl\lfloor2^n\min(x^{-1/2},2^n)\bigr\rfloor\quad(x>0),\qquad f_n(0,y)=0 .$$

*Rectangle indicators.* Since $x^{-1/2}\ge1$ on $(0,1]$, $f_n$ takes the values $k2^{-n}$ with $2^n\le k\le4^n$. The value $k2^{-n}$ is taken on $I_k\times[0,1]$, where $I_k=(4^n(k+1)^{-2},4^nk^{-2}]$ for $k<4^n$ and $I_{4^n}=(0,4^{-n}]$. These intervals partition $(0,1]$. So $f_n=\sum_kk2^{-n}1_{I_k\times[0,1]}$ is a finite sum of rectangle indicators with disjoint supports.

*Properties (i)–(iii).* Rounding down to a coarser dyadic grid and truncating at a lower level both decrease a value, so $0\le f_n\le f_{n+1}\le g$. Thus $g-f_n$ is nonincreasing in $n$, which is (ii). If $x\ge4^{-n}$ then $0\le g-f_n<2^{-n}$, which gives (i). On $(0,4^{-n}]$ the value is $f_n=2^n$, so
$$\int_Q(g-f_n)=\int_0^{4^{-n}}(x^{-1/2}-2^n)\,dx+\int_{4^{-n}}^1(g-f_n)\,dx<2^{-n}+2^{-n}=2^{1-n} ,$$
which is (iii).

*Interchange.* For each $f_n$ both sides of part 1(b) equal $\sum_kk2^{-n}|I_k|$, width times height. By step 3 of the proof of part 1(b), each side for $g$ differs from the corresponding side for $f_n$ by less than $2^{1-n}$. Hence both sides for $g$ equal $\lim_n\int_Qf_n=2$.

*Henstock–Sacks for $f_n$ by hand.* Write $f_n(x,y)=\phi_n(x)$. The profile $\phi_n$ is a step function on $[0,1]$. It jumps from $\phi_n(0)=0$ to $2^n$ at $0$ and then decreases to $\phi_n(1)=1$ in steps of $2^{-n}$, so its total variation is $2^{n+1}-1$. Let $\rho>0$, and let $J$ be a cell in the closed ball of radius $\rho$ about its tag $p$. For $q\in J$ we have $|q_x-p_x|\le\rho$. Hence $|\phi_n(p_x)-\phi_n(q_x)|$ is at most the total size of the jumps at points $t$ with $|t-q_x|\le\rho$. Sum over the cells of a prepartition fine for the constant gauge $\rho$. The cells have disjoint interiors, and each jump point $t$ is counted only at points $q$ of the strip $|q_x-t|\le\rho$, of area at most $2\rho$. Therefore
$$\Bigl|\sum_J\Bigl(|J|f_n(p_J)-\int_Jf_n\Bigr)\Bigr|\le\sum_J\int_J|\phi_n(p_{J,x})-\phi_n(q_x)|\,dq\le2\rho\,(2^{n+1}-1)<2^{n+2}\rho .$$
With $\rho=r_n=\delta_n2^{-n-2}$ the bound is less than $\delta_n$.

*Comparison.* Take $\varepsilon=2^{-m}$ with $m\ge1$, and $N_0=m+1$, so that $\int_Q(g-f_{N_0})<\varepsilon$. For $p=(x,y)$ with $x>0$ let $n(p)=\max\bigl(N_0,\lceil\tfrac12\log_2(1/x)\rceil\bigr)$, and let $n(p)=N_0$ when $x=0$. In the first case $4^{-n(p)}\le x$, so $|f_{n(p)}(p)-g(p)|<2^{-N_0}=\varepsilon/2$. In the second case the error is $0$. The index is unbounded near the edge $x=0$: it equals $k$ on $[4^{-k},4^{1-k})$ once $k>N_0$. Take $\delta_n=\varepsilon2^{-n-1}$ and $r_n$ as above. The gauge of the proof is $r(p)=r_{n(p)}=\varepsilon2^{-2n(p)-3}$, which lies between $\varepsilon x/32$ and $\varepsilon x/8$ near the edge. For an $r$-fine partition of $Q$ the three estimates are at most $\varepsilon/2$, at most $\sum_{n\ge N_0}\delta_n=\varepsilon2^{-N_0}$, and less than $\varepsilon$. So every $r$-fine tagged sum of $g$ lies within $2\varepsilon$ of $2$.

**Hand computation 3 (iterated integrals that cannot be exchanged).** Let
$$\phi(x,y)=\frac{x^2-y^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}\quad\text{on }[0,1]^2\setminus\{(0,0)\},\qquad\phi(0,0)=0 .$$
For $0<x\le1$, $\partial_y\bigl(y/(x^2+y^2)\bigr)=\phi(x,y)$, and the slice is continuous on $[0,1]$. So $\int_0^1\phi(x,y)\,dy=1/(1+x^2)$. The slice at $x=0$ is $-y^{-2}$, which is not integrable, but this is a single null slice. Hence
$$\int_0^1\Bigl(\int_0^1\phi(x,y)\,dy\Bigr)dx=\int_0^1\frac{dx}{1+x^2}=\frac\pi4 .$$
For $0<y\le1$, $\partial_x\bigl(-x/(x^2+y^2)\bigr)=\phi(x,y)$. So $\int_0^1\phi(x,y)\,dx=-1/(1+y^2)$, and the other iterated integral is $-\pi/4$. Both inner-integral functions are bounded and continuous on $(0,1]$, so both iterated integrals are integrals of integrable functions. Part 1(b) would force them to agree, so $\phi$ is not integrable on $[0,1]^2$. Directly, $\phi>0$ on $T=\{0<y<x\le1\}$, and Tonelli's theorem gives
$$\int_T\phi=\int_0^1\Bigl[\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}\Bigr]_{y=0}^{y=x}dx=\int_0^1\frac{dx}{2x}=\infty .$$
The two orders are fluxes. The fields $(0,V)$ with $V=y/(x^2+y^2)$ and $(U,0)$ with $U=-x/(x^2+y^2)$ both have divergence $\phi$ in the open square. Their face differences are the two inner integrals above, and their fluxes are $\pi/4$ and $-\pi/4$. Neither field is continuous at the corner, since $V(0,y)=1/y$ and $U(x,0)=-1/x$. The rectangular divergence theorem therefore applies to neither. Their difference $(x,y)/(x^2+y^2)$ is divergence free away from the origin, and its outward flux through $\partial Q$ is $\pi/2$. The same value is the outward flux from every quarter disc about the corner: the field is $1/\rho$ times the outward normal on the arc of radius $\rho$ and tangent to the two straight sides. The example appears in the [counterexample section of the Fubini article](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Fubini%27s_theorem&oldid=1383075598#Failure_of_Fubini%27s_theorem_for_non-integrable_functions) and on [p. 15 of Shkoller's notes](https://www.math.ucdavis.edu/~hunter/m218a_09/Lp_and_Sobolev_notes.pdf#page=15).

**Remarks.**

1. *The gauge limit needs no integrability.* Let $H(t)=t^2\sin(t^{-2})$ for $t\ne0$ and $H(0)=0$, and take $U(x,y)=H(x)$ and $V=0$ on $Q=[0,1]^2$. The field is continuous on $Q$ and differentiable at every point of the plane, with divergence $H'(x)$. The computation in the preceding subsection shows $\int_0^1|H'|=\infty$, so by Tonelli's theorem the divergence is not Bochner integrable on $Q$. The face-difference estimates of the preceding subsection still apply, with no exceptional points. Since the derivative exists at every point of $Q$, including its edges, they can be run on $Q$ itself. The GP sums of $H'(x)$ therefore converge to the flux $\int_0^1[H(1)-H(0)]\,dy=\sin1$. Part 1 does not apply, and the Bochner statement of the divergence theorem says nothing here. Integrability is the price of the two identifications, not of the face-difference identity.
2. *Formal conventions.* The pinned Fubini proof and the inner-box divergence lemma first dispose of incomplete targets, where both sides are $0$ by convention, and the comparison assumes a complete target; $\mathbb C$ is complete. Non-integrable slices receive the integral $0$, which does not affect almost-everywhere statements. Cells of a formal box partition are half-open, and closed cells differ from them by null sets. The comparison is proved for every parameter set whose gauges may vary with the tag, and the divergence proof uses it at the GP parameter.
3. *Reading level and sources.* The bodies in the table below are [Lean, read]. The inputs (N1)–(N5) remain [Lean], and Henstock–Sacks and its four named box-integral inputs are [Lean, read]. See the [epsilon–gauge equivalence](#from-the-box-filter-to-epsilon-and-gauges), [Cousin's lemma](#cousins-lemma-fine-tagged-partitions-of-a-box), [exact half-open complements](#exact-complements-by-splitting-half-open-boxes) and the [subbox Cauchy argument](#subbox-filters-and-the-cauchy-net-of-tagged-sums). The pinned comparison file bases its argument on [Gordon's monograph](https://bookstore.ams.org/GSM/4), whose Chapter 10 treats the McShane integral; we have not compared the two texts.

#### The Henstock–Sacks inequality: tagged sums over part of a rectangle

**Motivation.** The comparison proof of the preceding subsection controls the second of its three estimates by the Henstock–Sacks inequality, which was imported there as part of input (N6). The situation is this. A gauge $r_n$ is chosen for the simple function $f_n$ so that every $r_n$-fine tagged *partition* of $Q$ has tagged sum within $\delta_n$ of $\int_Qf_n$. The cells of the actual partition $\pi$ whose tags satisfy $n(p_J)=n$ form only an $r_n$-fine tagged *prepartition*: they cover part of $Q$, and the rest of $Q$ is covered by cells belonging to other indices. The proof needs the tagged sum over this part to be within $\delta_n$ of the sum of the integrals of $f_n$ over its cells. Nothing in the definition of the integral speaks about parts of $Q$. The inequality says that the definition nevertheless controls them, with the same tolerance. This subsection proves it from the pinned proof body, follows the actual argument through a three-valued step function, and shows by a counterexample why the third estimate of the comparison proof cannot be run with $L^1$ convergence alone.

**Example.** Let $f=1_{\{x\le1/2\}}+2\cdot1_{\{y\le1/2\}}$ on $Q=[0,1]^2$, with values $0,1,2,3$ on the four quarters and $\int_Qf=\tfrac12+1=\tfrac32$. Let $\pi$ be a tagged partition of $Q$ in which every cell has diameter at most $\rho$ in the maximum norm. A cell $J$ with tag $p$ contributes the error $|J|f(p)-\int_Jf=\int_J(f(p)-f(q))\,dq$. The integrand vanishes unless the vertical line $x=1/2$ separates $p_x$ from $q_x$, or the horizontal line $y=1/2$ separates $p_y$ from $q_y$. In the first case $|q_x-1/2|\le|q_x-p_x|\le\rho$, and the jump is $1$. In the second case $|q_y-1/2|\le\rho$, and the jump is $2$. The cells have disjoint interiors, so summing over $\pi$ gives
$$\Bigl|S(f,\pi)-\int_Qf\Bigr|\le\sum_J\int_J|f(p_J)-f(q)|\,dq\le1\cdot|\{|x-\tfrac12|\le\rho\}\cap Q|+2\cdot|\{|y-\tfrac12|\le\rho\}\cap Q|\le2\rho+4\rho=6\rho .$$
Thus the constant gauge $r=\varepsilon/6$ is a valid gauge for $f$ at tolerance $\varepsilon$. The same computation applies verbatim to a prepartition, since it never used that the cells cover $Q$. For a general integrable function no such direct computation is available, and the inequality below recovers the prepartition statement from the partition statement alone. Hand computation 4 runs the general proof on this $f$.

**Definitions.** We keep the notation of the preceding subsection: $K$ is a closed rectangle in $\mathbb R^2$ of positive area, cells are closed subrectangles with disjoint interiors, $S(F,\pi)=\sum_{J\in\pi}|J|F(p_J)$, and a gauge is a function $r:K\to(0,\infty)$. In the pinned library a box is a product $\prod_i(l_i,u_i]$ of half-open intervals with $l_i<u_i$ in every coordinate. Boxes are therefore never degenerate, the cells of a prepartition are pairwise disjoint as sets, and the closed hull of a cell is its closure. Fineness and the Henstock condition are tested on the closed hull: $\pi$ is $r$-fine if the closed hull of each cell $J$ lies in the closed ball of radius $r(p_J)$ about $p_J$, and a tag is admissible if it lies in the closed hull of $K$. The *distortion* of a box is the ratio of its longest side to its shortest side. The distortion of a prepartition is the maximum over its cells, and is $0$ for the empty prepartition.

A *parameter set* $\ell$ consists of three Boolean flags. We say that a tagged prepartition $\pi$ of $K$ is *$(c,r)$-admissible* for $\ell$, with $c\ge0$ a real number and $r$ a gauge, if

- (A1) $\pi$ is $r$-fine;
- (A2) if the Henstock flag is set, each tag lies in the closed hull of its own cell;
- (A3) if the distortion flag is set, every cell of $\pi$ has distortion at most $c$;
- (A4) if the distortion flag is set, there is a prepartition of $K$ with cells of distortion at most $c$ whose union is exactly $K\setminus\bigcup\pi$.

The Riemann flag, if set, requires $r$ to be constant; the comparison proof uses parameter sets without it, and nothing below depends on it. McShane integration is the parameter set with no flag set, so admissibility is fineness alone and $c$ plays no role. The GP integration of the divergence subsection has the Henstock and distortion flags set. Condition (A4) holds automatically for partitions, with the empty prepartition. Its purpose is the common-completion step in Lemma 1.

Let $F$ be integrable on $K$ for $\ell$ with integral $\int_KF$. For $\varepsilon>0$ and $c\ge0$, a *convergence gauge* $r_F(\varepsilon,c)$ is a gauge such that every $(c,r_F(\varepsilon,c))$-admissible tagged partition $\pi$ of $K$ satisfies $\|S(F,\pi)-\int_KF\|\le\varepsilon$. Such gauges exist by the definition of the integral as a limit along the filter generated by the admissible partitions; the pinned library fixes one choice for each $(\varepsilon,c)$ and calls it `convergenceR`. Two facts are used about this choice and nothing else. First, decreasing a gauge pointwise preserves admissibility of any given prepartition. Second, admissibility of a prepartition is tested only at its tags, so two gauges that agree at the tags of $\pi$ admit $\pi$ together.

**Named inputs.** Beyond (N1)–(N5) of the preceding subsection, the following parts of (N6) remain [Lean] and are used as stated.

- (N6a) The filter description of the integral: $F$ has integral $y$ if and only if for every $\varepsilon>0$ there are gauges $r_c$, one for each $c\ge0$, such that every $(c,r_c)$-admissible tagged partition $\pi$ has $\|S(F,\pi)-y\|\le\varepsilon$. Limits along this filter are unique, and the filter is nontrivial. [Filter description](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L200-L205) See the [epsilon–gauge equivalence](#from-the-box-filter-to-epsilon-and-gauges).
- (N6b) Cousin's lemma in a box: for every box $J$ and gauge $r$ there is an $r$-fine tagged partition of $J$ whose tags lie in their own closed cells and whose cells all have the distortion of $J$. Its proof is read in [the next subsection](#cousins-lemma-fine-tagged-partitions-of-a-box). [Cousin](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/SubboxInduction.lean#L94-L131)
- (N6c) The complement of the union of a prepartition of $K$ is the union of a prepartition of $K$. The pinned proof splits $K$ along finitely many hyperplanes through the faces of the given cells and keeps the pieces not contained in the union. [Complement](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Split.lean#L297-L350) See the [exact half-open complements](#exact-complements-by-splitting-half-open-boxes).
- (N6d) Two filter facts used for integrability on subboxes: a Cauchy net in a complete space converges, and tagged partitions of a subbox $J$ of $K$, viewed as prepartitions of $K$, are admissible for $K$ when they are admissible for $J$, with the ambient bound raised to the maximum of $c$ and the distortion of a chosen prepartition covering $K\setminus J$. [Embedding](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Filter.lean#L466-L480) See the [subbox Cauchy argument](#subbox-filters-and-the-cauchy-net-of-tagged-sums).

**Lemma 1 (two prepartitions with a common completion).** [Lean, read] Let $F$ be integrable on $K$, and let $\pi_1,\pi_2$ be tagged prepartitions of $K$ with the same union, $\pi_1$ being $(c_1,r_F(\varepsilon_1,c_1))$-admissible and $\pi_2$ being $(c_2,r_F(\varepsilon_2,c_2))$-admissible. Then
$$\|S(F,\pi_1)-S(F,\pi_2)\|\le\varepsilon_1+\varepsilon_2 .$$

*Proof.* Write $r_i=r_F(\varepsilon_i,c_i)$ and $r=\min(r_1,r_2)$, a gauge. By (N6c) there is a prepartition $\pi^c$ of $K$ with union $K\setminus\bigcup\pi_1$, which equals $K\setminus\bigcup\pi_2$. If the distortion flag is set, choose $\pi^c$ instead from (A4) of whichever of $\pi_1,\pi_2$ has the smaller bound $c_i$; then its cells have distortion at most $\min(c_1,c_2)$. Apply (N6b) to each cell of $\pi^c$ with the gauge $r$ and collect the resulting tagged partitions into one tagged prepartition $\pi^r$ of $K$. It is $r$-fine, its tags lie in their own cells, its union is $K\setminus\bigcup\pi_1$, and its distortion equals that of $\pi^c$.

Now $\pi_1\sqcup\pi^r$ is a tagged partition of $K$: the two unions are complementary in $K$, and tags are taken from the respective parts. It is $(c_1,r_1)$-admissible. Indeed (A1) holds because $\pi_1$ is $r_1$-fine and $\pi^r$ is $r$-fine with $r\le r_1$; (A2) holds for the cells of $\pi_1$ by assumption and for the cells of $\pi^r$ by construction; (A3) holds because the distortion of a disjoint union is the maximum of the two distortions, both at most $c_1$; (A4) holds for every partition. Hence $\|S(F,\pi_1\sqcup\pi^r)-\int_KF\|\le\varepsilon_1$ by the defining property of $r_1$. In the same way $\pi_2\sqcup\pi^r$ is a $(c_2,r_2)$-admissible tagged partition of $K$ and $\|S(F,\pi_2\sqcup\pi^r)-\int_KF\|\le\varepsilon_2$. Tagged sums are additive over disjoint unions, $S(F,\pi_i\sqcup\pi^r)=S(F,\pi_i)+S(F,\pi^r)$, so the common term $S(F,\pi^r)$ cancels:
$$\|S(F,\pi_1)-S(F,\pi_2)\|=\|S(F,\pi_1\sqcup\pi^r)-S(F,\pi_2\sqcup\pi^r)\|\le\varepsilon_1+\varepsilon_2 .\qquad\square$$
[Common completion](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L450-L468) · [Admissibility of the completion](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Filter.lean#L343-L364) · [Construction of the completion](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/SubboxInduction.lean#L148-L234)

The point of the lemma is that nothing is known about $S(F,\pi^r)$ by itself. It is only known to be the same number in the two partition sums. The two prepartitions may have different distortion bounds and different gauges, and the lemma does not require the gauges to be comparable, because the completion is fine for the smaller one.

**Lemma 2 (integrability on subboxes).** [Lean, read], using (N6a) and (N6d). Let the target space be complete, let $F$ be integrable on $K$, and let $J\subseteq K$ be a box. Then $F$ is integrable on $J$, and the tagged sums over admissible prepartitions of $K$ with union exactly $J$ converge to $\int_JF$.

*Proof.* Fix the prepartition $\pi_0$ of $K$ with the single cell $J$. By Lemma 1 with $\varepsilon_1=\varepsilon_2=\varepsilon/2$, any two admissible prepartitions of $K$ that both have union $J$ and are fine for the gauges $r_F(\varepsilon/2,\cdot)$ have tagged sums within $\varepsilon$ of each other. So the tagged sums form a Cauchy net along the filter of admissible prepartitions with union $J$, and by completeness they converge to some $y$. A tagged partition of $J$ is such a prepartition of $K$, and the admissibility conditions transfer at the shifted level by (N6d). Hence $F$ has integral $y$ on $J$ in the sense of (N6a), and $y=\int_JF$. $\square$ [Cauchy net and subboxes](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L473-L516) The [subbox Cauchy argument](#subbox-filters-and-the-cauchy-net-of-tagged-sums) gives the filter proof.

**Theorem (Henstock–Sacks inequality).** [Lean, read], conditional on (N6a)–(N6d). Let the target space be complete, let $F$ be integrable on $K$ for a parameter set $\ell$, and let $\varepsilon>0$ and $c\ge0$. Let $\pi$ be a $(c,r_F(\varepsilon,c))$-admissible tagged prepartition of $K$, and let $\pi_0$ be any prepartition of $K$ with $\bigcup\pi_0=\bigcup\pi$. Then
$$\Bigl\|S(F,\pi)-\sum_{J\in\pi_0}\int_JF\Bigr\|\le\varepsilon .$$
In particular, taking $\pi_0$ to be the cells of $\pi$ without their tags,
$$\Bigl\|\sum_{J\in\pi}\Bigl(|J|F(p_J)-\int_JF\Bigr)\Bigr\|\le\varepsilon .$$

*Proof.* It suffices to prove the bound $\varepsilon+\delta$ for every $\delta>0$. Let $m$ be the number of cells of $\pi_0$, put $\delta'=\delta/(m+1)$, and let $C$ be the larger of the distortions of $\pi_0$ and of a prepartition $\pi_0^c$ of $K$ with union $K\setminus\bigcup\pi_0$ from (N6c). The constant $C$ is needed only when the distortion flag is set; in the McShane case it can be ignored.

*Step 1: a good partition of each cell of $\pi_0$.* Let $J\in\pi_0$. By Lemma 2, $F$ is integrable on $J$. Let $\rho_J=\min\bigl(r_F(\delta',C),\,r_{F|_J}(\delta',C)\bigr)$, the pointwise minimum of a convergence gauge of $F$ on $K$ and one of $F$ on $J$, both at tolerance $\delta'$ and distortion bound $C$. The distortion of $J$ is at most $C$, so by (N6b) there is a $(C,\rho_J)$-admissible tagged partition $\pi_J$ of $J$. Since $\rho_J\le r_{F|_J}(\delta',C)$, the defining property of the latter gauge gives
$$\Bigl\|S(F,\pi_J)-\int_JF\Bigr\|\le\delta' .$$
Since also $\rho_J\le r_F(\delta',C)$, the partition $\pi_J$ is $(C,r_F(\delta',C))$-admissible as a prepartition of $J$.

*Step 2: glue.* Let $\Pi=\bigsqcup_{J\in\pi_0}\pi_J$, the tagged prepartition of $K$ whose cells are all cells of all $\pi_J$, each with its tag from $\pi_J$. Its union is $\bigcup\pi_0=\bigcup\pi$. It is $(C,r_F(\delta',C))$-admissible for $K$: (A1) and (A2) are inherited cell by cell, (A3) holds because the distortion of $\Pi$ is the maximum of the distortions of the $\pi_J$, each at most $C$, and (A4) holds with $\pi_0^c$, whose union is $K\setminus\bigcup\Pi$ and whose distortion is at most $C$. By additivity of tagged sums over the cells of $\pi_0$,
$$S(F,\Pi)=\sum_{J\in\pi_0}S(F,\pi_J).$$

*Step 3: compare.* Apply Lemma 1 to $\pi$, which is $(c,r_F(\varepsilon,c))$-admissible, and to $\Pi$, which is $(C,r_F(\delta',C))$-admissible, with the same union:
$$\|S(F,\pi)-S(F,\Pi)\|\le\varepsilon+\delta' .$$
By Step 1 and the triangle inequality for sums, $\|\sum_J a_J-\sum_Jb_J\|\le\sum_J\|a_J-b_J\|$,
$$\Bigl\|S(F,\Pi)-\sum_{J\in\pi_0}\int_JF\Bigr\|=\Bigl\|\sum_{J\in\pi_0}\Bigl(S(F,\pi_J)-\int_JF\Bigr)\Bigr\|\le m\delta' .$$
Adding the two displays,
$$\Bigl\|S(F,\pi)-\sum_{J\in\pi_0}\int_JF\Bigr\|\le\varepsilon+\delta'+m\delta'=\varepsilon+(m+1)\frac{\delta}{m+1}=\varepsilon+\delta .$$
Since $\delta>0$ was arbitrary, the bound $\varepsilon$ follows. The particular case is $\pi_0=\pi$ without tags, whose union is $\bigcup\pi$ by definition. $\square$ [Henstock–Sacks with $\pi_0$](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L533-L576) · [Particular case](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L591-L594) · [Gluing preserves admissibility](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Filter.lean#L393-L403) · [Additivity of tagged sums](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Basic.lean#L93-L97)

The constant $m+1$ rather than $m$ pays for the one use of Lemma 1 in Step 3; the $m$ cells of $\pi_0$ each pay $\delta'$ in Step 1. The two gauges in $\rho_J$ have different jobs. The gauge of $F|_J$ makes the sum over $\pi_J$ close to $\int_JF$. The gauge of $F$ on $K$ makes the glued prepartition $\Pi$ admissible for $K$ at tolerance $\delta'$, which is what Lemma 1 needs.

**Return to the comparison proof.** [Lean, read] In step 4 of the proof of part 1(a) of the preceding subsection, let $\pi$ be an $r$-fine tagged partition of $Q$ with $r(p)=r_{n(p)}(p)$, where $r_n=r_{f_n}(\delta_n,c)$ is a convergence gauge of the simple function $f_n$ at tolerance $\delta_n$. For each $n$ let $\pi_n$ be the tagged prepartition formed by the cells $J\in\pi$ with $n(p_J)=n$, tags included. The sums in the second estimate are regrouped as $\sum_J=\sum_n\sum_{J\in\pi_n}$, which is a finite sum over the values taken by $n(p_J)$. Three facts make the theorem applicable to $\pi_n$ and $f_n$.

1. *Admissibility passes to subfamilies.* (A1)–(A3) are conditions on individual cells. For (A4), the complement of $\bigcup\pi_n$ is the union of the complement of $\bigcup\pi$ and of the cells of $\pi$ not in $\pi_n$, so a prepartition for it is the disjoint union of the one supplied by (A4) for $\pi$ and of the removed cells, whose distortions are at most $c$ by (A3). [Subfamilies](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Filter.lean#L369-L391)
2. *The gauge is tested only at tags.* On the tags of $\pi_n$ we have $n(p_J)=n$ and therefore $r(p_J)=r_n(p_J)$. Since admissibility is tested at tags only, $\pi_n$ is $(c,r_n)$-admissible. This is where the choice of $n(p)$ as a function of the point, not of the cell, enters: $r$ must be a gauge for the partition to be called $r$-fine at all. [Tag test](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Partition/Filter.lean#L330-L341)
3. *Box integrals of $f_n$ are Bochner integrals.* By step 2 of that proof, $\int_Jf_n$ in the theorem is $\int_Jf_n\,d\lambda^2$ for every cell.

The theorem then gives $\|\sum_{J\in\pi_n}(|J|f_n(p_J)-\int_Jf_n)\|\le\delta_n$ for each $n$, and the second estimate is at most $\sum_n\delta_n<\varepsilon$, where the sum over the finitely many occurring indices is bounded by the full series. The parameter set there is GP, so the distortion clauses are active, and fact 1 is used in full. [Grouped call](https://github.com/leanprover-community/mathlib4/blob/0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474/Mathlib/Analysis/BoxIntegral/Integrability.lean#L261-L285)

**Hand computation 4 (the proof on a step function).** Let $f$ and $Q$ be as in the example, with $\int_Qf=3/2$, and use McShane admissibility, so that only fineness matters. The example gives the convergence gauge $r_f(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon/6$ for every tolerance, and the same bound $6\rho$ on every subrectangle, so $r_{f|_J}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon/6$ as well.

*The prepartition.* Let $\pi$ consist of the single cell $J_1=[0.4,0.6]^2$ with tag $p=(0.45,0.55)$. The farthest corner of $J_1$ from $p$ is at distance $0.15$ in the maximum norm, so $\pi$ is $\rho$-fine exactly for $\rho\ge0.15$. Take $\rho=0.15$ and $\varepsilon=6\rho=0.9$. Here $f(p)=1$, since $0.45\le1/2<0.55$, so $S(f,\pi)=0.04\cdot1=0.04$, while
$$\int_{J_1}f=1\cdot|J_1\cap\{x\le\tfrac12\}|+2\cdot|J_1\cap\{y\le\tfrac12\}|=0.1\cdot0.2+2\cdot0.2\cdot0.1=0.06 .$$
The quantity to be bounded is $|0.04-0.06|=0.02$, and the theorem promises at most $0.9$.

*Step 1.* Here $\pi_0=\{J_1\}$, so $m=1$ and $\delta'=\delta/2$. Take $\delta=0.6$, so $\delta'=0.3$ and $\rho_{J_1}=\min(0.3/6,0.3/6)=0.05$. Let $\pi_{J_1}$ be the partition of $J_1$ into the four squares of side $0.1$, each tagged at its centre: $(0.45,0.45)$, $(0.55,0.45)$, $(0.45,0.55)$, $(0.55,0.55)$, with values $3,2,1,0$. It is $0.05$-fine, and
$$S(f,\pi_{J_1})=0.01\cdot(3+2+1+0)=0.06=\int_{J_1}f ,$$
so the Step 1 error is $0\le\delta'$. Since the four squares lie on one side of each line, the tagged sum is exact.

*Step 2.* Here $\Pi=\pi_{J_1}$, with union $J_1$.

*Step 3, the completion of Lemma 1.* The complement $Q\setminus J_1$ is the union of the four rectangles $[0,0.4]\times[0,1]$, $[0.6,1]\times[0,1]$, $[0.4,0.6]\times[0,0.4]$ and $[0.4,0.6]\times[0.6,1]$. Cut them into squares of side $0.05$ with tags at the centres; all side lengths are multiples of $0.05$, so this is possible, and it gives a $0.05$-fine tagged prepartition $\pi^r$ with union $Q\setminus J_1$. Both lines $x=1/2$ and $y=1/2$ are grid lines, so every small square lies on one side of each, and
$$S(f,\pi^r)=\int_{Q\setminus J_1}f=\tfrac32-0.06=1.44 .$$
Now $\pi\sqcup\pi^r$ is a $0.15$-fine partition of $Q$, and $S(f,\pi\sqcup\pi^r)=0.04+1.44=1.48$, within $\varepsilon=0.9$ of $3/2$ as the gauge promises; the actual error is $0.02$. Also $\Pi\sqcup\pi^r$ is a $0.05$-fine partition of $Q$, and $S(f,\Pi\sqcup\pi^r)=0.06+1.44=1.50$, within $\delta'=0.3$ of $3/2$; the actual error is $0$. Subtracting, $|S(f,\pi)-S(f,\Pi)|=|1.48-1.50|=0.02\le\varepsilon+\delta'=1.2$, and the term $1.44$ cancelled without being estimated.

*Total.* The proof's bound is $\varepsilon+\delta'+m\delta'=0.9+0.3+0.3=1.5=\varepsilon+\delta$, and letting $\delta\to0$ gives $0.9$. The actual quantity is $0.02$. The gap is the price of using only the defining property of the gauge, not the structure of $f$; the example at the start of the subsection, which uses that structure, gives $0.02\le6\rho=0.9$ directly as well, and the two bounds agree because for this $f$ the direct argument already applies to prepartitions.

**Hand computation 5 (why $L^1$ convergence alone is not enough).** The third estimate in the comparison proof is
$$\sum_J\int_J\|f_{n(p_J)}-g\|\le\int_Q\|f_{N_0}-g\|\le\varepsilon ,$$
and its first inequality rests on property (ii), the pointwise monotonicity $\|f_n(q)-g(q)\|\le\|f_{N_0}(q)-g(q)\|$ for $n\ge N_0$ and every $q$. One might hope to replace (ii) by (iii), the $L^1$ convergence $\int_Q\|f_n-g\|\to0$, and argue that each tag may choose its own index. This fails, even for indices chosen as a function of the point and even without using fineness, which the first inequality above does not use.

Let $g=0$ on $Q=[0,1]^2$ and, for $n\ge1$,
$$f_n(x,y)=n\cdot1_{(1/(n+1),\,1/n]}(x) .$$
Each $f_n$ is a rectangle indicator times a constant, so a simple function. Property (i) holds: a point with $x=0$ has $f_n=0$ for all $n$, and a point with $x\in(0,1]$ lies in exactly one interval $(1/(k+1),1/k]$, so $f_n(x,y)=0$ for all $n\ne k$. Property (iii) holds: $\int_Q\|f_n-g\|=n\bigl(\tfrac1n-\tfrac1{n+1}\bigr)=\tfrac1{n+1}\to0$. Property (ii) fails: $\|f_n(q)-g(q)\|$ is $0$ for $n<k$, then $k$, then $0$ again. Now fix $N_0\ge2$, so that $\int_Q\|f_{N_0}-g\|=1/(N_0+1)$, fix $M>N_0$, and define the index function $n(p)=k$ when $p=(1/(k+1),0)$ with $N_0\le k\le M$, and $n(p)=N_0$ otherwise. Then $n(p)\ge N_0$ everywhere, and $\|f_{n(p)}(p)-g(p)\|=0$ at every $p$ that will be used as a tag below, because $f_k$ vanishes at $x=1/(k+1)$, the open end of its interval. Take the tagged partition of $Q$ with the vertical strips $J_k=[\tfrac1{k+1},\tfrac1k]\times[0,1]$ for $N_0\le k\le M$, tagged at $(1/(k+1),0)$, and the two remaining strips $[0,\tfrac1{M+1}]\times[0,1]$ and $[\tfrac1{N_0},1]\times[0,1]$, tagged at $(0,0)$ and $(1,1)$, where $f_{N_0}$ vanishes because $N_0\ge2$. Its third estimate is
$$\sum_J\int_J\|f_{n(p_J)}-g\|\ \ge\ \sum_{k=N_0}^M\int_{J_k}f_k=\sum_{k=N_0}^M\frac1{k+1}\ >\ \ln\frac{M+2}{N_0+1},$$
which is unbounded in $M$, while the claimed bound is $1/(N_0+1)$. What (ii) supplies, and (iii) does not, is an integrable envelope: under (ii), $\|f_{n(q)}(q)-g(q)\|\le\|f_{N_0}(q)-g(q)\|$ for every index function $n\ge N_0$, and the right side is integrable. Here $\sup_{n\ge N_0}f_n=\sum_{k\ge N_0}k\,1_{(1/(k+1),1/k]}$ has integral $\sum_{k\ge N_0}1/(k+1)=\infty$, so no such envelope exists. A choice of index depending on the cell rather than on the tag would be worse still: then $p\mapsto r_{n}(p)$ would not be a gauge, the phrase "$r$-fine" would depend on the partition it is meant to test, and the grouping of fact 2 above would have nothing to group by.

**Remarks.**

1. *Tags, boundaries and degenerate cells.* A McShane tag may lie anywhere in the closed rectangle $K$, including on $\partial K$ and outside its own cell; a Henstock tag lies in the closed hull of its cell. In the pinned library both are membership statements in closed sets, so tags on the common face of two cells are admissible for both. Degenerate rectangles do not occur as cells, and the half-open convention makes the cells of a prepartition disjoint as sets. The completion steps of Lemma 1 and of the theorem produce cells inside the given boxes only, so no cell ever leaves $K$.
2. *Where completeness is used.* Lemma 1 does not use completeness of the target space. The theorem uses it once, through Lemma 2, to know that $F$ is integrable on each cell of $\pi_0$ and that $\int_JF$ exists. For the simple functions $f_n$ of the comparison proof, integrability on every cell is known directly from Lemma 3(a) of the preceding subsection and linearity, so the theorem could be run for them without Lemma 2; the pinned body nevertheless applies the general statement.
3. *Two forms of the inequality.* The classical Saks–Henstock lemma for real-valued functions on an interval is often stated with the sum of absolute values, $\sum_{J\in\pi}\bigl||J|F(p_J)-\int_JF\bigr|\le2\varepsilon$, which follows from the form proved here by applying it separately to the cells with positive and with negative error. The pinned library proves only the norm-of-the-sum form, and the comparison proof uses only that form. The lemma appears in the [Encyclopedia of Mathematics entry on the Kurzweil–Henstock integral](https://encyclopediaofmath.org/wiki/Kurzweil-Henstock_integral), which gives both forms on an interval, and in Fremlin's [vector-valued treatment](https://www1.essex.ac.uk/maths/people/Fremlin/n10805.pdf), §1D, whose proof completes a fine prepartition to a fine partition exactly as in Lemma 1. The pinned file states that its development is based on Gordon's monograph, which we have not compared with it.
4. *Reading level.* The bodies in the table below and the four named inputs (N6a)–(N6d) are [Lean, read] at the scope of the linked subsections. The rectangle integration supply still cites inputs (N1)–(N5) [Lean] at this point of the argument.
