#### Where interval lengths in real Lebesgue measure come from

**Motivation.** Every one-dimensional integral in this chapter ultimately spends three properties of real Lebesgue measure $\lambda$ (`volume : Measure ℝ`): a bounded interval with ordered endpoints $a \le b$ has finite measure $b - a$, singletons carry mass zero so open, half-open and closed intervals have the same integral, and every real point has an open neighborhood of finite measure so local filter estimates apply. In Lemma 3.3 (`windowHyp_of_rpow`) below, passing from the closed window $[Y, Y^{1+\delta}]$ to the oriented integral $\int_Y^{Y^{1+\delta}}$ rewrites $\int_{[Y, Y^{1+\delta}]}$ to $\int_{(Y, Y^{1+\delta}]}$ by deleting the single left endpoint $Y$. In the fundamental theorem of calculus for oriented interval integrals—used on horizontal wedge slices to build holomorphic primitives on disks and on circle arcs in the archive's winding-number calculation—local finiteness supplies an integrable neighborhood at the moving endpoint, while the constant-integral formula $\int_u^v c\,dx = (v - u)\cdot c$ and the norm bound $\bigl\lVert\int_u^v f(x)\,dx\bigr\rVert \le C\lvert v - u\rvert$ turn measure-theoretic small-set errors into derivatives with respect to real length.

Where do those interval lengths actually come from in the pinned library, and why can an oriented integral be negative when every set has nonnegative measure? Perhaps surprisingly, Mathlib does **not** define `(volume : Measure ℝ)` directly by interval differences: $\mathbb R$ inherits `volume` as a one-dimensional real inner product space, namely the additive Haar measure normalized on the unit parallelepiped of `stdOrthonormalBasis ℝ ℝ`. Separately, Mathlib associates a Borel Stieltjes measure to any monotone right-continuous function $f : R \to \mathbb R$ by feeding the single-interval envelope cost into Carathéodory's outer-measure construction, evaluates it on $(a, b]$ by two right-continuity shifts and Heine–Borel compactness, and bridges the two constructions at `Real.volume_eq_stieltjes_id` by applying Haar uniqueness to the identity function $x \mapsto x$. Opening this chain discharges the real-volume boundary left open by the local filter-integrability and endpoint-FTC packages without assuming any interval formula in advance.

**Hand calculation 1 (oriented intervals, half-open partitions and integral signs).** [Expository] Give $E = \mathbb R^2$ the norm $\lVert(u, v)\rVert = \lvert u\rvert + \lvert v\rvert$, equip $(\mathbb R, \mathcal B(\mathbb R))$ with Lebesgue measure $\lambda$, and take breakpoints $-1 < \frac{1}{2} < 2$. Define $f : \mathbb R \to \mathbb R^2$ by
$$
f(x) = \begin{cases}
(5, 5), & x = -1,\\
(2, -1), & x \in (-1, \tfrac{1}{2}),\\
(10, -7), & x = \tfrac{1}{2},\\
(-4, 3), & x \in (\tfrac{1}{2}, 2],\\
(0, 0), & \text{otherwise.}
\end{cases}
$$
Write $(t)_+ := \max(t, 0)$. Under Theorem 1 below, every half-open interval $(a, b]$ has nonnegative set measure $\lambda((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$ and every singleton has measure zero:
- *Forward versus reversed set measures.* On the ordered partition $(-1, 2] = (-1, \frac{1}{2}] \sqcup (\frac{1}{2}, 2]$, the set measures are $\lambda((-1, \frac{1}{2}]) = \frac{3}{2}$, $\lambda((\frac{1}{2}, 2]) = \frac{3}{2}$, and $\lambda((-1, 2]) = \frac{3}{2} + \frac{3}{2} = 3$. When the endpoints are reversed, $(2, -1] = (\frac{1}{2}, -1] = (2, \frac{1}{2}] = \emptyset$, so $\lambda((2, -1]) = (-1 - 2)_+ = 0$—**never** $-3$. Meanwhile the unordered half-open support $\operatorname{uIoc}(2, -1) := (\min(2, -1), \max(2, -1)] = (-1, 2]$ still has positive measure $\lvert -1 - 2\rvert = 3$.
- *Seam spike, endpoint exclusion and orientation sign.* The left value $f(-1) = (5, 5)$ lies outside $(-1, 2]$, and even on $[-1, 2]$ contributes $(5, 5)\,\lambda(\{-1\}) = (0, 0)$. The interior seam $x = \frac{1}{2}$ belongs to $(-1, \frac{1}{2}]$ and not to $(\frac{1}{2}, 2]$, yet the spike $f(\frac{1}{2}) = (10, -7)$ lies on $\{\frac{1}{2}\}$ of measure zero, so
$$
\int_{(-1, 1/2]} f\,d\lambda = \frac{3}{2}(2, -1) = \bigl(3, -\tfrac{3}{2}\bigr), \qquad
\int_{(1/2, 2]} f\,d\lambda = \frac{3}{2}(-4, 3) = \bigl(-6, \tfrac{9}{2}\bigr),
$$
and $\int_{(-1, 2]} f\,d\lambda = (-3, 3)$ while $\int_{(2, -1]} f\,d\lambda = \int_\emptyset f\,d\lambda = (0, 0)$. Mathlib defines the oriented integral by subtracting the two nonnegative-measure half-open integrals:
$$
\int_a^b f(x)\,d\mu := \int_{(a, b]} f\,d\mu - \int_{(b, a]} f\,d\mu.
$$
Thus $\int_{-1}^2 f\,d\lambda = (-3, 3) - (0, 0) = (-3, 3)$, whereas reversing the limits gives
$$
\int_2^{-1} f\,d\lambda = \int_{(2, -1]} f\,d\lambda - \int_{(-1, 2]} f\,d\lambda = (0, 0) - (-3, 3) = (3, -3) = -\int_{-1}^2 f\,d\lambda.
$$
For a constant vector $c = (1, 2)$, $\int_2^{-1} c\,d\lambda = \bigl(\lambda((2, -1]) - \lambda((-1, 2])\bigr)c = (0 - 3)(1, 2) = (-3, -6) = (-1 - 2)c$: the factor $-3$ arises from subtracting two nonnegative set lengths, not from a set of negative measure. Off the null singleton $\{\frac{1}{2}\}$, $\lVert f(x)\rVert$ equals $3$ on $(-1, \frac{1}{2})$ and $7$ on $(\frac{1}{2}, 2]$, so $\lVert f\rVert \le C := 7$ $\lambda$-almost everywhere on $\operatorname{uIoc}(2, -1)$ and
$$
\Bigl\lVert\int_2^{-1} f\,d\lambda\Bigr\rVert = 6 \le \int_{\operatorname{uIoc}(2, -1)} \lVert f\rVert\,d\lambda = \frac{3}{2}\cdot 3 + \frac{3}{2}\cdot 7 = 15 \le 7\,\lvert -1 - 2\rvert = 21.
$$
- *Non-monotone cycle and two-sided endpoint quotients.* Take the non-monotone triple $(-1, 2, \frac{1}{2})$, which overshoots $\frac{1}{2}$ to $2$ and returns to $\frac{1}{2}$. Since $\int_2^{1/2} f\,d\lambda = (0, 0) - (-6, \frac{9}{2}) = (6, -\frac{9}{2})$, adjacent additivity holds with signs:
$$
\int_{-1}^2 f\,d\lambda + \int_2^{1/2} f\,d\lambda = (-3, 3) + \bigl(6, -\tfrac{9}{2}\bigr) = \bigl(3, -\tfrac{3}{2}\bigr) = \int_{-1}^{1/2} f\,d\lambda.
$$
At the interior point $b_0 = 1 \in (\frac{1}{2}, 2)$, $\int_{-1}^1 f\,d\lambda = (3, -\frac{3}{2}) + \frac{1}{2}(-4, 3) = (1, 0)$. For $h = \frac{1}{4}$, $\int_{-1}^{5/4} f\,d\lambda = (0, \frac{3}{4})$ uses $(1, \frac{5}{4}]$ of length $\frac{1}{4}$; for $h = -\frac{1}{4}$, $\int_{-1}^{3/4} f\,d\lambda = (2, -\frac{3}{4})$ subtracts $(\frac{3}{4}, 1]$ of length $\frac{1}{4}$. Both difference quotients $\frac{1}{h}\bigl(\int_{-1}^{1+h} f\,d\lambda - \int_{-1}^1 f\,d\lambda\bigr)$ equal $(-4, 3) = f(1)$.

**Hand calculation 2 (countable open-set decomposition with a countable endpoint set and an accumulation point).** [Expository] For each integer $n \ge 0$, set $a_n := 1 - 2^{-n}$, $b_n := 1 - 3\cdot 2^{-(n+2)}$, and $I_n := (a_n, b_n)$. The first four components are $(0, \frac{1}{4})$, $(\frac{1}{2}, \frac{5}{8})$, $(\frac{3}{4}, \frac{13}{16})$ and $(\frac{7}{8}, \frac{29}{32})$, of lengths $\frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{8}, \frac{1}{16}, \frac{1}{32}$. Because $b_n = 1 - \frac{3}{4}\cdot 2^{-n} < 1 - \frac{1}{2}\cdot 2^{-n} = a_{n+1} < 1$, even the closed intervals $[a_n, b_n]$ are pairwise disjoint, and both $a_n$ and $b_n$ increase strictly to $1$. Let
$$
U := \bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty I_n \subseteq (0, 1).
$$
1. *Single-envelope cost versus countable-cover measure.* Every single half-open interval $(a, b]$ containing $U$ must satisfy $a \le \inf U = 0$ and $b \ge \sup U = 1$, while $U \subseteq (0, 1]$. Thus the raw single-interval envelope cost $\ell(U)$ defined below equals $1 - 0 = 1$. By contrast, each open component $I_n$ is Borel of measure $b_n - a_n = 2^{-(n+2)}$, the partial unions $U_N = \bigsqcup_{n=0}^{N-1} I_n$ have measures $\frac{1}{4}, \frac{3}{8}, \frac{7}{16}, \frac{15}{32}, \dots, \frac{1}{2}(1 - 2^{-N})$, and countable additivity gives
$$
\lambda(U) = \sum_{n=0}^\infty \lambda(I_n) = \sum_{n=0}^\infty 2^{-(n+2)} = \frac{1/4}{1 - 1/2} = \frac{1}{2} < 1 = \ell(U).
$$
2. *Countable boundary and open-gap check.* Put $E_0 := \bigcup_{n=0}^\infty \{a_n, b_n\}$ and $E := E_0 \cup \{1\}$. Every point of $E$ lies in $\overline U$ since $a_n, b_n \in \overline{I_n}$ and $1 = \lim_{n\to\infty} b_n$. Conversely, the complement of $U \cup E$ is
$$
\mathbb R \setminus (U \cup E) = (-\infty, 0) \sqcup \Bigl(\bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty (b_n, a_{n+1})\Bigr) \sqcup (1, \infty),
$$
a union of open intervals, so $U \cup E$ is closed; hence $\overline U = U \cup E$ and $\partial U = \overline U \setminus U = E$. Because $E$ is countable and every singleton has $\lambda$-measure zero, $\lambda(E_0) = \lambda(E) = 0$. Consequently, adjoining left or right endpoints or taking the full closure leaves the measure unchanged:
$$
\lambda(U) = \lambda\Bigl(\bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty (a_n, b_n]\Bigr) = \lambda\Bigl(\bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty [a_n, b_n]\Bigr) = \lambda(\overline U) = \frac{1}{2}.
$$
Meanwhile the open gap union $G := \bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty (b_n, a_{n+1})$ inside $(0, 1)$ has gap lengths $a_{n+1} - b_n = 2^{-(n+2)}$ and measure $\lambda(G) = \frac{1}{2}$, matching the disjoint partition $(0, 1] = U \sqcup G \sqcup (E \setminus \{0\})$ of total measure $\frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2} + 0 = 1$. Countability of $\partial U$ is essential here: if one instead deletes open middle intervals of total length $\frac{1}{2}$ to form a Smith–Volterra–Cantor open complement $V \subseteq (0, 1)$, the countably many component endpoints still have measure zero, whereas $\partial V = \overline V \setminus V$ is an uncountable nowhere-dense Cantor set of measure $1 - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{2} > 0$.

**Definitions and named inputs.** Let $\mathbb R$ carry its order topology and Borel $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal B(\mathbb R)$ (the $\sigma$-algebra generated by all open subsets of $\mathbb R$). In `Mathlib/MeasureTheory/Measure/Stieltjes.lean`, a `StieltjesFunction R` bundles a map $f : R \to \mathbb R$ that is monotone ($x \le y \implies f(x) \le f(y)$) and right-continuous at every point ($\lim_{y \to x^+} f(y) = f(x)$) [Lean, read]. On $R = \mathbb R$, which is nonempty and has neither a least element (`botSet = ∅`) nor a greatest element (`Iotop a b = Ioo a b`), the identity `StieltjesFunction.id` is $f(x) = x$, with both right and left limits equal to $x$ (`id_leftLim`) [Lean, read]. Its **single-interval envelope cost** (`StieltjesFunction.length`) is defined on **every** subset $S \subseteq \mathbb R$ by
$$
\ell(S) := \inf\bigl\{ (b - a)_+ \in [0, \infty] : a, b \in \mathbb R,\ S \subseteq (a, b] \bigr\},
$$
with $\inf \emptyset = \infty$ when $S$ is not bounded above and below. [Pinned `Stieltjes.lean:62–169,266–276`][ch3-rv-stieltjes-def]

To avoid circularity, we state explicitly the four general inputs that remain `[Lean]` (or are inherited from Chapter 15's audited sections) and contain **no** real interval-length assumption:

- **(T1) Heine–Borel finite subcover on $[a, b]$.** [Lean] Every covering of a closed bounded real interval $[a, b]$ by open sets admits a finite subcovering (`isCompact_Icc.elim_finite_subcover_image`).
- **(C1) Outer measure from a raw set cost and Carathéodory splitting.** [Lean, read] *(Proved and audited in Chapter 15; `OuterMeasure/OfFunction.lean:57–130`, `OuterMeasure/Caratheodory.lean:55–214`.)* For *any* set $X$ and *any* function $m : \mathcal P(X) \to [0, \infty]$ satisfying $m(\emptyset) = 0$, the countable-cover infimum
$$
\mu^*(S) := \inf\Bigl\{ \sum_{i=0}^\infty m(T_i) : (T_i)_{i \in \mathbb N} \subseteq \mathcal P(X),\ S \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty T_i \Bigr\}
$$
is an outer measure on $X$ ($\mu^*(\emptyset) = 0$, monotone, countably subadditive) satisfying $\mu^*(S) \le m(S)$ for all $S \subseteq X$. The Carathéodory family $\mathcal C(\mu^*) := \{ E \subseteq X : \forall T \subseteq X,\ \mu^*(T) = \mu^*(T \cap E) + \mu^*(T \setminus E) \}$ is a $\sigma$-algebra on which $\mu^*$ is countably additive over pairwise disjoint sequences (`iUnion_eq_of_caratheodory`), and any set $E \subseteq X$ that splits the raw cost subadditively—meaning $m(T \cap E) + m(T \setminus E) \le m(T)$ for all $T \subseteq X$—belongs to $\mathcal C(\mu^*)$ (`ofFunction_caratheodory`). [Pinned `OfFunction.lean`][ch3-rv-offunc] · [Pinned `Caratheodory.lean`][ch3-rv-cara]
- **(U1) Rational open-interval uniqueness for locally finite Borel measures.** [Lean, read] Two Borel measures $\mu,\nu$ on $\mathbb R$, with $\mu$ locally finite, are equal when they agree on $(p,q)$ for every rational pair $p,q$. The finite-restriction proof of this input is given [below](#how-rational-open-intervals-determine-a-real-borel-measure). [Pinned `Real.measure_ext_Ioo_rat`][ch3-rv-borel-real]
- **(U2) Inner-product basis definition of `volume` and $\sigma$-finite Haar uniqueness.** [Lean, read] *(Haar uniqueness proved and audited in [Chapter 15, §15.4.3.3](../chapters/15-two-proofs-side-by-side.md#15433-uniqueness-of-haar-measure-proportionality-normalization-and-the-constant-in-the-half-density-transport); `Haar/OfBasis.lean:97–125,187–202,255–320`, `Haar/Basic.lean:670–686`.)* On $\mathbb R$ as a one-dimensional real inner product space, `(volume : Measure ℝ)` is defined as `b₀.toBasis.addHaar = addHaarMeasure K₀`, where $b_0 = \text{`stdOrthonormalBasis ℝ ℝ`}$ and $K_0 = \text{`parallelepiped b₀`}$ equals either $[0, 1]$ or $[-1, 0]$ (`parallelepiped_orthonormalBasis_one_dim`, since a unit vector in $\mathbb R$ is $\pm 1$). For any $\sigma$-finite left-translation-invariant Borel measure $\mu$ on $\mathbb R$, `addHaarMeasure_unique` gives $\mu = \mu(K_0)\cdot\operatorname{addHaarMeasure}(K_0)$. [Pinned `OfBasis.lean`][ch3-rv-basis] · [Pinned `Haar/Basic.lean`][ch3-rv-haar]

**Lemma 1 (single-interval envelope and compact-to-open peeling).** [Lean, read], conditional on **(T1)**.
1. *Envelope properties (`length_empty`, `length_mono`, `length_Ioc`).* On $\mathbb R$, $\ell(\emptyset) = 0$, $S_1 \subseteq S_2 \implies \ell(S_1) \le \ell(S_2)$, and $\ell((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$ for all $a, b \in \mathbb R$.
2. *Compact-to-open subadditivity (`length_subadditive_Icc_Ioo`).* For any $a, b \in \mathbb R$ and real sequences $(c_i)_{i \in \mathbb N}, (d_i)_{i \in \mathbb N}$, if $[a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty (c_i, d_i)$, then $(b - a)_+ \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty (d_i - c_i)_+$. [Pinned `Stieltjes.lean:281–307,334–364`][ch3-rv-peel]

**Proof.** For (1), $\emptyset \subseteq (0, 0]$ gives $\ell(\emptyset) \le (0 - 0)_+ = 0$, and monotonicity is immediate from shrinking the set of admissible envelopes. Taking $(a, b]$ itself gives $\ell((a, b]) \le (b - a)_+$. Conversely, suppose $(a, b] \subseteq (a', b']$. If $b \le a$, then $(b - a)_+ = 0 \le (b' - a')_+$. If $a < b$, then $b \in (a, b] \subseteq (a', b']$ forces $b \le b'$; if $a < a'$, setting $x := \min\bigl(\frac{a + a'}{2}, b\bigr)$ would give $x \in (a, b]$ with $x < a'$, contradicting $x \in (a', b']$. Thus $a' \le a < b \le b'$, so $(b - a)_+ = b - a \le b' - a' = (b' - a')_+$. Taking the infimum over $(a', b']$ yields $\ell((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$.

For (2), **(T1)** extracts a finite subset $F \subseteq \mathbb N$ such that $[a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i \in F} (c_i, d_i)$. Since $\sum_{i \in F} (d_i - c_i)_+ \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty (d_i - c_i)_+$, it suffices to prove by strong induction on $\lvert F\rvert$, universally quantified over $b \in \mathbb R$ with $a$ fixed, that $[a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i \in F} (c_i, d_i)$ implies $(b - a)_+ \le \sum_{i \in F} (d_i - c_i)_+$. If $b \le a$, the left-hand side is $0$. Otherwise $a < b$, so $b \in [a, b]$ lies in $(c_i, d_i)$ for some $i \in F$, meaning $c_i < b < d_i$. Set $F' := F \setminus \{i\}$, so $\lvert F'\rvert = \lvert F\rvert - 1$. Every $x \in [a, c_i]$ satisfies $a \le x \le c_i < b$, hence belongs to $[a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{j \in F} (c_j, d_j)$, and cannot lie in $(c_i, d_i)$ because $x \le c_i$; thus $[a, c_i] \subseteq \bigcup_{j \in F'} (c_j, d_j)$ (vacuously if $c_i < a$). By the induction hypothesis at $(F', c_i)$, subadditivity $(u + v)_+ \le u_+ + v_+$, and $b < d_i$,
$$
(b - a)_+ \le (c_i - a)_+ + (b - c_i)_+ \le \sum_{j \in F'} (d_j - c_j)_+ + (d_i - c_i)_+ = \sum_{j \in F} (d_j - c_j)_+. \qquad \square
$$

**Lemma 2 (outer-measure recovery, Borel measurability and trimming).** [Lean, read], conditional on **(T1)** and **(C1)**. Let $\mu^*$ be the outer measure built from $\ell$ by **(C1)**.
1. *Exact half-open outer measure (`outer_Ioc`).* For all $a, b \in \mathbb R$, $\mu^*((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$.
2. *Ray splitting and Borel sets (`measurableSet_Ioi`, `borel_le_measurable`).* Every open ray $(c, \infty)$ lies in $\mathcal C(\mu^*)$, and hence $\mathcal B(\mathbb R) \subseteq \mathcal C(\mu^*)$.
3. *No trimming gap (`outer_trim`, `StieltjesFunction.measure`).* For every subset $S \subseteq \mathbb R$, $\inf\{ \mu^*(B) : S \subseteq B,\ B \in \mathcal B(\mathbb R) \} = \mu^*(S)$, so $\mu^*$ directly defines a Borel measure $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}} := \text{`StieltjesFunction.id.measure`}$ with $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$. [Pinned `Stieltjes.lean:367–527`][ch3-rv-outer]

**Proof.** (1) By **(C1)** and Lemma 1(1), $\mu^*((a, b]) \le \ell((a, b]) = (b - a)_+$. For the lower bound, if $b \le a$ then $(b - a)_+ = 0 \le \mu^*((a, b])$. Assume $a < b$, let $(a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty S_i$, and suppose $\sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(S_i) < \infty$ (otherwise the bound is trivial). Given $\varepsilon > 0$, set $\delta := \varepsilon / 2 > 0$ and $\varepsilon_i := \delta \cdot 2^{-(i+1)}$, so $\sum_{i=0}^\infty \varepsilon_i = \delta$. First shift the lower endpoint right to $a' := a + \delta > a$ (for a general Stieltjes function $f$, right-continuity at $a$ chooses $a' > a$ with $f(a') - f(a) < \delta$). Next, since $\ell(S_i) < \infty$, choose $p_i, q'_i \in \mathbb R$ with $S_i \subseteq (p_i, q'_i]$ and $(q'_i - p_i)_+ < \ell(S_i) + \varepsilon_i / 2$, and shift each upper endpoint right to $q_i := q'_i + \varepsilon_i / 2 > q'_i$ (again using right-continuity at $q'_i$ for general $f$). Then $S_i \subseteq (p_i, q'_i] \subseteq (p_i, q_i)$ and $(q_i - p_i)_+ \le (q'_i - p_i)_+ + \varepsilon_i / 2 < \ell(S_i) + \varepsilon_i$. Every $x \in [a', b]$ satisfies $a < a' \le x \le b$, so $[a', b] \subseteq (a, b] \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty (p_i, q_i)$. Lemma 1(2) gives
$$
(b - a)_+ \le (b - a')_+ + (a' - a)_+ \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty (q_i - p_i)_+ + \delta \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(S_i) + \delta + \delta = \sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(S_i) + \varepsilon.
$$
Taking the infimum over covers $(S_i)$ and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ yields $(b - a)_+ \le \mu^*((a, b])$.

(2) Fix $c \in \mathbb R$ and $T \subseteq \mathbb R$. By **(C1)** (`ofFunction_caratheodory`), $(c, \infty) \in \mathcal C(\mu^*)$ follows once $\ell(T \cap (c, \infty)) + \ell(T \setminus (c, \infty)) \le \ell(T)$. If no $(a, b]$ contains $T$, $\ell(T) = \infty$. Otherwise let $T \subseteq (a, b]$. Since $T \cap (c, \infty) \subseteq (\max(a, c), b]$ and $T \setminus (c, \infty) \subseteq (a, \min(b, c)]$, Lemma 1(1) bounds the left-hand side by $(b - \max(a, c))_+ + (\min(b, c) - a)_+$. Across all four order cases—$b \le a$ ($0 + 0 = 0$), $a < b \le c$ ($0 + (b - a) = b - a$), $c \le a < b$ ($(b - a) + 0 = b - a$), and $a < c < b$ ($(b - c) + (c - a) = b - a$)—this sum equals $(b - a)_+$. Taking the infimum over $(a, b] \supseteq T$ proves $(c, \infty) \in \mathcal C(\mu^*)$. Because $\mathcal C(\mu^*)$ is a $\sigma$-algebra (**C1**), it also contains $(-\infty, d] = \mathbb R \setminus (d, \infty)$, $(-\infty, d) = \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty (-\infty, d - \frac{1}{n}]$, every open interval $(c, d) = (c, \infty) \cap (-\infty, d)$, every open set $U \subseteq \mathbb R$ (as the union of rational open intervals $(p, q) \subseteq U$, $p, q \in \mathbb Q$), and therefore $\mathcal B(\mathbb R)$.

(3) Write $\mu^*.\mathrm{trim}(S) := \inf\{ \mu^*(B) : S \subseteq B,\ B \in \mathcal B(\mathbb R) \}$. Monotonicity gives $\mu^*(S) \le \mu^*.\mathrm{trim}(S)$. Conversely, if $\mu^*(S) < \infty$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, pick $S \subseteq \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty T_i$ with $\sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(T_i) < \mu^*(S) + \varepsilon / 2$, and for each $i$ pick $(a_i, b_i] \supseteq T_i$ with $(b_i - a_i)_+ < \ell(T_i) + \varepsilon 2^{-(i+2)}$. Each $(a_i, b_i] = (a_i, \infty) \cap (-\infty, b_i]$ is Borel, so $B := \bigcup_{i=0}^\infty (a_i, b_i] \in \mathcal B(\mathbb R)$ covers $S$, and countable subadditivity with part (1) gives
$$
\mu^*.\mathrm{trim}(S) \le \mu^*(B) \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty \mu^*\bigl((a_i, b_i]\bigr) = \sum_{i=0}^\infty (b_i - a_i)_+ \le \sum_{i=0}^\infty \ell(T_i) + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} < \mu^*(S) + \varepsilon.
$$
Letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ proves $\mu^*.\mathrm{trim} = \mu^*$. In Mathlib (`MeasureSpaceDef.lean:115–116`), a `Measure ℝ` consists of an outer measure that is countably additive on disjoint Borel sequences and satisfies `trim ≤ self`; both fields are now discharged directly by `f.outer` without an extra `toMeasure` wrapper (`Stieltjes.lean:517–522`). $\square$ [Pinned `MeasureSpaceDef.lean:101–175`][ch3-rv-mdef]

**Theorem 1 (real Lebesgue interval lengths, atom-freeness, Haar identification and consumer bounds).** [Lean, read], conditional on **(T1)**, **(C1)**, **(U1)** and **(U2)**. Let $\lambda := (\text{`volume`} : \text{`Measure ℝ`})$.
1. *Identification (`volume_eq_stieltjes_id`).* $\lambda$ equals $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}} = \text{`StieltjesFunction.id.measure`}$ on all subsets of $\mathbb R$, is left- and right-translation-invariant, and assigns mass $1$ to $[0, 1]$ and $[-1, 0]$.
2. *Atoms, all four intervals and open decompositions (`volume_singleton`, `volume_Ioc`, `volume_Ioo`, `volume_Icc`, `volume_Ico`, `volume_uIoc`, `nullSingletonClass_volume`).* Every singleton $\{x\}$ and every countable subset $C \subseteq \mathbb R$ satisfy $\lambda(\{x\}) = \lambda(C) = 0$. For all $a, b \in \mathbb R$,
$$
\lambda\bigl((a, b]\bigr) = \lambda\bigl((a, b)\bigr) = \lambda\bigl([a, b]\bigr) = \lambda\bigl([a, b)\bigr) = (b - a)_+,
$$
and on unordered intervals $\lambda(\operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)) = \lambda(\operatorname{uIoo}(a, b)) = \lambda(\operatorname{uIcc}(a, b)) = \lvert b - a\rvert$. Every pairwise disjoint countable union $U = \bigsqcup_{n \in J} (a_n, b_n)$ ($a_n \le b_n$) satisfies $\lambda(U) = \sum_{n \in J} (b_n - a_n)$, and adjoining any countable set $E$ gives $\lambda(U \cup E) = \lambda(U)$.
3. *Local finiteness and integration consumers (`locallyFinite_volume`, `integral_const`, `norm_integral_le_of_norm_le_const_ae`).* For every $x \in \mathbb R$, $\lambda((x - 1, x + 1)) = 2 < \infty$, so $\lambda$ is locally finite and finite at every filter finer than $\mathcal N(x)$. For any real Banach space $E$, $a, b \in \mathbb R$, $c \in E$, and $f : \mathbb R \to E$:
   - $\int_a^b c\,d\lambda = (b - a)\cdot c$ (using completeness of $E$ when $c \ne 0$ and $a \ne b$);
   - if $\lVert f(x)\rVert \le C$ for $\lambda$-almost every $x \in \operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)$ (using the named set-integral bound `norm_setIntegral_le_of_norm_le_const_ae'` `[Lean]`), then $\bigl\lVert\int_a^b f(x)\,d\lambda\bigr\rVert \le C\lvert b - a\rvert$. [Pinned `Lebesgue/Basic.lean:54–223`][ch3-rv-lebesgue] · [Pinned `IntervalIntegral/Basic.lean:762–839`][ch3-rv-int-basic]

**Proof.** First establish singleton nullity and the four interval formulas for $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$. Fix $x \in \mathbb R$. Since $\{x\} \subseteq (x - \frac{1}{n+1}, x]$ for every $n \in \mathbb N$, monotonicity and Lemma 2(3) give $0 \le \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(\{x\}) \le \frac{1}{n+1}$ for all $n$, hence $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(\{x\}) = 0$. *(For a general Stieltjes function $f$, `measure_singleton` at `Stieltjes.lean:530–566` chooses $u_n \uparrow a$, writes $(u_0, a] = \{a\} \sqcup \bigsqcup_{n=0}^\infty (u_n, u_{n+1}]$ of total finite measure $f(a) - f(u_0) < \infty$, and cancels the telescopic sum $\sum_{n=0}^\infty (f(u_{n+1}) - f(u_n)) = f(a^-) - f(u_0)$ to obtain the jump formula $\mu_f(\{a\}) = (f(a) - f(a^-))_+$, which vanishes when $f$ is continuous from the left.)* Countable subadditivity then gives $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(C) \le \sum_{c \in C} \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(\{c\}) = 0$ for every countable $C$.

For $[a, b]$: if $a > b$, $[a, b] = \emptyset$ and $(b - a)_+ = 0$; if $a \le b$, the disjoint Borel decomposition $[a, b] = \{a\} \sqcup (a, b]$ gives $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([a, b]) = 0 + (b - a) = (b - a)_+$ (`measure_Icc`). For $(a, b)$: if $b \le a$, $(a, b) = \emptyset$ and $(b - a)_+ = 0$; if $a < b$, $(a, b] = (a, b) \sqcup \{b\}$ gives $b - a = \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}((a, b)) + 0$, so $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}((a, b)) = (b - a)_+$ (`measure_Ioo`; for general $f$, cancelling the finite atom $f(b) - f(b^-) < \infty$ from $f(b) - f(a)$ yields $(f(b^-) - f(a))_+$). Similarly $[a, b) = \{a\} \sqcup (a, b)$ when $a < b$ gives $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([a, b)) = (b - a)_+$ (`measure_Ico`). In particular $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}((x - 1, x + 1)) = 2 < \infty$ and $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([x - 1, x + 1]) = 2 < \infty$, so $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ is locally finite (`instIsLocallyFiniteMeasure`, `Stieltjes.lean:727–731`) and $\sigma$-finite via $\mathbb R = \bigcup_{n=0}^\infty (-(n+1), n+1)$.

Now bridge $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ to $\lambda = \text{`volume`}$ (`volume_eq_stieltjes_id`, `Lebesgue/Basic.lean:54–70`). For any $a \in \mathbb R$ and $p, q \in \mathbb Q$, the preimage of $(p, q)$ under $x \mapsto a + x$ is $(p - a, q - a)$, so
$$
\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}\bigl((a + \cdot)^{-1}(p, q)\bigr) = \bigl((q - a) - (p - a)\bigr)_+ = (q - p)_+ = \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}\bigl((p, q)\bigr).
$$
Since $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ is locally finite, **(U1)** implies $(a + \cdot)_*\lambda_{\mathrm{id}} = \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ for all $a \in \mathbb R$, i.e. `IsAddLeftInvariant λ_id` (and right translation equals left translation on commutative $\mathbb R$). On both branches $K_0 \in \{[0, 1], [-1, 0]\}$ of `parallelepiped (stdOrthonormalBasis ℝ ℝ)` in **(U2)**, $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([0, 1]) = (1 - 0)_+ = 1$ and $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}([-1, 0]) = (0 - (-1))_+ = 1$, so $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(K_0) = 1$. Since $\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}$ is $\sigma$-finite and left-invariant, **(U2)** (`addHaarMeasure_unique`) yields
$$
\lambda_{\mathrm{id}} = \lambda_{\mathrm{id}}(K_0)\cdot\operatorname{addHaarMeasure}(K_0) = 1\cdot\lambda = \lambda.
$$
Parts (1) and (2) now transfer verbatim to $\lambda$: unordered intervals satisfy $\max(a, b) - \min(a, b) = \lvert b - a\rvert$, pairwise disjoint open intervals sum by **(C1)** on $\mathcal B(\mathbb R)$, and $\lambda(U \cup E) = \lambda(U) + \lambda(E \setminus U) = \lambda(U)$ since $E \setminus U \subseteq E$ is null.

For part (3), $(x - 1, x + 1) \in \mathcal N(x)$ has measure $2 < \infty$, so every filter $\ell' \le \mathcal N(x)$ contains $(x - 1, x + 1)$ (`FTCFilter.finiteAt_inner`). In a complete space $E$, `setIntegral_const` gives $\int_S c\,d\lambda = \lambda.\mathrm{real}(S)\cdot c$ whenever $\lambda(S) < \infty$, where $\lambda.\mathrm{real}(S) := (\lambda(S)).\mathrm{toReal}$. Evaluating on $(a, b]$ and $(b, a]$ and applying the real identity $(t)_+ - (-t)_+ = t$ at $t = b - a$ (`max_zero_sub_eq_self`, `IntervalIntegral/Basic.lean:837–838`) gives
$$
\int_a^b c\,d\lambda = \bigl((b - a)_+ - (a - b)_+\bigr)\cdot c = (b - a)\cdot c.
$$
Finally, $\bigl\lVert\int_a^b f\,d\lambda\bigr\rVert = \bigl\lVert\int_{\operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)} f\,d\lambda\bigr\rVert$ (`norm_integral_eq_norm_integral_uIoc`). Since $\lambda(\operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)) = \lvert b - a\rvert < \infty$ and $\lambda.\mathrm{real}(\operatorname{uIoc}(a, b)) = \lvert b - a\rvert$, `norm_setIntegral_le_of_norm_le_const_ae'` (`Bochner/Set.lean:594–615`) yields $\bigl\lVert\int_a^b f\,d\lambda\bigr\rVert \le C\lvert b - a\rvert$. $\square$ [Pinned `Bochner/Set.lean:588–728`][ch3-rv-set]

**What breaks—and what survives—upon adding a Dirac atom.** [Expository] To isolate the exact role of atom-freeness (`NullSingletonClass`, i.e. $\mu(\{x\}) = 0$ for all $x$), stay on the **same measurable space** $(\mathbb R, \mathcal B(\mathbb R))$ and set
$$
\mu := \lambda + 2\,\delta_{1/2},
$$
which is the Stieltjes measure of the monotone right-continuous jump function $F(x) = x + 2\,\mathbf 1_{[1/2, \infty)}(x)$ (`StieltjesFunction.measure_add`, `Measure/Dirac.lean:74`).
- *What survives (and why set length is never negative).* $\mu$ is a locally finite, $\sigma$-finite Borel measure with values in $[0, \infty]$. Every reversed half-open set $(1, 0] = \emptyset$ still has $\mu((1, 0]) = 0 \ge 0$, while $\mu((0, 1]) = 1 + 2 = 3$. Thus $\int_1^0 1\,d\mu = \mu((1, 0]) - \mu((0, 1]) = 0 - 3 = -3$: the negative sign belongs entirely to the oriented integral difference, not to any negative measure of $(1, 0]$. Moreover, adjacent-interval additivity $\int_a^b f\,d\mu + \int_b^c f\,d\mu = \int_a^c f\,d\mu$ (`integral_add_adjacent_intervals`, `IntervalIntegral/Basic.lean:1095–1098`) holds with no atom-freeness hypothesis on $\mu$ when both `IntervalIntegrable f μ a b` and `IntervalIntegrable f μ b c` hold. For the constant function here both pieces have finite mass, and $(0, 1] = (0, \frac{1}{2}] \sqcup (\frac{1}{2}, 1]$ places the seam point $\frac{1}{2}$ in the left piece once and in the right piece not at all: $\int_0^{1/2} 1\,d\mu + \int_{1/2}^1 1\,d\mu = \frac{5}{2} + \frac{1}{2} = 3 = \int_0^1 1\,d\mu$.
- *What fails without atom-freeness.* At $x_0 = \frac{1}{2}$, $\mu(\{\frac{1}{2}\}) = F(\frac{1}{2}) - F(\frac{1}{2}^-) = \frac{5}{2} - \frac{1}{2} = 2 \ne 0$. Consequently:
  1. *Open, half-open and closed intervals split apart:* $\mu((0, \frac{1}{2})) = \mu([0, \frac{1}{2})) = \frac{1}{2}$ whereas $\mu((0, \frac{1}{2}]) = \mu([0, \frac{1}{2}]) = \frac{5}{2}$; symmetrically, $\mu((\frac{1}{2}, 1)) = \mu((\frac{1}{2}, 1]) = \frac{1}{2}$ whereas $\mu([\frac{1}{2}, 1)) = \mu([\frac{1}{2}, 1]) = \frac{5}{2}$. In Hand calculation 2, since $a_1 = \frac{1}{2} \in E = \overline U \setminus U$, $\mu(U) = \frac{1}{2} \ne \frac{5}{2} = \mu(\overline U)$.
  2. *Endpoint-conversion and single-point modification lemmas fail:* $\int_{[1/2, 1]} 1\,d\mu = \frac{5}{2} \ne \frac{1}{2} = \int_{(1/2, 1]} 1\,d\mu$, breaking `integral_Icc_eq_integral_Ioc` (`Bochner/Set.lean:706–707`, the step used at `WindowHypOfRpow.lean:52`), and the spike $f(\frac{1}{2}) = (10, -7)$ in Hand calculation 1 shifts $\int_{-1}^2 f\,d\mu$ by $2\bigl((10, -7) - (2, -1)\bigr) = (16, -12) \ne (0, 0)$.
  3. *Translation invariance, $(b - a)\cdot c$, $C\lvert b - a\rvert$ and ordinary left-side FTC fail:* $\mu((0, \frac{1}{2})) = \frac{1}{2} \ne \frac{5}{2} = \mu((\frac{1}{4}, \frac{3}{4}))$; $\int_0^1 c\,d\mu = 3c \ne (1 - 0)c$; $\bigl\lvert\int_0^1 1\,d\mu\bigr\rvert = 3 > 1\cdot\lvert 1 - 0\rvert$; and for $G(x) := \int_0^x 1\,d\mu$, right increments at $\frac{1}{2}$ satisfy $G(\frac{1}{2} + h) - G(\frac{1}{2}) = \mu((\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2} + h]) = h$ ($h > 0$), whereas left increments satisfy $G(\frac{1}{2} + h) - G(\frac{1}{2}) = -\mu((\frac{1}{2} + h, \frac{1}{2}]) = h - 2 \to -2 \ne 0$ as $h \uparrow 0$ because $(\frac{1}{2} + h, \frac{1}{2}]$ captures the atom at $\frac{1}{2}$. *(If one instead keeps monotonicity and drops right-continuity, setting $F_{\mathrm{left}}(x) := x + 2\,\mathbf 1_{(1/2, \infty)}(x)$, the raw differences $F_{\mathrm{left}}(\frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{n+1}) - F_{\mathrm{left}}(\frac{1}{2}) = 2 + \frac{1}{n+1} \to 2 \ne 0$ fail downward countable additivity along $(\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{n+1}] \downarrow \emptyset$.)* [Pinned `Measure/Dirac.lean:67–80`][ch3-rv-dirac]

**Remarks on classical literature, attribution and verification scope.**

1. *All-subset envelope versus semiring premeasures.* [Paper] Henri Lebesgue introduced linear outer measure from countable open-interval coverings in his 1902 thesis *Intégrale, longueur, aire* (*Annali di Matematica Pura ed Applicata* (3) **7**, 1902, pp. 231–359), and Constantin Carathéodory formulated outer measures on all subsets and the symmetric cut criterion without inner measure in *Über das lineare Maß von Punktmengen — eine Verallgemeinerung des Längenbegriffs* (*Nachr. Ges. Wiss. Göttingen, Math.-Phys. Kl.*, 1914, pp. 404–426) [Carathéodory (1914)][ch3-rv-cara-paper]. Patrick Billingsley, *Probability and Measure* (3rd ed., Wiley, 1995), proves finite peeling and Heine–Borel subadditivity on $(a, b]$ in Theorem 1.3 (pp. 12–13), extends semiring premeasures via Carathéodory's criterion in Theorems 11.1 and 11.3 and Example 11.3 (pp. 165–168), and constructs Stieltjes measures $\mu((a, b]) = F(b) - F(a)$ for nondecreasing right-continuous $F : \mathbb R \to \mathbb R$ (specializing to $F(x) = x$ on p. 176) in Theorem 12.4 and the proof of Theorem 12.5 (pp. 175–179), using the exact two right-continuity shifts and Heine–Borel step on p. 179; see also Terence Tao, [245A Notes 1](https://terrytao.wordpress.com/2010/09/09/245a-notes-1-lebesgue-measure/), Lemmas 6–7 for box outer-measure recovery and Exercise 17 for the Carathéodory criterion. Whereas Billingsley first restricts to the semiring of finite intervals and checks premeasure additivity there, Mathlib's `StieltjesFunction` defines $\ell$ directly on all subsets, checks Carathéodory's inequality only on open rays $(c, \infty)$, proves `outer_trim`, and identifies `StieltjesFunction.id.measure` with the inner-product Haar `volume` by `addHaarMeasure_unique`. Because both the classical theorems and pinned Mathlib cover the mathematical result in full, no mathematical priority is claimed here.
2. *Credits and verification boundary.* File-header authors for the pinned Stieltjes, real-Borel, basis-Haar and Lebesgue files are Johannes Hölzl, Sébastien Gouëzel, Yury Kudryashov, Kexing Ying and Mario Carneiro, at Mathlib `0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474` inside Archive A2 (`9631b4f020199e580ba86178cb56c6e9b09f690c2369a80dc4334aa93f49c7fc`). The declarations in Lemma 1, Lemma 2 and Theorem 1 are at author-level local R3 `[Lean, read]` modulo the stated inputs **(T1)**, **(C1)**, **(U1)**, **(U2)** and `norm_setIntegral_le_of_norm_le_const_ae'`; filter pullback, endpoint FTC, Bochner $L^1$ extension, simple-function density, Chapter 15's peak concentration and Chapter 17's radial Haar/Gamma retain their separate scopes. Read-only source inspection is distinct from whole-archive Lean kernel replay, `Main.lean` proof-term reachability, and peer review. #### How rational open intervals determine a real Borel measure

The preceding construction needs one uniqueness argument before it can invoke Haar uniqueness. Let $\ell$ be `StieltjesFunction.id.measure` and let $T_a(x)=a+x$. The real-interval formula gives the mass of $T_a^{-1}(p,q)$ for each rational pair $p,q$. To prove $(T_a)_*\ell=\ell$ we must get from these tests to every Borel set. Both measures have infinite total mass, so subtracting the mass of a set from the mass of the whole line is unavailable. The argument first compares restrictions to sets of finite mass. [Interval-length construction](#where-interval-lengths-in-real-lebesgue-measure-come-from) · [Pinned translation call][ch3-u1-volume]

**Two examples.** [Expository] Use the interval laws and the atomic Stieltjes measure from the preceding subsection.

1. Take $a=3/2$ and $\nu=(T_{3/2})_*\ell$. For rational $p<q$,
   $$
   \nu((p,q))=\ell((p-3/2,q-3/2))=(q-3/2)-(p-3/2)=q-p.
   $$
   The empty intervals with $q\le p$ have mass zero for both measures. For a specific finite restriction take $I=(-1/2,5/4)$ and $B_0=(-1,1)$. The mass of $I$ in both measures is $7/4$. Then $I\cap B_0=(-1/2,1)$ and $B_0\setminus I=(-1,-1/2]$. For $\ell$ their masses are $3/2$ and $1/2$. For $\nu$ the preimages of these pieces are $(-2,-1/2)$ and $(-5/2,-2]$, with the same masses. The total on $B_0$ is $2$ for each measure, so the mass of the complementary piece is also recovered as $2-3/2=1/2$. The theorem below gives $\nu=\ell$ as Borel measures. For example, the interval with irrational endpoint $[0,\sqrt{2})$ has mass $\sqrt{2}$ for both. The same general calculation works for every real $a$, since the interval law for $\ell$ takes real endpoints.
2. Let $F(x)=x+2\mathbf 1_{[1/2,\infty)}(x)$ and use its Borel Stieltjes measure $\eta=\ell+2\delta_{1/2}$. Its values on rational open intervals are $(q-p)_++2\mathbf 1_{\{p<1/2<q\}}$. The interval $(0,1/2)$ puts the atom at the excluded endpoint, so both $\eta$ and $\ell$ give it mass $1/2$. The test $(1/4,3/4)$ contains the atom and has masses $5/2$ and $1/2$ under $\eta$ and $\ell$, respectively. The shrinking rational intervals $J_n=(1/2-2^{-n-2},1/2+2^{-n-2})$ for $n\ge0$ intersect in $\{1/2\}$. Their respective masses are $2+2^{-n-1}$ and $2^{-n-1}$. The first term is finite for each measure, so continuity from above recovers the singleton masses $2$ and $0$. Indeed one applies continuity from below to $J_0\setminus J_n$ and subtracts from the finite mass of $J_0$. The atom is visible using open tests alone.

**Definitions.** Mathlib calls a family $\mathcal P$ a $\pi$-system if every nonempty intersection of two members belongs to $\mathcal P$. In particular the family used here is exactly
$$
\mathcal P_{\mathbb Q}=\{(p,q):p,q\in\mathbb Q,\ p<q\},
$$
viewed as a family of subsets of $\mathbb R$. A Dynkin system on $X$ contains $\varnothing$ and is closed under complements in $X$ and under countable pairwise disjoint unions. A finite spanning sequence for $\mu$ in $\mathcal P$ is a sequence $(B_n)_{n\ge0}$ in $\mathcal P$ with $\mu(B_n)<\infty$ for every $n$ and $\bigcup_n B_n=X$. The restriction $\mu|_B$ is a measure on the same measurable space, with $(\mu|_B)(A)=\mu(A\cap B)$ for measurable $A$. In particular $(\mu|_B)(X)=\mu(B)$. [Pinned definitions][ch3-u1-pisystem] · [Pinned restriction formula][ch3-u1-restrict-apply]

**Lemma (Dynkin generation).** [Lean, read] If $\mathcal P$ is a $\pi$-system, its least Dynkin system is $\sigma(\mathcal P)$.

**Proof.** Form the least Dynkin system $D$ by closing the generators under the three rules defining a Dynkin system. In Lean these are the constructors of `GenerateHas` together with the constructor for a generator. Induction on those rules gives minimality. If $U,V\in D$ and $V\subseteq U$, then $U\setminus V=(U^c\cup V)^c\in D$ because $U^c$ and $V$ are disjoint.

For $S\in D$ set $D_S=\{A\subseteq X:A\cap S\in D\}$. It contains $\varnothing$. When $A\cap S\in D$, the complement test is $A^c\cap S=S\setminus(A\cap S)$, which belongs to $D$ by the nested difference rule. Intersecting a disjoint union with $S$ distributes over the union and preserves disjointness. Thus $D_S$ is a Dynkin system. For $S\in\mathcal P$, each $A\in\mathcal P$ belongs to $D_S$ because $A\cap S$ is either empty or a generator. Minimality now gives $D\subseteq D_S$ for each generator $S$. Fix an arbitrary $T\in D$. The first inclusion and commutativity of intersection give $S\cap T\in D$ for every generator $S$, so $D_T$ contains $\mathcal P$. A second application of minimality gives $D\subseteq D_T$. This proves closure of $D$ under binary intersections.

For an arbitrary sequence $A_n\in D$, disjointify by taking $C_n=A_n\cap\bigcap_{k<n}A_k^c$. This finite intersection lies in $D$, with the empty intersection interpreted as $X=\varnothing^c$. The $C_n$ are disjoint and have the same union as the $A_n$, so that union belongs to $D$. Now $D$ is a $\sigma$-algebra containing $\mathcal P$, and $\sigma(\mathcal P)$ is itself a Dynkin system containing $\mathcal P$. The two minimality inclusions prove the assertion. The four resulting induction cases on $\sigma(\mathcal P)$ are empty, basic, complement and disjoint countable union. [Pinned `DynkinSystem.generate_inter`, `generateFrom_eq` and `induction_on_inter`][ch3-u1-dynkin]

**Lemma (uniqueness with a finite spanning sequence).** [Lean, read] On $(X,\mathcal A)$ with $\mathcal A=\sigma(\mathcal P)$ for a $\pi$-system $\mathcal P$, let $\mu,\nu$ be measures agreeing on $\mathcal P$. If $\mu$ has a finite spanning sequence in $\mathcal P$, then $\mu=\nu$.

**Proof.** Fix one spanning set $B=B_n$. It has equal finite mass for both measures. For measurable $A$ consider the assertion $\mu(A\cap B)=\nu(A\cap B)$. For $A=\varnothing$ both sides vanish. For $A\in\mathcal P$ the intersection is empty or belongs to $\mathcal P$, which gives equality. In the complement case, decompose $B=(A\cap B)\sqcup(A^c\cap B)$. The common first summand is finite by monotonicity, so it cancels in the two equal sums for $\mu(B)=\nu(B)$. Finally, for pairwise disjoint measurable $A_j$ each intersected piece $A_j\cap B$ is measurable and the pieces are disjoint. Countable additivity sums the equalities over $j$. The preceding induction therefore gives $\mu|_B=\nu|_B$ on the whole of $\mathcal A$. This is the complement cancellation in `ext_of_generateFrom_of_cover`, with the empty and nonempty intersection branches supplied by `ext_of_generateFrom_of_cover_subset`.

For the passage from restrictions to measures, put $C_n=B_n\setminus\bigcup_{k<n}B_k$. The $C_n$ are measurable, disjoint, contained in $B_n$ and cover $X$. For each measurable $A$, $\mu(A\cap C_n)=\nu(A\cap C_n)$ follows by testing the restriction equality at $A\cap C_n$. Summing over $n$ gives $\mu(A)=\nu(A)$, including when the common sum is infinite. This proves gluing for measurable covers.

In the source, `ext_of_generateFrom_of_iUnion` passes the countable range of the $B_n$ to the union-of-sets version. `restrict_iUnion_congr` glues the pieces by forming finite partial unions and taking the directed supremum of their masses. For two measurable pieces $S,T$ the finite step at $A$ uses the disjoint decomposition into $A\cap S$ and $(A\setminus S)\cap T$. Mathlib implements that binary step for arbitrary $S,T$. It takes a common measurable superset $H\supseteq A\cap S$ of the same mass under each measure. Replacing $A\cap S$ by $H$ in the union with $A\cap T$ preserves its mass by the library's equal-mass enlargement lemma. The new union splits into $H$ and $(A\setminus H)\cap T$, giving $(\mu|_S)(A)+(\mu|_T)(A\setminus H)$, and the same expression for $\nu$. The equality of the two restrictions on $S$ and on $T$ proves equality on $S\cup T$. Finite induction and continuity for directed countable unions finish the library proof. The common measurable superset and equal-mass enlargement enter here as named measure interfaces. With measurable generators, one may take $H=A\cap S$ and use ordinary disjoint additivity. For an increasing cover the gluing can also be written as $\mu(A)=\sup_n\mu(A\cap B_n)=\sup_n\nu(A\cap B_n)=\nu(A)$. The general theorem needs a countable cover and allows it to overlap. [Pinned `FiniteSpanningSetsIn.ext`][ch3-u1-sfinite] · [Pinned finite induction and gluing][ch3-u1-restrict]

**Theorem (U1, rational open-interval uniqueness).** [Lean, read] Let $\mu$ and $\nu$ be measures on $(\mathbb R,\mathcal B(\mathbb R))$. Suppose $\mu$ is locally finite and $\mu((p,q))=\nu((p,q))$ for all $p,q\in\mathbb Q$. Then $\mu=\nu$. This is `Real.measure_ext_Ioo_rat`. The local finiteness hypothesis belongs to $\mu$.

**Proof.** The named topological inputs are density of the rationals, the real interval neighborhood basis, and Heine–Borel compactness of closed bounded intervals. The rational interval basis gives $\sigma(\mathcal P_{\mathbb Q})=\mathcal B(\mathbb R)$. Each generator is open, and each open set is a union of the rational intervals it contains, a countable subfamily of the basis. Indeed, around any point in the open set choose a small open real interval contained in the set and move its endpoints inward to rationals on opposite sides of the point. This is the use of the named rational basis in `borel_eq_generateFrom_Ioo_rat`. The intersection of $(p,q)$ and $(r,s)$ is $(\max(p,r),\min(q,s))$. When it is nonempty, the left endpoint is strictly smaller than the right and both are rational. This verifies the $\pi$-system property with exactly the convention in `isPiSystem_Ioo_rat`.

Take $B_n=(-(n+1),n+1)$. Its endpoints are rational and it is nonempty. For any $x\in\mathbb R$, the integer $n=\lfloor|x|\rfloor$ satisfies $|x|<n+1$, so the $B_n$ exhaust $\mathbb R$. Local finiteness means that each $x$ has a neighborhood of finite $\mu$-mass. On the compact set $[-(n+1),n+1]$, choose open subneighborhoods of finite mass, take a finite subcover, and use finite subadditivity. The compact interval has finite mass, and hence so does $B_n$. This is the route through `measure_Ioo_lt_top` in the source. Agreement on $B_n$ makes $\nu(B_n)$ finite too. The spanning sequence proves $\sigma$-finiteness for both measures and, because it lies inside the test family, supplies the earlier uniqueness lemma. The same intervals also give a finite open neighborhood of each point for $\nu$. [Pinned real specialization][ch3-u1-real] · [Pinned finiteness on compacts and open intervals][ch3-u1-finite]

The general second-countable instance `sigmaFinite_of_locallyFinite` uses a different cover selection. Choose a finite-mass neighborhood at each point, use second countability to obtain countably many of these neighborhoods still covering the space, and enumerate their image, padding with $\varnothing$ if needed. Taking same-mass measurable supersets gives the usual measurable cover even if the chosen neighborhoods were arbitrary sets. In U1 the explicit rational intervals simultaneously provide measurable sets, finite mass and membership in the generating family. [Pinned `SigmaFinite` and the neighborhood-cover instance][ch3-u1-sfinite-general]

**Completion.** The theorem compares measures on the fixed Borel $\sigma$-algebra. For a Borel measure $\rho$, its completion adds the sets $A$ with $A\triangle E\subseteq N$ for some Borel $E,N$ with $\rho(N)=0$. Complements keep the same exceptional set, and for a countable union one takes the union of the exceptional sets, which is again Borel and null. These sets form a $\sigma$-algebra containing the Borel sets and all subsets of Borel null sets. Its measure on $A$ is $\rho(E)$. If $E'$ is another representative with exceptional set $N'$, then $E\triangle E'\subseteq N\cup N'$. The two Borel differences have zero mass, so $\rho(E)=\rho(E\cap E')=\rho(E')$, also for infinite values. When two Borel measures have been shown equal they have the same Borel null sets, the same completed $\sigma$-algebra, and the same completed values by this formula. The pinned `Measure.completion` uses the same underlying outer measure on its null-measurable space. `Measure.ext` uses the trimmed outer measure determined by the Borel values. The restriction step keeps the original Borel $\sigma$-algebra, and completion changes the measurable space after the uniqueness argument. [Pinned `Measure.ext`][ch3-u1-measure-ext] · [Pinned `Measure.completion`][ch3-u1-completion] · [Completion and integral transport](#completing-a-borel-measure-and-transporting-integrals)

**What the hypotheses buy.** [Expository]

- *Finite mass on the spanning tests.* For a countable $D\subseteq\mathbb R$ write $c_D(A)=\#(A\cap D)$ with infinite cardinality recorded as $\infty$. This is a Borel measure because counting on disjoint sets adds. Take $D=\mathbb Q$ and $D'=\mathbb Q\setminus\{0\}$. Every nonempty rational open interval contains infinitely many points of both sets, so all the rational tests agree, and both measures also give the empty set zero. They differ on $\{0\}$, with masses $1$ and $0$. Each measure is $\sigma$-finite using the individual rational singletons and $\mathbb R\setminus\mathbb Q$ as a countable measurable cover. Every generator has infinite mass, so this example retains Borel generation and ordinary $\sigma$-finiteness while losing finite spanning sets in the generator.
- *Generation of the target $\sigma$-algebra.* Retain only the tests $(-r,r)$ for positive rational $r$. These form a $\pi$-system with a finite spanning sequence for $\delta_{-1}$ and $\delta_1$. The two measures give $0$ if $r\le1$ and $1$ if $r>1$, yet they differ on $\{1\}$. Every member of the generated $\sigma$-algebra is invariant under $x\mapsto-x$. In particular it cannot contain $\{1\}$.
- *Covering the whole space.* Keep the rational open intervals disjoint from $\{0\}$. They remain a $\pi$-system. Their union is $\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}$, so the generated $\sigma$-algebra contains $\{0\}$ and all open sets $O$, since $O\setminus\{0\}$ is a countable union of retained intervals and $O\cap\{0\}$ is either empty or $\{0\}$. The zero measure and $\delta_0$ agree on every test, all tests have finite mass, and the measures differ on $\{0\}$. There is no spanning sequence in this family because its union misses zero.

**Return to real volume.** In `Real.volume_eq_stieltjes_id` the first argument of U1 is the locally finite $\ell$ and the competing measure is $(T_a)_*\ell$. For every $x$, the inherited interval law gives $\ell((x-1,x+1))=2$, a finite neighborhood. For each real $a$ and every rational $p,q$, the equality of tested masses is $\ell((p-a,q-a))=((q-a)-(p-a))_+=(q-p)_+=\ell((p,q))$. The calculation uses the inherited `StieltjesFunction.measure_Ioo` and the translation preimage identity. The actual call returns $\ell=(T_a)_*\ell$, and `Eq.symm` supplies the orientation required by `IsAddLeftInvariant`. The existing **(U2)** then uses the unit parallelepiped mass to identify Haar `volume` with $\ell$. The interval-length, Stieltjes and outer-measure constructions, Haar uniqueness and the four downstream bridges are supplied by the preceding reading. [Pinned `Lebesgue/Basic.lean:54–70`][ch3-u1-volume] · [Four consumer bridges](#where-interval-lengths-in-real-lebesgue-measure-come-from)

**Attribution and reading scope.** Billingsley states and proves the Dynkin generation argument in *Probability and Measure*, 3rd ed., Theorem 3.2, with Lemma 6 supplying the disjointification step after intersection closure. Theorem 10.3 uses agreement on a generating $\pi$-system with a countable finite-mass cover in that system. Theorem 12.1 recognizes translated Lebesgue measure by bounded rectangles, and Problems 3.10(b) and 10.5 treat completion. Billingsley uses the convention that a binary intersection may be empty and still belongs to the family. Adjoining the empty set reconciles the conventions and contributes the automatic equality of the two zero masses. The rational endpoints and the particular restriction chain above follow the pinned Mathlib proof. The local R3 reading covers the cited uniqueness bodies and their direct handoff. Source reading, Lean kernel replay, declaration-level reachability from `Main.lean`, and peer review record different evidence. [Billingsley, pp. 41–42, 49, 163–164, 172][ch3-u1-billingsley] · [Source ledger](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-3-rational-open-intervals-and-borel-uniqueness)

#### Completing a Borel measure and transporting integrals

The rational interval tests above determine a measure on the Borel sets. The next step concerns a different question. An integrand can change on an arbitrary subset of a measure-zero Borel set. What changes in the measurable space, and what can we still compute with the original Borel measure? The answer uses the same almost-everywhere filter on both spaces. It also gives a way to choose one original strongly measurable representative for the entire integrand. [Rational interval uniqueness](#how-rational-open-intervals-determine-a-real-borel-measure) · [Pinned completion][ch3-nc-completion]

**Two examples.** [Expository] Let $C$ be the middle-thirds Cantor set and put $K=1/8+C/4$. Its $n$th closed approximation consists of $2^n$ pairwise disjoint intervals of length $1/(4\cdot3^n)$, starting with $[1/8,3/8]$. Each interval gives two intervals of one third its length at the next stage. Their combined length is $(2/3)^n/4$. Thus $K$ is closed and has Lebesgue measure zero. It has cardinality $\mathfrak c$ by ternary coding: at a first differing digit $k$, the leading difference $2/3^k$ exceeds the maximum tail difference $1/3^k$. Borel sets have at most $\mathfrak c$ countable codes of operations on a countable basis, while $K$ has $2^{\mathfrak c}$ subsets. Choose a non-Borel $S\subseteq K$. The calculations will hold for every $S\subseteq K$. [Tao, middle thirds construction, Exercise 10][ch3-nc-tao]

1. Write $L=(0,1/2]$, $R=(1/2,1]$, $A=L\setminus S$, and $f=3\mathbf 1_A-2\mathbf 1_R$. The Borel sets $L\setminus K$ and $L$ bracket $A$, differ by $K$, and each has mass $1/2$. In the completion of Borel Lebesgue measure $\lambda$, the mass of $A$ is therefore $1/2$. The set $A$ is non-Borel for the chosen $S$, since $S=L\setminus A$. The Borel representative $g=3\mathbf 1_L-2\mathbf 1_R$ agrees with $f$ off $K$. The two half-interval integrals are $3/2$ and $-1$, so the integral from $0$ to $1$ is $1/2$ and the reversed integral is $-1/2$. The integral of $|f|$ on the whole line is $3/2+1=5/2$. The same values follow from the Borel simple function $g$.
2. Take $\eta=\lambda+2\delta_p$ on the original Borel line with $p=1/2$, the jump measure from the preceding subsection. Its Borel null sets are precisely the Lebesgue-null Borel sets omitting $p$. Here $K$ omits $p$. The same bracket gives $\eta^c(A)=5/2$, because $\eta(L)=1/2+2$ and $\eta(K)=0$. Let $F=f+2\mathbf 1_{\{p\}}$ and $G=g+2\mathbf 1_{\{p\}}$. Both have value $5$ at $p$ and they agree off $S$. On the disjoint pieces $A\setminus\{p\}$, $\{p\}$ and $R$ their completed integral for $F$ is $3/2+5\cdot2-1=21/2$, with absolute integral $3/2+10+1=25/2$. The two oriented pieces contribute $23/2$ and $-1$, and reversal gives $-21/2$. Computing with $G$ uses the original Borel pieces $L\setminus\{p\}$, $\{p\}$ and $R$ of the same masses. In fact the completed sigma-algebras for $\lambda$ and $\eta$ coincide. If $D\triangle E\subseteq N$ with $E,N$ Borel and $\lambda(N)=0$, give $E$ the membership of $D$ at $p$ by adding or deleting $\{p\}$. The resulting Borel representative differs from $D$ only in $N\setminus\{p\}$. The reverse inclusion follows since every $\eta$-null Borel set is $\lambda$-null. On this common sigma-algebra the formula is $\eta^c(D)=\lambda^c(D)+2\mathbf 1_D(p)$.

**Definitions.** Let $(X,\mathcal A,\mu)$ be a measure space. Write $\mu^o(T)=\inf\{\mu(B):B\in\mathcal A,\ T\subseteq B\}$ for the canonical outer value on any subset $T\subseteq X$. Mathlib writes $\mu(T)$ for that value, including for sets outside $\mathcal A$. A predicate holds $\mu$-almost everywhere if its failure set has outer value zero. A set is null measurable when it agrees almost everywhere with an original measurable set. In Lean, `NullMeasurableSpace X μ` is a type tag on the same points, with the measurable structure `eventuallyMeasurableSpace` for the filter `ae μ`. Its empty-set, complement and countable-union fields are witnessed by the empty set, the complement of the chosen representative, and the union of the chosen representatives. [Pinned eventual measurability][ch3-nc-eventual] · [Pinned null measurable space][ch3-nc-space] · [Pinned outer null filter][ch3-nc-ae]

**Lemma (measurable brackets).** [Lean, read] For every $D\subseteq X$, the following conditions are equivalent.

1. $D$ is null measurable.
2. There are $E,N\in\mathcal A$ with $\mu(N)=0$ and $D\triangle E\subseteq N$.
3. There are $B_-,B_+\in\mathcal A$ with $B_-\subseteq D\subseteq B_+$ and $\mu(B_+\setminus B_-)=0$.

**Proof.** If a set has outer value zero, take measurable supersets of mass less than $2^{-n}$ for $n\ge1$. Their intersection is an original measurable null superset. Apply this to the failure set of equality of membership in $D$ and its representative $E$. Conversely, containment in an original null set implies outer value zero. From (2) take $B_-=E\setminus N$ and $B_+=E\cup N$. From (3) take $E=B_-$ and $N=B_+\setminus B_-$. Mathlib makes bracket choices with `toMeasurable`: enlarge a representative $E$ by the measurable null hull of $D\setminus E$, then use the analogous upper hull of $D^c$ and complement it for the lower bracket. In particular, $\mu^o(D)=\mu(B_-)=\mu(B_+)=\mu(E)$ by monotonicity and the null error, including when that common value is infinite. [Pinned measurable hulls][ch3-nc-hull] · [Pinned upper and lower representatives][ch3-nc-bracket]

**Theorem (completion).** [Lean, read] The null measurable sets form a sigma-algebra $\mathcal A^c_\mu$. On it the rule $\mu^c(D)=\mu(E)$ from (2) gives a complete measure extending $\mu$. Its canonical outer value on every subset is $\mu^o$, so $\forall^{\mathrm{a.e.},\mu^c}x,P(x)$ is equivalent to $\forall^{\mathrm{a.e.},\mu}x,P(x)$ for every predicate $P$. [Expository] On the same points, $(\mathcal A^c_\mu,\mu^c)$ is the unique least complete extension and completion is idempotent.

**Proof.** An original measurable set uses $N=\varnothing$. Complements preserve the symmetric difference and, for a sequence of sets, the symmetric difference of the unions is contained in the union of the null bounds. This proves sigma-closure. Two representatives differ inside the union of their null bounds. Both have the same measure as their intersection by disjoint additivity with zero on the leftover pieces. This gives a well-defined value even at infinity. For pairwise disjoint completed sets $D_i$, choose brackets as above. Their original measurable lower sets $B_{-,i}$ are disjoint, and $\bigcup_i B_{-,i}$ represents $\bigcup_i D_i$ off the union of the bracket differences. Thus the completed measure is countably additive. The source uses the stronger `measure_iUnion₀` for pairwise a.e.-disjoint sets. One can disjointify lower representatives in countable order, removing from each all its intersections with earlier representatives, which are original null sets. For each index the total discarded part is null. The union stays a.e. equal to the original union since there are countably many indices. The code achieves the disjointification with its a.e.-disjointness lemma.

For an arbitrary $T$, the infimum over completed supersets is at most the infimum over original measurable supersets. In the other direction each completed superset of $T$ has an original upper bracket with the same mass. This gives equality of the infima. Mathlib supplies this invariant by retaining the original `toOuterMeasure` and checking the trim field by antitonicity in the measurable space. Hence `completion_apply` and `ae_completion` are definitional equalities. If $\mu^c(D)=0$ for a completed measurable $D$, its upper bracket is original measurable and null, so every subset of $D$ is completed measurable. The same argument in outer-value form proves `completion.isComplete`: every set of outer value zero agrees almost everywhere with the empty set.

Let $(\mathcal D,\rho)$ be any complete extension on $X$, so $\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal D$ and $\rho|_{\mathcal A}=\mu$. Every subset of the original $N$ of (2) belongs to $\mathcal D$ and has $\rho$-mass zero. In particular $D\triangle E\in\mathcal D$, hence $D\in\mathcal D$ and $\rho(D)=\mu(E)$. This forces both the least domain and its values. Applying the result to the already complete space gives the same sigma-algebra and measure after a second completion, under the identity on the underlying points. U1 first identifies the original Borel measures under its local finiteness and rational-test hypotheses. Their Borel null sets then agree, so the two least complete extensions have the same domain and values. On two points, take only $\varnothing$ and the whole space with total mass $1$. This space is already complete. The two different point masses on the power set are complete extensions agreeing on the original domain. [Pinned construction and complete-space test][ch3-nc-completion] · [Billingsley, Problem 3.10 and Problem 10.5][ch3-nc-billingsley]

**Lemma (original representatives).** [Lean, read] Measurability on $\mathcal A^c_\mu$ is `NullMeasurable`, meaning that every measurable target set has null measurable preimage. If the target sigma-algebra is countably generated, this gives an original measurable representative equal almost everywhere to the given function. Conversely, an original measurable representative agreeing almost everywhere always gives completed measurability. [Expository] The corresponding transport for a.e. strong measurability works for any real Banach target.

**Proof.** Choose a bracket for the preimage of each member of a countable generating family. Outside the union $N$ of their differences, all these preimages agree with the original lower representatives. Replace the function on $N$ by a fixed target value. The preimage of each generator is now the lower preimage representative off $N$, with $N$ included exactly when the fixed value lies in the generator. Measurability follows by generation. For the empty target, existence of the function forces an empty domain. The converse holds because every preimage changes only on the same outer-null failure set. The library also has `NullMeasurable.aemeasurable_of_aerange` when the function lies almost everywhere in a subset with countably generated trace sigma-algebra. On the completed domain its proof uses `ae_completion` and a null-hull patch to force the range into that subset everywhere, then descends the map into the subtype by the countable-generator argument. The empty-subset case forces the measure to be zero. Its strong-measurability corollary uses an a.e. separable range with a pseudometrizable Borel target. The trace on a separable pseudometrizable subspace has a countable base, whose members generate its Borel sigma-algebra. [Pinned countably generated targets][ch3-nc-aemeas] · [Pinned a.e. separable-range criterion][ch3-nc-strong]

[Expository] Here is a direct construction for strong measurability. Suppose $f$ has a completed strongly measurable representative $h$. Choose completed simple functions $h_n$ converging pointwise to $h$. For every fibre of every $h_n$, choose an original measurable bracket. Cover the failure of $f=h$ by an original measurable null set as well. The union of these countably many original null sets is a single original measurable null set $N$. On $X\setminus N$ the original lower sets of each $h_n$ are exactly its fibres there. Replace each $h_n$ on $N$ by zero. The resulting original simple functions converge everywhere, to $h$ off $N$ and to zero on $N$. This limit $g$ is original strongly measurable and equals $f$ almost everywhere on either measure. Conversely, an original simple-function sequence remains a sequence of simple functions on the completed domain. Keep the same representative and use the same null filter. This proves the two directions for `AEStronglyMeasurable`, including when the Banach space itself is nonseparable.

**Theorem (integral transport).** [Expository] For any function $f$ into a real Banach space, original and completed Bochner integrability are equivalent. When integrable, the integrals agree with the integral of any original strongly measurable representative $g$ with $g=f$ almost everywhere. For the Mathlib total Bochner integral the values agree for nonintegrable $f$ as well, since both return zero in that case. For every original measurable set $J$, these assertions also hold relative to the two measures restricted to $J$, with equality of representatives required only almost everywhere on $J$. On the real Borel line, for all real $a,b$, interval integrability and the oriented integral from $a$ to $b$ transport in both directions. A representative can be chosen on the original line for the restriction to $(\min(a,b),\max(a,b)]$.

**Proof.** For a strongly measurable original representative, the nonnegative norm integral is identical on the two spaces. For instance, use an increasing sequence of original nonnegative simple functions approximating its norm by truncated dyadic rounding. Each approximant has the same integral because all its fibres are original measurable. Take the supremum and use invariance under almost-everywhere equality to return to $f$. This transports the finite-norm condition. The representative lemma transports the other condition, a.e. strong measurability. To compare Bochner values, trimming the completed measure to $\mathcal A$ recovers $\mu$ by equality on original sets. The pinned `integral_trim_ae` therefore applies using the original a.e. strongly measurable representative. That lemma uses congruence in both measures to reduce to `integral_trim`. For original integrable simple functions the fibre sums agree. For an original strongly measurable integrable function the pinned `SimpleFunc.approxOn` sequence takes values in the separable set built from its range and zero. It approximates the function in $L^1$ for both measures. The common integral sequence has the same limit by $L^1$ continuity. This comparison uses the existing Bochner extension. [Pinned trim][ch3-nc-trim] · [Pinned Bochner comparison][ch3-nc-integral]

For an original measurable $J$, the restricted outer values are $\mu^o(T\cap J)$ on any subset $T$ in both spaces. Their almost-everywhere filters coincide, and trimming the completed ambient restriction to the original sigma-algebra gives the original restriction. Apply the preceding proof to the same two sigma-algebras with these restricted measures. In the descent construction the simple-function fibres still have globally null bracket errors, and the failure of $f=h$ now has an original hull null for the original restriction. Their union is null for that restriction. Hence the same argument gives a representative defined on $X$ with the required restricted equality. The argument allows a completed measurable $J$ as well, using `restrict_apply₀'` on the original side. For real endpoints the support $\operatorname{uIoc}(a,b)=(\min(a,b),\max(a,b)]$ is original Borel. The pinned `intervalIntegrable_iff` uses integrability on that set. The integral subtracts the integrals on $(a,b]$ and $(b,a]$. If $a=b$ both are empty, and reversing distinct endpoints changes only the sign. Equality almost everywhere on the unordered half-open support is enough for `intervalIntegrable_congr_ae` and `intervalIntegral.integral_congr_ae_restrict`. [Pinned restriction and a.e. tests][ch3-nc-restrict] · [Pinned interval definitions and transport][ch3-nc-interval] · [Pinned interval congruence][ch3-nc-interval-ae]

**Why the hypotheses are needed.** [Expository]

- *Null control.* Allow arbitrary measurable $N$ in (2) and $N=X$ admits every subset. The assignment can already give two answers on the empty set with Lebesgue measure, using representatives $\varnothing$ and $[0,1]$. Some sets would also be admitted beyond the completion. Choose a Vitali transversal $V\subseteq[0,1]$ for equivalence modulo $\mathbb Q$. Its translates by rationals in $[-1,1]$ lie in $[-1,2]$. If $v+q=v'+q'$, then $v-v'$ is rational, so the transversal gives $v=v'$ and $q=q'$. Every $x\in[0,1]$ differs from its chosen representative by a rational in $[-1,1]$, so these disjoint translates cover $[0,1]$. Borel translation invariance sends null brackets to null brackets and preserves their masses, so it extends to the completion. If $V$ were in the completion, a zero mass would give its union of translates mass zero and a positive mass would give infinite mass. The union has mass at least $1$ and at most $3$, ruling out both cases. [Tao, Proposition 12][ch3-nc-tao]
- *Null tests depend on the measure.* The change from $G$ to $g$ in the second example has support $\{p\}$. It is $\lambda$-a.e. invisible and changes the $\eta$ integral on $(0,1]$ by $4$. Likewise for the constant function $1$, the $\eta$ integrals on $[p,1]$ and $(p,1]$ are $5/2$ and $1/2$. Completion preserves this atom and every restricted mass used in the calculation.
- *Adjacent intervals.* The pin requires both `IntervalIntegrable f μ a b` and `IntervalIntegrable f μ b c`. With those conditions, the supports of both legs cover $(\min(a,b,c),\max(a,b,c)]$, so $f$ is integrable there. Splitting the three endpoints into ordered pieces of this support gives additivity, with signs accounting for any reversal and zero for any empty interval. For the audited failure, take $f(x)=1/x$ on $(0,1]$ and $1$ on $(1,2]$, zero elsewhere. On each $(2^{-n-1},2^{-n}]$ the norm integral is at least $2^n2^{-n-1}=1/2$ under $\lambda$. Thus $f$ is nonintegrable on $(0,1]$ and $(0,2]$, while its integral on $(1,2]$ is $1$. The library's total values give $0+1\ne0$. To see why the other premise matters individually, take $1$ on $(0,1]$, take $1/(2-x)$ on $(1,2)$ and set zero elsewhere. The reflected dyadic intervals give the same divergent lower bounds, hence $1+0\ne0$. [Pinned adjacent addition and default value][ch3-nc-adjacent]

**Actual uses and scope.** [Lean, read] The Stieltjes producer chooses the original Borel domain via `outer_trim` and `StieltjesFunction.measure`. The proof of `Real.volume_eq_stieltjes_id` spends U1 on the translation test, before any completion enters. In the Landau-window archive, `windowHyp_of_rpow` uses `integrableOn_congr_fun` and `setIntegral_congr_fun` on the Borel closed interval. It then uses `integral_Icc_eq_integral_Ioc`, whose general primed variant pays for deleting the left endpoint with the hypothesis that this singleton has mass zero. In the Laplace step `iteratedDeriv_laplace_meas` obtains a.e. strong measurability from Borel measurability, and `iteratedDeriv_laplace_hasDerivAt` uses `ae_restrict_iff'` on the open half-line and the supplied norm bounds. These proofs integrate on the original real line. The winding calculation in A2 gets both interval integrability and the endpoint measurability and continuity inputs from its continuous integrand. The direct `ae_completion` call read here is in Mathlib's `NullMeasurable.aemeasurable_of_aerange`. The original archive proofs use the a.e. and representative interfaces just described. In particular the transport theorem preserves integral values and integrability. Pointwise regularity, bounds on a prescribed set, and a derivative's value at a prescribed endpoint remain separate hypotheses. [Archive and pinned consumer ledger](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-3-borel-completion-and-integral-transport)

[Paper] Billingsley gives the null-error construction and the least complete extension in *Probability and Measure*, third edition, Problem 3.10(b), and asks for the general measure space in Problem 10.5. The pinned Mathlib files give the a.e. and integral tools in the proof above. The completion and transport are presented here with their classical and library attributions. The section records an author-level local R3 source reading. Lean kernel replay, declaration-level reachability from `Main.lean` and peer review have separate scopes. [Billingsley, pp. 49 and 164][ch3-nc-billingsley] · [Source ledger](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-3-borel-completion-and-integral-transport)
